## Local Work, Global Progress:

A Convergence Theory of AI Agent Collaboration

Ivan Ilin

Affiliation: KAUST

email: ivan.ilin@kaust.edu.sa

 and 
Peter Richtárik

Affiliation: KAUST

email: peter.richtarik@kaust.edu.sa

© none

Abstract.

Teams of large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly applied to complex tasks, but their collaboration protocols are mostly designed empirically, without convergence guarantees. We formalize collaborative problem solving as block optimization: each agent improves one part of a shared solution, guided by its local objective plus a correction for how its changes affect the other parts. A coordinator checks every proposal against the global objective and merges proposals that do not interfere with each other. We prove linear convergence up to an error floor that vanishes when the corrections are exact. Interfering proposals can also be merged once their combination has been checked; the rate then depends on how well the proposals actually combine, not on worst-case interactions. With GPT-OSS-120B agents on robot routing, a three-agent team finds collision-free plans in every run, better than those of a single agent that controls all robots. On a larger routing task, a six-agent team completes its search about twice as fast as the single agent (in median). Merging the best checked combination of proposals, rather than only proposals that do not interfere, cuts the team’s search time by about 30%. With constants measured on the runs, our convergence bound holds in every round and follows the observed progress. We also test the theory on query planning in SQLite and on a synthetic task.

## 1. Introduction

Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly asked to produce artifacts that are too large for a single request, such as software with many modules or plans for many robots. Human organizations handle such work by division of labor, and dividing work among agents promises two benefits. First, each request becomes smaller and easier, so a task that overwhelms one agent can become solvable. Second, the parts can be searched in parallel, so a solution arrives sooner. We do not aim to reduce monetary cost: like a construction crew compared with a single builder, a team spends more in total but completes work that one agent would finish slowly or not at all.

Not every task benefits from a team, and not every team needs coordination. A simple task, like changing a light bulb, is best done by one worker. If the parts do not interact, for example when one agent waters a garden while another goes shopping, agents can simply work in parallel. The interesting and common case lies in between: large tasks whose parts are coupled, such as the modules of a program, the components of an aircraft, or the routes of robots that share a floor. Each part is manageable alone, but a change to one part can change what is best for the others.

Coupling creates a coordination problem: improving each part in isolation need not improve the whole. A module can pass its own tests while violating another module’s interface; a robot can reach its goal along a route that collides with a robot controlled by another agent. A global check can reject a harmful proposal, but rejection does not tell the agent which alternative to try next: it needs information that changes how it ranks its own candidates.

We study this problem through finite block optimization. An agent is a local search procedure, for example an LLM, that owns one block of a shared solution and searches it while the other blocks stay frozen. A correction added to its local objective tells the agent how its changes affect the other blocks. Agents search their corrected objectives in parallel. A verifier then scores each proposal against the current shared solution, and a coloring of the dependency graph selects proposals that do not interfere (Figure 1). The correction steers the proposals, while verification and coloring ensure that the shared solution never gets worse.

Figure 1. One epoch of Algorithm 1 on a constructed example with illustrative gains. Staircases show each agent’s best corrected score so far, which need not reach the minimum $\widehat{f}_{b}^{\,*}$ of the corrected objective. Each proposal is verified alone, and the coordinator accepts the color with the largest total gain: blocks 2 and 3 ( $3+2=5$ ) rather than the single largest gain ( $4$ ).A five-stage pipeline. Stage one: a coordinator holds the shared solution x with three blocks; each agent b receives its own block, where its search starts, and its correction, computed from the other blocks, which stay frozen. Stage two: three agents, drawn in blue, orange and magenta, each minimize their local objective plus correction over their own block; a small staircase shows the corrected score of the best candidate dropping at each of three private steps, from zero at the current block to the proposal, which stays above a dashed line marking the minimum. Stage three: a verifier evaluates each proposal alone against the same base, giving gains four, three and two. Stage four: in the dependency graph block one is connected to blocks two and three, which are not connected to each other. Color one contains blocks two and three with total gain five and is marked as selected; color two contains block one with gain four. Stage five: the new base replaces blocks two and three and keeps block one, and the loss drops by five. A solid arrow returns to the start for the next epoch; a dashed arrow shows that verified values update the correction.

We test the protocol with GPT-OSS-120B agents on robot routing, where robots of different agents can collide. A team of three agents finds collision-free plans in every run, better than those of a single agent that controls all robots at once. The same team without corrections rarely avoids collisions. On a larger task, a team of six agents also searches about twice as fast as the single agent in median. The coordinator can even merge interfering proposals once the verifier confirms that together they lower the loss more than the non-interfering ones alone. This rule, which our theory covers, cuts the team’s search time by about 30% and lets it reach low losses sooner than single agents searching in parallel. With constants measured on the runs, the convergence bound holds in every round, so theory and practice agree. Two smaller studies, on query planning in SQLite and on a synthetic objective, explore the theory and its limits further.

Contributions.

(1) 

We formalize collaborative problem solving as block optimization with corrected local objectives, and introduce a protocol of parallel private search, exact verification and compatible merging (Section 3).

(2) 

We prove linear convergence with an explicit error floor (Theorem 2). The rate separates task geometry, local search quality and merge compatibility.

(3) 

We show that the coordinator can accept the best verified combination of the agents’ proposals, keeping the compatible work of interfering blocks, with the same guarantees and a rate set by how well the proposals actually combine (Section 5).

(4) 

We show how agents can learn reusable corrections from global evaluations, with a joint convergence rate for the solution and the correction (Appendix A.4), and account for calibration, local work and synchronization costs (Sections 6 and 7).

(5) 

We evaluate the protocol with teams of three and six LLM agents on robot routing and relate the observed convergence to the theorem; a database study shows when acquiring correction information pays off, and a synthetic study confirms the error floor (Section 8).

The appendices contain further theory and examples (Appendix A), extended related work (Appendix B), and complete experimental protocols and outcomes.

The implementation and code for reproducing our experiments are available at https://github.com/vectozavr/local-work-global-progress.

## 1.1. Notation

All key notation used in this paper is summarized in tabular form in the Notation section before the appendices; see Table 2.

## 2. Related work

Block optimization and distributed coordination.

Block coordinate descent and its convergence theory are classical (Bertsekas and Tsitsiklis, 1989; Tseng, 2001; Nesterov, 2012), as are its parallel and inexact variants (Richtárik and Takáč, 2016; Tappenden et al., 2016). When parallel coordinate updates interfere through shared terms, prior analyses bound the interference (Bradley et al., 2011; Richtárik and Takáč, 2016) or group weakly coupled coordinates (Scherrer et al., 2012). We instead verify every proposal and then either merge only blocks of one color, whose gains add exactly, or check the merged combination as a whole. Distributed constraint optimization assigns coupled variables to agents: MGM coordinates improving moves, DSA randomizes local updates, and DPOP propagates conditional utility tables (Maheswaran et al., 2004; Zhang et al., 2005; Petcu and Faltings, 2005). Our addition is an explicit interface for correcting candidate values and a bound that converts the correction error into lost global progress.

Persistent corrections and identification.

ProxSkip and SCAFFOLD correct local work through control variates (Mishchenko et al., 2022; Karimireddy et al., 2020). Our corrections play the analogous role for discrete search, adjusting candidate scores of disjoint blocks rather than gradients; whether they also admit ProxSkip-type acceleration remains open. Our update for learning corrections (Section 6) is a randomized Kaczmarz, or sketch-and-project, step (Strohmer and Vershynin, 2009; Gower and Richtárik, 2015), and additive Bayesian optimization and linear-bandit identification also exploit known structure and informative measurements (Kandasamy et al., 2015; Soare et al., 2014).

LLMs as evaluator-guided optimizers.

LLMs can serve as search operators when paired with an automatic evaluator. OPRO proposes new solutions from a history of scored ones, and FunSearch and AlphaEvolve evolve programs under automated evaluation (Yang et al., 2024; Romera-Paredes et al., 2024; Novikov et al., 2025). Our agents are such evaluator-guided searchers, each restricted to one block. The theory asks what feedback the evaluator must provide so that parallel block searches add up to global progress.

LLM collaboration and multi-robot planning.

MetaGPT and ChatDev coordinate software-development artifacts, and AutoGen composes conversing agents (Hong et al., 2024; Qian et al., 2024; Wu et al., 2024). GPTSwarm optimizes agent graphs, and AgentPrune removes redundant communication (Zhuge et al., 2024; Zhang et al., 2025b). Reflexion retains feedback-derived text across trials, and EvoMAC adapts collaboration using verification feedback (Shinn et al., 2023; Hu et al., 2025). Decomposed prompting splits complex tasks into simpler subtasks (Khot et al., 2023). In robotics, one LLM agent per robot can negotiate a joint plan through dialogue and re-plan from motion-planner feedback such as collisions (Mandi et al., 2024), and LLMs can decompose instructions and allocate the subtasks among robots (Kannan et al., 2024). Centralized, decentralized and hybrid LLM planners have also been compared as teams grow (Chen et al., 2024a). LLMs solve multi-agent path finding on empty maps but still fail on standard maps with obstacles (Chen et al., 2025), which is the difficulty our routing study targets. These works show empirically that decomposition and feedback help; our analysis states which properties of the agents and of the feedback yield convergence. Appendix B gives a broader comparison.

## 3. Model and protocol

This section turns the coordination problem from the introduction into an optimization model. We first define the shared task and the corrected objective that each agent searches (Section 3.1), then the dependency graph that determines which changes can be combined safely (Section 3.2), and finally the protocol that combines them (Section 3.3, Algorithm 1).

## 3.1. Task and corrected objectives

The shared solution is $x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})$ : agent $b$ owns block $x_{b}$ , which takes values in a nonempty finite set $\mathcal{X}_{b}$ . The team solves

(1)

 $\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}}F(x),\qquad\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{X}_{m},$ 

for a loss $F:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}$ . An exact verifier returns $F(x)$ for any solution, although its formula need not be known to the agents. Each agent can also cheaply evaluate a local objective $L_{b}:\mathcal{X}_{b}\to\mathbb{R}$ of its candidate blocks $u\in\mathcal{X}_{b}$ , which may ignore how $u$ interacts with the other blocks.

The optimal loss is $F^{*}$ , and the conditional change $f_{b}(u;x)$ is how much the global loss changes if agent $b$ alone replaces its block by $u$ :

(2)

 $F^{*}\coloneqq\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}}F(x),\qquad f_{b}(u;x)\coloneqq F(x_{-b},u)-F(x).$ 

These quantities serve the analysis; agents need not know them. The exact correction $R_{b}$ is the part of the conditional change that the local objective misses, and an agent searches the corrected objective $\widehat{f}_{b}$ built from an available approximation $\widehat{R}_{b}$ :

(3)

 $\displaystyle R_{b}(u;x)$ 
 $\displaystyle\coloneqq f_{b}(u;x)-[L_{b}(u)-L_{b}(x_{b})],$ 

(4)

 $\displaystyle\widehat{f}_{b}(u;x)$ 
 $\displaystyle\coloneqq L_{b}(u)-L_{b}(x_{b})+\widehat{R}_{b}(u;x).$ 

The four functions $f_{b},R_{b},\widehat{R}_{b},\widehat{f}_{b}:\mathcal{X}_{b}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}$ take a candidate block and the current solution. If $\widehat{R}_{b}=R_{b}$ , then $\widehat{f}_{b}=f_{b}$ . We require anchoring, $\widehat{R}_{b}(x_{b};x)=0$ , so that keeping the current block always scores zero. A useful correction $\widehat{R}_{b}(u;x)$ must vary with the candidate $u$ , because adding the same constant to every candidate cannot change their ranking; constructing and communicating it costs work, which Section 7 accounts for.

Neither $L_{b}$ nor $\widehat{R}_{b}$ has to be given to the agent as a formula. An LLM agent receives its task and the information behind the correction in its prompt, for example which collisions the verifier has found, and proposes candidates that it judges better. The functions $L_{b}$ and $\widehat{R}_{b}$ describe the preferences that this information conveys; they let us state what the search must achieve (Assumption 2) without modeling how the agent reasons.

Example.

In the routing task of Section 8.1, $x_{b}$ lists the moves of the three robots that agent $b$ controls, and $L_{b}$ adds their delivery costs and the collisions among them. The exact correction $R_{b}$ is the change in the penalty for collisions with the other agents’ robots. The implemented correction $\widehat{R}_{b}$ computes this change only for the robot pairs whose collisions the verifier has already reported, using the other robots’ current routes.

## 3.2. Dependency graph and coloring

We write the loss as a sum of $M$ real-valued terms, called factors:

(5)

 $F(x)=\sum_{a=1}^{M}\ell_{a}(x_{N(a)}),$ 

where factor $\ell_{a}$ depends only on the blocks in a set $N(a)\subseteq\{1,\ldots,m\}$ , and $x_{N(a)}\coloneqq(x_{b})_{b\in N(a)}$ denotes these blocks. Every loss can be written in this form, trivially as the single factor $\ell_{1}=F$ with $N(1)=\{1,\ldots,m\}$ . Only the sets $N(a)$ must be known; the factors themselves, like the formula of $F$ , may stay hidden in the verifier.

For example, the loss $F(x)=(x_{1}-x_{2})^{2}+(x_{1}-x_{3})^{2}$ of three blocks has two factors, $\ell_{1}(x_{1},x_{2})=(x_{1}-x_{2})^{2}$ and $\ell_{2}(x_{1},x_{3})=(x_{1}-x_{3})^{2}$ , so $N(1)=\{1,2\}$ and $N(2)=\{1,3\}$ .

The dependency graph has one vertex per block and joins blocks $b\neq b^{\prime}$ whenever some factor depends on both, that is, $b,b^{\prime}\in N(a)$ for some $a$ . It records how the objective couples the blocks, not which agents communicate. A proper coloring with $\kappa$ colors is a map $\chi:\{1,\ldots,m\}\to\{1,\ldots,\kappa\}$ that gives adjacent blocks different colors; it splits the blocks into the color classes $\mathcal{C}_{j}\coloneqq\{b:\chi(b)=j\}$ . In the example above, the graph has the edges $\{1,2\}$ and $\{1,3\}$ , and $\chi(1)=2$ , $\chi(2)=\chi(3)=1$ is a proper coloring with $\kappa=2$ , as in step 4 of Figure 1.

The blocks of one factor are pairwise adjacent, so they all have different colors. If several blocks of one color change at once, each factor therefore sees at most one of the changes, and the individual changes add exactly: for every set $S\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{j}$ and candidates $U_{b}$ , $b\in S$ ,

(6)

 $F(x_{-S},U_{S})-F(x)=\sum_{b\in S}f_{b}(U_{b};x),$ 

where $(x_{-S},U_{S})$ is $x$ with each block $x_{b}$ , $b\in S$ , replaced by $U_{b}$ . This makes the merge of Section 3.3 safe.

Fewer colors are better, because this merge keeps at least a fraction $1/\kappa$ of the total verified gain (see (8) in Section 3.3); a greedy coloring needs at most one more color than the maximum degree of the graph. At one extreme, a separable task with exact local objectives, $F(x)=\sum_{b}L_{b}(x_{b})$ up to a constant, has a graph without edges: $\kappa=1$ , the exact correction is zero, every agent can optimize alone and all improvements can be merged at once. At the other extreme, the single factor $\ell_{1}=F$ gives a complete graph and $\kappa=m$ , so only one block can safely be replaced per epoch: changes of two blocks that share a factor may each lower the loss and yet raise it together (Appendix A.1). Corrections and coloring matter exactly when blocks share factors. This safety has a price: the coloring separates any two blocks that could interact for some candidates, even when the proposals of an epoch do not. Section 5 lets the coordinator accept proposals of different colors together whenever the verifier confirms that they lower the loss more than the best color alone.

## 3.3. Search, verification and merging

Algorithm 1 states the protocol, and Figure 1 illustrates one epoch: a round of private search, verification and merging. Within an epoch, all agents search from the same frozen solution $x$ , called the base. Before the search, the correction is updated with the latest verified values, in one of the ways described in Section 6. Each agent returns a proposal $U_{b}$ after $H$ private steps: the best candidate it has found, or its current block if no candidate improves on it ( $\mathrm{Search}_{H}$ in Algorithm 1). The verifier then records its gain over the base,

(7)

 $g_{b}\coloneqq\max\{0,-f_{b}(U_{b};x)\}.$ 

The coordinator then accepts the strictly improving proposals of the color with the largest total gain, which gives the new base $x^{+}$ . By (6), these gains add exactly:

Algorithm 1 Corrected local search with verified merging

 1:

 $x^{0}\in\mathcal{X}$ ; local objectives $L_{b}$ ; proper coloring $\chi$ with $\kappa$ colors; private horizon $H$ ; number of epochs $K$ 

 2:

 for $k=0,1,\ldots,K-1$ do

 3:

   Update the correction with verified values (Section 6)

 4:

   for $b=1,\ldots,m$ in parallel do

 5:

     $\widehat{f}_{b}(u;x^{k})\leftarrow L_{b}(u)-L_{b}(x^{k}_{b})+\widehat{R}_{b}(u;x^{k})$ 

 6:

     $U_{b}\leftarrow\mathrm{Search}_{H}\bigl(\widehat{f}_{b}(\cdot\,;x^{k}),\,x^{k}_{b}\bigr)$ $\triangleright$ $H$ private steps

 7:

     $g_{b}\leftarrow\max\{0,\,F(x^{k})-F(x^{k}_{-b},U_{b})\}$ $\triangleright$ verify

 8:

   end for

 9:

    $j^{k}\leftarrow\argmax_{1\leq j\leq\kappa}\sum_{b\in\mathcal{C}_{j}}g_{b}$ $\triangleright$ best color

 10:

    $S^{k}\leftarrow\{b\in\mathcal{C}_{j^{k}}:g_{b}>0\}$ 

 11:

    $x^{k+1}\leftarrow x^{k}$ 

 12:

    $x^{k+1}_{b}\leftarrow U_{b}$ for all $b\in S^{k}$ $\triangleright$ merge (or Alg. 2)

 13:

 end for

(8)

 $F(x)-F(x^{+})=\max_{1\leq j\leq\kappa}\sum_{b\in\mathcal{C}_{j}}g_{b}\geq\frac{1}{\kappa}\sum_{b}g_{b}.$ 

The coordinator thus keeps at least the average gain of a color, and the shared objective never increases. Applying all individually helpful proposals at once could increase it, because adjacent blocks share factors.

The best color discards the verified improvements of all other colors, even when they would combine well. Section 5 shows that the coordinator may instead accept the best verified combination of the proposals (Algorithm 2), with the same guarantees and a rate set by how well the proposals actually combine.

## 4. Convergence and the error floor

The guarantee rests on three ingredients: accurate corrections $\widehat{R}_{b}$ in (4), effective local search and favorable task geometry. Throughout, $\mathcal{H}$ denotes everything fixed before a search: the base, correction and past randomness. Expectations are over the agents’ randomness, and all constants must hold at every base the run reaches.

Correction accuracy where it matters.

An approximate correction $\widehat{R}_{b}$ can be badly wrong for poor candidates that no reasonable search returns. What matters is its accuracy at two candidates: a true block minimizer $u_{b}^{*}$ of $f_{b}(\cdot\,;x)$ , which the agent aims to find, and the proposal $U_{b}$ that it actually returns.

Assumption 1 (Anchored correction accuracy).

There are constants $\delta_{b}^{*},\delta_{b}^{U}\geq 0$ such that at every epoch the correction is anchored, $\widehat{R}_{b}(x_{b};x)=0$ , and its errors at some true block minimizer $u_{b}^{*}$ and at the returned proposal $U_{b}$ satisfy

(9)

 $\begin{gathered}|\widehat{R}_{b}(u_{b}^{*};x)-R_{b}(u_{b}^{*};x)|\leq\delta_{b}^{*},\\
\mathbb{E}\bigl[|\widehat{R}_{b}(U_{b};x)-R_{b}(U_{b};x)|\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}\bigr]\leq\delta_{b}^{U}.\end{gathered}$ 

A uniform bound $\sup_{u\in\mathcal{X}_{b}}|\widehat{R}_{b}(u;x)-R_{b}(u;x)|\leq\delta_{b}$ implies (9) with $\delta_{b}^{*}=\delta_{b}^{U}=\delta_{b}$ , but it can be far larger. In the routing experiments of Section 8.1, for example, a uniform bound exceeds $10^{4}$ , whereas the errors in (9) measured during the runs are zero at the true block minimizers and about 40 on average at the returned proposals. To verify the assumption, one need not find $u_{b}^{*}$ , which may be hard or impossible to compute; a bound on the error there suffices, and it can follow from the structure of the objective.

Next, local search must make progress on the corrected objective. Let $\widehat{f}_{b}^{*}\coloneqq\min_{u\in\mathcal{X}_{b}}\widehat{f}_{b}(u;x)$ . Since the unchanged block scores zero, an agent can gain at most $0-\widehat{f}_{b}^{*}$ , and we require its $H$ private steps to capture, in expectation, a fixed fraction of this amount. The staircases in step 2 of Figure 1 depict such progress.

Assumption 2 (Conditional local contraction).

The solver starts from the unchanged block, and for some $0<\rho_{b}<1$ its $H$ steps satisfy

(10)

 $\mathbb{E}[\widehat{f}_{b}(U_{b};x)-\widehat{f}_{b}^{*}\mid\mathcal{H}]\leq(1-\rho_{b})^{H}(0-\widehat{f}_{b}^{*}).$ 

With $q_{b}(H)\coloneqq 1-(1-\rho_{b})^{H}$ and $q(H)\coloneqq\min_{b}q_{b}(H)$ , $q_{b}(H)$ is the guaranteed fraction of the available corrected improvement that agent $b$ captures in $H$ steps; in the routing task of Section 8.1, LLM agents capture on average about $\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}$ of this improvement in $H=3$ steps.

Finally, improving single blocks must be enough to make global progress. Let the best block decrease $D_{b}(x)$ be the largest decrease that agent $b$ alone can achieve, and $\Delta(x)$ the remaining gap to the optimum:

(11)

 $D_{b}(x)\coloneqq-\min_{u\in\mathcal{X}_{b}}f_{b}(u;x)\geq 0,\qquad\Delta(x)\coloneqq F(x)-F^{*}.$ 

Whenever the solution is suboptimal, the agents together must be able to improve it in proportion to the remaining gap.

Assumption 3 (Block error bound).

There is a constant $\mu\in(0,1]$ such that every reached base satisfies

(12)

 $\sum_{b}D_{b}(x)\geq\mu\Delta(x).$ 

This excludes single-block traps, where only a coordinated change of several blocks helps, for example two modules whose shared interface can be changed only by editing both at once. On a finite domain, nothing more is needed: some $\mu>0$ exists if and only if every solution that no single agent can improve is optimal. Rearranged, (12) bounds the unknown gap, $\Delta(x)\leq\mu^{-1}\sum_{b}D_{b}(x)$ , so upper bounds on the block improvements certify how close a solution is to the optimum, although $F^{*}$ is unknown (Proposition A.1). In the routing experiments, the ratio $\sum_{b}D_{b}(x)/\Delta(x)$ is at least 0.43 at every recorded base from which the team starts a round (Section 8.1), and on the synthetic task all three assumptions are certified (Section 8.3).

Lemma 1 (Correction error becomes lost progress).

Under Assumptions 1 and 2,

(13)

 $\mathbb{E}[g_{b}\mid\mathcal{H}]\geq q_{b}(H)D_{b}(x)-q_{b}(H)\delta_{b}^{*}-\delta_{b}^{U}.$ 

Proof.

Condition on $\mathcal{H}$ , and write the correction error as $\varepsilon_{b}(u)\coloneqq\widehat{R}_{b}(u;x)-R_{b}(u;x)$ . By (3) and (4), $\widehat{f}_{b}=f_{b}+\varepsilon_{b}$ . At the true minimizer, $\widehat{f}_{b}^{*}\leq\widehat{f}_{b}(u_{b}^{*};x)=-D_{b}(x)+\varepsilon_{b}(u_{b}^{*})\leq-D_{b}(x)+\delta_{b}^{*}$ , and local contraction with anchoring gives $\mathbb{E}\widehat{f}_{b}(U_{b};x)\leq q_{b}(H)\widehat{f}_{b}^{*}$ . Hence

 $\displaystyle\mathbb{E}f_{b}(U_{b};x)$ 
 $\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\widehat{f}_{b}(U_{b};x)-\varepsilon_{b}(U_{b})]\leq q_{b}(H)\widehat{f}_{b}^{*}+\delta_{b}^{U}$ 

 $\displaystyle\leq-q_{b}(H)D_{b}(x)+q_{b}(H)\delta_{b}^{*}+\delta_{b}^{U},$ 

and $g_{b}\geq-f_{b}(U_{b};x)$ proves the claim. Verification also ensures $g_{b}\geq 0$ when this lower bound is negative.
∎

Theorem 2 (Convergence with a correction-error floor).

Under the factor structure, proper coloring and Assumptions 1–3, let

(14)

 $\alpha\coloneqq\frac{\mu q(H)}{\kappa},\qquad B\coloneqq\frac{1}{\kappa}\sum_{b}\bigl(q_{b}(H)\delta_{b}^{*}+\delta_{b}^{U}\bigr).$ 

From a deterministic $x^{0}$ , the iterates of Algorithm 1 satisfy, for every $K\geq 0$ ,

(15)

 $\boxed{\mathbb{E}\Delta(x^{K})\leq(1-\alpha)^{K}\Delta(x^{0})+\frac{B}{\alpha}\bigl[1-(1-\alpha)^{K}\bigr]}\,.$ 

Exact corrections give geometric decay to zero expected gap.

Proof.

By (8), Lemma 1 and the block error bound,

 $\mathbb{E}[F(x)-F(x^{+})\mid\mathcal{H}]\geq\frac{1}{\kappa}\sum_{b}q_{b}(H)D_{b}(x)-B\geq\alpha\Delta(x)-B.$ 

Total expectation gives $\mathbb{E}\Delta(x^{k+1})\leq(1-\alpha)\mathbb{E}\Delta(x^{k})+B$ . Iterating and summing the geometric series proves (15).
∎

The rate has three distinct factors: useful block directions ( $\mu$ ), finding them ( $q$ ), and retaining compatible gains ( $1/\kappa$ ); Section 5 sharpens the third. More local work improves only the second. The floor $B/\alpha$ comes from correction error: more private search cannot bring it below $\sum_{b}(\delta_{b}^{*}+\delta_{b}^{U})/\mu$ , but a more accurate correction lowers it. The floor belongs to an upper bound, not a level at which the search must stall. If the correction error does not change which candidate scores best, the agent returns the same proposal as with an exact correction and loses nothing; in the synthetic task of Section 8.3, for example, small correction biases leave every run at the optimum. Figure 4 evaluates the recurrence behind (15) with per-round values measured on LLM runs, and Figure 5(b) compares observed mean convergence with (15) using certified constants.

## 5. Merging interfering proposals

Algorithm 1 accepts only the improving proposals of one color. Blocks of one color share no factors, so this merge is safe for every candidate and keeps at least a fraction $1/\kappa$ of the verified gain (8). The price is that the verified improvements of all other colors are thrown away. The coloring is built for the worst case: it separates every pair of blocks that could interact for some candidates, whether or not the proposals of a given epoch actually do. In practice, proposals of adjacent blocks often do not interfere at all, and even proposals that do interfere can lower $F$ more together than either does alone. Throwing all of this work away is wasteful. This section shows how the coordinator can keep it and what the theory then guarantees.

Accepting the best combination.

A merge accepts the proposals of some set $S\subseteq\{1,\ldots,m\}$ of blocks and keeps the other blocks unchanged, giving $(x_{-S},U_{S})$ as in (6); $S=\emptyset$ leaves $x$ unchanged. Algorithm 1 uses $S=S^{k}$ , the improving blocks of the best color (line 9). Instead, the coordinator can evaluate $\Phi(S)\coloneqq F(x_{-S},U_{S})$ for every $S$ and accept the set with the lowest loss, even if it contains proposals that interfere (Algorithm 2). Because $S^{k}$ is one of these candidates, this best-combination merge never gains less than the best color: the equality in (8) becomes an inequality, and the gain is still at least $\kappa^{-1}\sum_{b}g_{b}$ .

With many blocks, checking all $2^{m}$ sets is impractical, so the coordinator can use a greedy search instead. Starting from $S^{k}$ , it repeatedly adds the block whose proposal lowers $\Phi$ most, as long as one does; it never raises the loss of the best-color plan, so the same bound holds. Theorem 2 and the convergence results for learned corrections in Appendix A.4 use the merge only through the inequality $F(x)-F(x^{+})\geq\kappa^{-1}\sum_{b}g_{b}$ in (8), so they hold for both versions of Algorithm 2.

Algorithm 2 Best-combination merge

 1:

 base $x^{k}$ , proposals $U_{1},\ldots,U_{m}$ , $S^{k}$ (line 9 of Algorithm 1)

 2:

 if all $2^{m}$ sets can be checked then

 3:

    $S^{k}\leftarrow\argmin_{S}\Phi(S)$ $\triangleright$ all combinations

 4:

 else

 5:

   while $\min_{b\notin S^{k}}\Phi(S^{k}\cup\{b\})<\Phi(S^{k})$ do $\triangleright$ greedy

 6:

     $S^{k}\leftarrow S^{k}\cup\bigl\{\argmin_{b\notin S^{k}}\Phi(S^{k}\cup\{b\})\bigr\}$ 

 7:

   end while

 8:

 end if

 9:

 $x^{k+1}\leftarrow(x^{k}_{-S^{k}},U_{S^{k}})$ $\triangleright$ replaces lines 10–11 of Alg. 1

Realized number of colors.

Theorem 2 needs the merge to keep at least a fraction $1/\kappa$ of the total verified gain $\sum_{b}g_{b}$ . How much Algorithm 2 keeps in epoch $k$ is measured by its realized number of colors

(16)

 $\kappa^{*}_{k}\coloneqq\frac{\sum_{b}g_{b}}{F(x^{k})-F(x^{k+1})},$ 

and $\kappa^{*}_{k}\coloneqq 1$ if no block improves. In words, the merge gains $\sum_{b}g_{b}/\kappa^{*}_{k}$ , whereas the best color is only guaranteed $\sum_{b}g_{b}/\kappa$ ; since the merge never gains less than the best color, $\kappa^{*}_{k}\leq\kappa$ . When all $2^{m}$ sets are checked, the merge accepts the combination with the largest gain, so no other way of combining this epoch’s proposals gives a smaller $\kappa^{*}_{k}$ . The realized number $\kappa^{*}_{k}$ is not the number of accepted groups: if three proposals that gain 100 each gain only 110 together, accepting all three can still be best, and then $\kappa^{*}_{k}=300/110\approx 2.7$ ; if proposals help each other, $\kappa^{*}_{k}<1$ . In the routing experiments of Section 8.1, where any two agents’ robots can collide and Algorithm 1 accepts one block per round on one map, Algorithm 2 accepts on average 1.7 blocks in each round that makes progress, and $\kappa^{*}_{k}$ averages about 1.2 against $\kappa=3$ .

Corollary 1 (Convergence with realized colors).

Under the assumptions of Theorem 2, suppose that Algorithm 2 has $\kappa^{*}_{k}\leq\bar{\kappa}$ in every epoch, for some $\bar{\kappa}\geq 1$ . Then its iterates satisfy (15) with $\kappa$ replaced by $\bar{\kappa}$ in $\alpha$ and $B$ of (14).

Proof.

By (16), $F(x)-F(x^{+})\geq\sum_{b}g_{b}/\kappa^{*}_{k}\geq\bar{\kappa}^{-1}\sum_{b}g_{b}$ . The first relation is an equality unless no block improves, in which case $\sum_{b}g_{b}=0$ and the merge never raises the loss; the second holds because the gains are nonnegative. This is (8) with $\bar{\kappa}$ in place of $\kappa$ , and the proof of Theorem 2 uses the coloring only through (8).
∎

Effective number of colors.

Corollary 1 holds for any $F$ , but $\kappa^{*}_{k}$ can only be measured after the epoch. We can, however, bound it from above in advance. Let us assume that blocks interact only in pairs, that is, every factor of $F$ in (5) depends on at most two blocks. This is the case whenever the coupling consists of pairwise conflicts, as in the routing task of Section 8.1, where each collision term involves one pair of robots. For a base $x$ with proposals $U_{b}$ and $b\neq b^{\prime}$ , the interaction

(17)

 $\xi_{bb^{\prime}}\coloneqq F(x_{-\{b,b^{\prime}\}},U_{b},U_{b^{\prime}})-F(x)-f_{b}(U_{b};x)-f_{b^{\prime}}(U_{b^{\prime}};x)$ 

is zero unless $b$ and $b^{\prime}$ share a factor, and expanding the pairwise factors gives, for every set $S$ of blocks,

(18)

 $F(x_{-S},U_{S})-F(x)=\sum_{b\in S}f_{b}(U_{b};x)+\sum_{\{b,b^{\prime}\}\subseteq S}\xi_{bb^{\prime}}.$ 

Let $I^{k}\coloneqq\{b:g_{b}>0\}$ be the improving blocks of epoch $k$ . Two improving blocks $b$ and $b^{\prime}$ conflict if $\xi_{bb^{\prime}}>0$ , that is, if their proposals together gain less than the sum of their gains, and a set of improving blocks is conflict-free if no two of its blocks conflict. The effective number of colors $\kappa_{k}$ is the smallest number of conflict-free sets that cover $I^{k}$ ( $\kappa_{k}\coloneqq 1$ if $I^{k}=\emptyset$ ), and $\deg_{k}(b)$ is the number of other improving blocks that conflict with $b$ . Whereas $\kappa$ must separate every pair of blocks that could interact for some candidates, $\kappa_{k}$ counts only the groups that epoch $k$ needs. Two blocks of one color share no factor and therefore never conflict, so the $\kappa$ color classes already split $I^{k}$ into at most $\kappa$ conflict-free groups; and one group per improving block always works. Hence $\kappa_{k}\leq\min\{\kappa,|I^{k}|\}$ whenever $I^{k}$ is nonempty. Moreover, if every improving block conflicts with at most $d$ others, $d+1$ groups are always enough: assign the blocks to groups one at a time; a block conflicts with at most $d$ blocks, so at least one of $d+1$ groups contains none of them, and the block joins that group. Hence $\kappa_{k}\leq 1+\max_{b\in I^{k}}\deg_{k}(b)$ for nonempty $I^{k}$ .

Proposition 2 (Realized and effective colors).

If every factor of $F$ in (5) depends on at most two blocks, then every conflict-free set $S$ of improving blocks satisfies

(19)

 $F(x)-F(x_{-S},U_{S})\geq\sum_{b\in S}g_{b}.$ 

Consequently, Algorithm 2 with all combinations checked gains at least $\kappa_{k}^{-1}\sum_{b}g_{b}$ , that is, $\kappa^{*}_{k}\leq\kappa_{k}$ .

Proof.

For an improving block, $f_{b}(U_{b};x)=-g_{b}$ . By (18), accepting a set $S$ therefore changes $F$ by $-\sum_{b\in S}g_{b}$ plus the interactions $\xi_{bb^{\prime}}$ of its pairs. If $S$ is conflict-free, all these interactions are at most zero, which proves (19). Now split the improving blocks into $\kappa_{k}$ conflict-free groups. Their gains add up to $\sum_{b}g_{b}$ , so the best group alone gains at least $\kappa_{k}^{-1}\sum_{b}g_{b}$ . The merge checks this group along with all other sets and accepts a plan at least as good, so $F(x)-F(x^{+})\geq\kappa_{k}^{-1}\sum_{b}g_{b}$ , which by (16) is $\kappa^{*}_{k}\leq\kappa_{k}$ .
∎

What this means.

The three numbers of colors are ordered, $\kappa^{*}_{k}\leq\kappa_{k}\leq\kappa$ . The number $\kappa$ in Theorem 2 is the price of throwing away all colors but one, and it is fixed by how the objective could couple the blocks. Corollary 1 replaces it by an upper bound $\bar{\kappa}$ on $\kappa^{*}_{k}$ , which reflects how well the proposals of each epoch actually combine; the error floor $B/\alpha$ stays the same. The effective number $\kappa_{k}$ gives a guarantee that can be stated in advance: if no improving proposal conflicts with more than $d$ others, then $\kappa^{*}_{k}\leq\kappa_{k}\leq d+1$ , and Corollary 1 holds with $\bar{\kappa}=d+1$ . This matters most for large teams: on a densely coupled task $\kappa$ grows with the number of agents, whereas $d+1$ need not. The realized number can be smaller still, because accepting conflicting proposals together counts whenever it lowers $F$ most.

Cost of checking and limits.

Checking all $2^{m}$ combinations is affordable for a few agents, as in our experiments with three. For more agents, pairwise interactions help: by (18), the loss of every combination of improving blocks follows from one check per improving block and one per pair of them, so about $|I^{k}|^{2}/2$ checks replace $2^{m}$ . Appendix E.9 gives a simple rule that picks a combination from these checks and keeps Corollary 1 with $\bar{\kappa}=d+1$ . Combining proposals cannot, however, remove the need for Assumption 3. At a single-block trap with exact corrections no agent finds an improvement, so every agent returns its current block and no combination of the returned proposals changes anything; Appendix E.9 discusses ways around this.

## 6. Learning corrections

Before every epoch, Algorithm 1 updates the correction with verified values (line 2). This can happen in two ways. First, agents can receive the verifier’s reports in their prompts; in the routing task of Section 8.1, for example, every collision that the verifier reports becomes a known pair whose penalty the correction adds.

Second, when the hidden part of the objective has a known structure, the correction can be learned numerically. Suppose that $F(x)$ equals $G(x)+\langle\vartheta,\phi(x)\rangle$ up to a constant, with a known function $G$ and known features $\phi$ but unknown weights $\vartheta$ . Every evaluated trial solution $z$ then gives one linear equation, $F(z)-F(x)-[G(z)-G(x)]=\langle\phi(z)-\phi(x),\vartheta\rangle$ . The estimate $\widehat{\vartheta}$ moves by the smallest amount that satisfies it, a sketch-and-project step (Strohmer and Vershynin, 2009; Gower and Richtárik, 2015). Updating the estimate in this way before an epoch is called calibration. Agents keep the learned weights across epochs and recompute the known terms when the base changes. If the trial solutions explore every unknown direction on average, the solution and the correction contract jointly at a linear rate set by the slower of search and learning, with no error floor (Theorem A.5 in Appendix A.4). Already evaluated proposals, including rejected ones, can be reused as measurements (Proposition A.6). Without such structure, sparse evaluations cannot determine the values of unqueried candidates (Appendix A.5.2). Appendix G.7 tests numerical learning on the synthetic task.

The cost of learning.

Learning repays its queries when the needed information is sparse and reused; otherwise measuring the alternatives in advance is cheaper (Appendix A.5 accounts for the cost of calibration). If the unknown terms are linear in individual binary variables, one extra verifier query identifies one coefficient exactly, so at most one query per variable is ever needed, and a computable bound on the remaining correction error contracts linearly together with the gap (Proposition A.7, Corollary A.8).

## 7. Private work and synchronization

Longer private search makes each epoch more productive but also slower, whereas frequent synchronization spends time on verification and context transfer. Let one parallel private step cost $c>0$ , and let each synchronization, including correction construction, context transfer and verification, cost $C\geq 0$ . With exact corrections, Theorem 2 shows that $K_{\varepsilon}(H)=\lceil\kappa\log(\Delta(x^{0})/\varepsilon)/(\mu q(H))\rceil$ epochs reach expected gap $\varepsilon$ , at cost at most $(cH+C)K_{\varepsilon}(H)$ , where $\lceil\cdot\rceil$ rounds up to an integer. With $\lambda=-\log(1-\min_{b}\rho_{b})$ , dropping the ceiling and the constant factor $\kappa\log(\Delta(x^{0})/\varepsilon)/\mu$ and using $q(H)=1-e^{-\lambda H}$ gives the relaxed cost $J(H)\coloneqq(cH+C)/(1-e^{-\lambda H})$ for $H>0$ , whose unique minimizer for $C>0$ satisfies (Proposition A.3)

(20)

 $e^{\lambda H_{*}}=1+\lambda H_{*}+\lambda C/c.$ 

Costlier synchronization therefore calls for longer private work: $H_{*}\approx\sqrt{2C/(c\lambda)}$ when $\lambda C/c$ is small. Section 8.3 compares this choice with observed completion costs.

## 8. Experiments

We test the theory in three settings. The main study, robot routing with LLM agents (Section 8.1), asks three questions. (Q1) Can decomposition with corrections let LLM agents solve a coupled task that a single agent struggles with? (Q2) How much does the team gain by merging the best checked combination of proposals (Section 5) instead of a single color? (Q3) Does the bound of Theorem 2, evaluated round by round with constants measured on the runs, hold for the team’s progress, and how closely? Two smaller studies test the remaining parts of the theory. Query planning in SQLite (Section 8.2) asks (Q4) when acquiring correction information pays off (Section 6). A synthetic task with certified constants (Section 8.3) asks (Q5) how tight the bound is, whether a persistent correction error leaves the predicted floor, and how long agents should search between synchronizations (Section 7).

## 8.1. Robot routing with LLM agents

Each robot must reach its goal while avoiding walls and other robots, a multi-agent pathfinding (MAPF) task (Stern et al., 2019). The rules are simple and visual, yet the task remains hard for LLMs (Chen et al., 2025). Specialized solvers such as conflict-based search (Sharon et al., 2012) can solve this problem exactly, and we use them only to certify the optimum. Most importantly, coupling is built in: robots of different agents can collide, so the best routes for one agent’s robots can block another agent’s.

Three views of the same recorded plan, which is optimal. Left: robot one goes down from its start, then right and down to its goal, with Start at tick zero and Finish at tick twenty, and arrival cost twenty. Center: all nine robot routes, with coincident segments separated within their cells. Right: one agent's robots zero, one and six highlighted, with the six other robots faded. Numbered filled markers show starts; matching flags show goals. Gray cells are walls.

Figure 2. Recorded plan at the optimum (first map, team seed 19 after round 4): nine delivered, zero collisions, $F=F^{*}=144$ . (a) Robot 1: arrival at tick 20, cost 20. (b) All nine routes. (c) Block $\{r_{0},r_{1},r_{6}\}$ with peers faded. Markers: starts; flags: goals. Overlapping segments are offset within cells; spatial crossings need not coincide in time.Three views of the same recorded plan, which is optimal. Left: robot one goes down from its start, then right and down to its goal, with Start at tick zero and Finish at tick twenty, and arrival cost twenty. Center: all nine robot routes, with coincident segments separated within their cells. Right: one agent's robots zero, one and six highlighted, with the six other robots faded. Numbered filled markers show starts; matching flags show goals. Gray cells are walls.

Software was our first target, and the introduction uses it as an example. In code, however, strong coupling between the parts that different agents would own appears mainly in large systems, because good design keeps modules apart (Parnas, 1972). Experiments at that scale are hard to control and to analyze: the best program is unknown, so one cannot say how far a team is from it or which interactions slowed it down. Routing, on the other hand, provides a certified optimum, lower bounds and exact block minima, so every constant of the theory can be measured. This small, fully analyzable setting showed us what actually happens during a run. Observations made in it inspired two results of this paper: the realized number of colors (Section 5) and the error condition (9), which concerns only two candidates per agent instead of all of them (Section 4). Nothing in the protocol is specific to routing, so it applies to code as well, at a scale where the parts are strongly coupled (Appendix C.1). Below we describe the task and the agents, compare the team with single agents (Q1), test the merge of Section 5 (Q2) and finally confront the runs with the theory (Q3).

Rules.

Agents write explicit command lists for nine robots on a $16\times 16$ grid with walls, over 48 time steps, which we call ticks. At each tick a robot moves one cell up, down, left or right, or stays in place with wait; directions refer to the map, not to the robot. A route may not enter a wall, and a robot that reaches its goal stays there. The pictures below show, from left to right, the commands, a blocked move, a legal route that arrives at tick 4, and a swap.

Collisions couple the robots. Two robots collide when they occupy the same cell at the same tick. They also collide when they exchange cells during one tick, as in the rightmost picture above, since they would have to pass through each other. Two routes can each avoid every wall and still collide. In the example below, if A and B both move, they meet in the center at tick 1. If B waits one tick instead, A passes first and both arrive safely.

Three panels show the mean gap to the optimum against search time, with the gap on a logarithmic axis; the panels for map 1 and map 2 share a time axis up to about 1,570 seconds, and the two-floor panel extends to about 2,000 seconds. On map 1, the team with best combination falls fastest and ends near 5 after about 950 seconds; the team with Algorithm 1 ends near 6 after about 990 seconds; the team unsafe falls more slowly and ends near 5 after about 1,260 seconds; the best-of-three falls fast at first and levels off near 19; the single agent ends near 12; the team without correction stalls near 310. On map 2, the team with best combination falls fastest but levels off near 13, above Algorithm 1, which ends near 7; the team unsafe ends near 10; the best-of-three near 22 and the single agent near 20; the team without correction stalls near 180. On two floors, the team with best combination falls fastest and ends near 18 after about 1,010 seconds, Algorithm 1 near 13 after about 1,010 seconds, the team unsafe near 15 after about 1,280 seconds, the best-of-six near 21 and the single agent near 29 after about 1,970 seconds; the team without correction stalls near 495. Dotted lines continue the final values of the team without correction. Stars mark curve endpoints.

Figure 3. Mean gap $F-F^{*}$ against search time (logarithmic gap axis); 20 runs per curve, 10 without correction. (a, b) Maps 1 and 2: nine robots, three team agents; (c) both maps as two floors: 18 robots, six team agents. Best-of- $W$ : $W$ parallel single agents, as many as team agents. Dotted: final mean of the team without correction. Variants: Section 8.1.Three panels show the mean gap to the optimum against search time, with the gap on a logarithmic axis; the panels for map 1 and map 2 share a time axis up to about 1,570 seconds, and the two-floor panel extends to about 2,000 seconds. On map 1, the team with best combination falls fastest and ends near 5 after about 950 seconds; the team with Algorithm 1 ends near 6 after about 990 seconds; the team unsafe falls more slowly and ends near 5 after about 1,260 seconds; the best-of-three falls fast at first and levels off near 19; the single agent ends near 12; the team without correction stalls near 310. On map 2, the team with best combination falls fastest but levels off near 13, above Algorithm 1, which ends near 7; the team unsafe ends near 10; the best-of-three near 22 and the single agent near 20; the team without correction stalls near 180. On two floors, the team with best combination falls fastest and ends near 18 after about 1,010 seconds, Algorithm 1 near 13 after about 1,010 seconds, the team unsafe near 15 after about 1,280 seconds, the best-of-six near 21 and the single agent near 29 after about 1,970 seconds; the team without correction stalls near 495. Dotted lines continue the final values of the team without correction. Stars mark curve endpoints.

Loss, local objectives and corrections.

The delivery cost of robot $i$ is $c_{i}(x)=\tau_{i}$ , where $\tau_{i}$ is the tick at which it reaches its goal. If the robot does not arrive within the horizon, its cost is $c_{i}(x)=100+48+d_{i}$ instead, where $d_{i}$ is its remaining shortest-path distance to the goal. Here 100 is the penalty for non-delivery; we also add the horizon 48, so that an undelivered robot always costs at least 100 more than a delivered one, and $d_{i}$ rewards getting closer. Each collision adds a penalty of 100:

(21)

 $F(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{8}c_{i}(x)+100\sum_{0\leq i<j\leq 8}C_{ij}(x),$ 

where $C_{ij}(x)$ counts the ticks at which robots $i$ and $j$ collide. In the example above, the safe plan costs $2+3=5$ , since A arrives at tick 2 and B at tick 3. If both move at once, each arrives at tick 2, but the collision raises the loss to 104. We call a plan safe if it delivers every robot without collisions.

Agent $b$ controls a set $\mathcal{I}_{b}$ of three robots, and its block $u$ lists their commands. Its local objective keeps what it controls, the delivery costs of its robots and the collisions among them:

(22)

 $L_{b}(u)=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{b}}c_{i}(u_{i})+100\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\\
i,j\in\mathcal{I}_{b}\end{subarray}}C_{ij}(u_{i},u_{j}).$ 

Collisions between robots of different agents are the reason why the blocks are coupled. The correction accounts for them, but only for the pairs that the agent already knows about. Let $\mathcal{P}^{k}$ contain the pairs of robots of different agents whose collision the verifier has found before round $k$ . With every other robot $j$ kept on its current route $x_{j}^{k}$ , agent $b$ ’s correction is the change in the penalty for these pairs:

(23)

 $\widehat{R}_{b}(u;x^{k})=100\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{i,j\}\in\mathcal{P}^{k}\\
i\in\mathcal{I}_{b},\ j\notin\mathcal{I}_{b}\end{subarray}}\big[C_{ij}(u_{i},x_{j}^{k})-C_{ij}(x_{i}^{k},x_{j}^{k})\big].$ 

The agent learns these pairs through its prompt: the verifier reports every collision it finds, and from then on the agent sees the other robot’s current route. The LLM never computes (22) or (23); it searches with what its prompt shows. The system scores each of its attempts by $L_{b}+\widehat{R}_{b}$ and keeps the best one as the proposal $U_{b}$ . Any two agents’ robots may collide, so the dependency graph is complete, $\kappa=3$ , and Algorithm 1 accepts one block per round.

Maps.

Figure 2 shows the first map, with three $2\times 2$ walls: (a) one robot’s route and cost, (b) an optimal plan found by the team, and (c) the three robots of one agent. Every run starts from the plan in which all robots wait at their starts, which costs $F=1476$ on this map. No plan can cost less than the sum of the robots’ shortest-path distances, 144 here, and collision-free plans with exactly this cost exist, such as the one in Figure 2(b), so the optimum is $F^{*}=144$ . A plan with a collision or an undelivered robot costs at least $F^{*}+100$ , so any plan within 100 of the optimum is safe. We did not rely on a single map. We repeated every experiment on a second map, on which the robots start from different positions, head to different goals and the walls are placed differently (Figure C.1 in Appendix C). Its optimum is $F^{*}=147$ , and we report the results on both maps.

Agents.

All agents are the open-weight model GPT-OSS-120B (OpenAI, 2025) with medium reasoning, temperature 0.6 and top- $p=0.95$ , served by Groq through OpenRouter (Appendix C.6). Every prompt states the rules once, then shows what the agent can see of the current plan: a labeled map, the routes and costs of its own robots, a timeline of every robot it can see, drawings of the collisions it knows about, and the attempts already checked. A team agent sees its own robots and the robots in its correction (23). The prompt gives no strategy advice. We tried several prompts and found that the way the plan is presented matters a great deal; we use the best one we found (Appendix D). The team has three agents, each owning three robots (Figure 2(c)). Each round is one epoch of Algorithm 1: every agent gives up to $H=3$ answers on the frozen plan, and the coordinator accepts the best checked block.

What the correction does.

Recall robots A and B from the collision example, and suppose they belong to different agents. Once B’s agent knows about A, its correction shows that moving now costs a collision, 100, while waiting costs only one tick. The optimal plan in Figure 2(b) came from such a repair. In team seed 19 on the first map, robot $r_{1}$ swapped cells at tick 11 with robot $r_{8}$ of another agent, a pair that an earlier check had already reported. In round 4, the agent owning $r_{1}$ moved it to another shortest route with the same arrival tick 20. This removed the collision and lowered $F$ from 244 to the optimum 144 (Appendix E.6). The agent’s local loss did not change; only the correction, built from $r_{8}$ ’s route, made the new route better.

Table 1. All outcomes per variant and task (20 runs, 10 without correction); mean $\pm$ sample SD. Safe runs: share of runs whose final plan delivers every robot without collisions. $F-F^{*}$ : final gap to the optimum ( $F^{*}=144$ , 147, 291). Bold: best per column (mean and SD separately), excluding the team without correction. Best-of- $W$ : $W=3$ , or 6 on two floors.

Baselines.

We compare the team with two baselines that use the same model and the same kind of prompt. The single agent sees and scores all nine robots. Each answer replaces the route of one robot of its choice; the agent accepts every improvement at once and stops after 15 answers in a row without improvement. Of the single-agent protocols we tried, this one was the most reliable (Appendix D). The best-of- $W$ runs $W$ copies of the single agent in parallel on the same plan and accepts the best improving answer of each round. With $W$ equal to the number of team agents, three on one map, it matches the team’s parallelism. It stops after five rounds without improvement. So does the team; since each round gives every agent three answers, a team agent, like the single agent, has 15 answers to find an improvement before the run stops. Every variant also stops once the plan is safe and within 9 of the optimum ( $F\leq 153$ on the first map and $F\leq 156$ on the second). This threshold only ends a run early, and every run is judged by its final plan. Search time adds up the latencies of the model’s answers, network overhead included; in a parallel round only the slowest agent counts (Appendix C.6). Unless stated otherwise, each variant has 20 runs per map, which differ in the model’s sampling seed.

The team finds the best plans.

Table 1 lists the outcomes of every variant, and Figure 3(a,b) shows how the mean gap $F-F^{*}$ falls with search time on the two maps. Both maps tell the same story. All three protocols reach near-optimal plans, but the team’s plans are the best and the most consistent. The team is safe in all 40 runs and meets the threshold in 39 of them, whereas the single agent meets it in 32 and the best-of-three in 31. The team also ends with the smallest gap on both maps: its mean is lower on map 1 (permutation tests, $p<0.001$ against the single agent and $p=0.011$ against the best-of-three) and its median on map 2 ( $p\leq 0.002$ ), where a few baseline runs that end with collisions make the difference in means less clear ( $p=0.26$ and $p=0.08$ ). Of the three, only the team’s mean gap falls below 10. The single agent and the team need similar time in median, but the single agent is less predictable: on the first map, seven of its runs took longer than 700 seconds, whereas only two team runs did. Three single agents in parallel are the fastest, with median search times of about four and a half minutes, against seven for the team ( $p=0.011$ on map 1 and $p=0.022$ on map 2). They pay for it with worse plans, and 3 of their 40 runs end unsafe. At nine robots, parallel search thus buys speed, whereas decomposition with corrections buys the best plans. Part of the team’s own speed comes from parallelism: played one after another, its recorded answers would take 1.8 times as long on the first map (Appendix E.10). The team also asks the model more often than the single agent, 47 answers against 28 on the first map, and costs USD 0.23 per run against 0.16 (Table D.7).

Is the correction needed?

If the agents’ parts were independent, the correction would not matter. To test this, we removed it: agents are told nothing about other agents’ robots, so they receive neither the collision reports nor the peers’ routes that carry the correction, while verification and merging stay unchanged (Appendices E and D.2). We ran this variant 10 times per map, and only 2 of the 20 runs end safe (Table 1). Every run on the first map delivers all nine robots but keeps one to five collisions, all between robots of different agents. Without the collision penalties its final gap would average 3.1, close to the corrected team’s 5.9: each agent solves its own part well, and the remaining loss comes entirely from interactions between the parts. In most runs, the search also ends early, but not because the agents run out of answers. In 6 of 10 runs on the first map, every agent’s robots soon take shortest routes without colliding with each other. Each block is then optimal for its own objective, so no agent can improve it, and the plan freezes with its collisions; 7 of 10 runs on the second map end this way. The coupling is real, and the correction is what resolves it.

A larger task: two floors.

To test a larger task, we stack both maps as the two floors of one building. Each floor keeps its own nine robots, so the task has 18 robots, loss $F=F_{1}+F_{2}$ and optimum $F^{*}=144+147=291$ . Robots on different floors never meet. The team has six agents, three per floor, with the same blocks as before. Its dependency graph consists of two separate triangles, so it still needs $\kappa=3$ colors and can accept one block on each floor per round. Since each team agent sees only its own floor, a two-floor team run is simply a run on each floor, played side by side round by round; each round lasts as long as its slowest agent on either floor. We therefore build the two-floor team runs from the recorded runs of both maps, pairing seed $s$ of the first map with seed $s$ of the second, without new model calls. The single agent, in contrast, sees both floors in one prompt and changes one route on either floor per answer. The parallel baseline now uses six such agents, as many as the team has (Appendix E.4).

With 18 robots the team’s advantage grows (Table 1, Figure 3(c)). All three protocols stay safe in every run, but the team meets both thresholds in 19 runs, against 9 for the single agent and 16 for the best-of-six. The team also ends with the best plans: its median final gap is 11, against 22.5 for the single agent and 15.5 for the best-of-six ( $p\leq 0.004$ ). The single agent now struggles with the size of the task. Still changing one route per answer, it needs 63 answers on average, more than twice as many as on one map, and its median search takes 1200 seconds, twice the team’s 595 ( $p<0.001$ ). The team’s time grows less, because both floors are searched at the same time. The best-of-six is faster than Team (Alg. 1) on average, though not significantly in median (means 517 and 656 seconds, $p=0.01$ ; medians 511 and 595, $p=0.19$ ), and it ends with worse plans. Without correction, no run is safe.

Keeping the discarded work.

Because the dependency graph is complete, Algorithm 1 accepts one block per round and throws the other agents’ checked proposals away. In the runs above this happened in most rounds that made progress: in 79 of 121 such rounds on the first map and in 64 of 115 on the second, at least one discarded proposal would also have lowered the loss. On average, the improving proposals of a round, each counted on its own, gained 1.5 times as much as the accepted one. This waste led us to Algorithm 2. In Team (Alg. 2), the coordinator checks every nonempty combination of the changed blocks’ proposals, at most $2^{3}-1=7$ , and accepts the one with the lowest loss; with three agents, checking them all is cheap. Theorem 2 still applies, because the best combination is never worse than the best color. For comparison, Team unsafe merges all changed blocks without any check and keeps the best plan it has seen. Both variants differ from Team (Alg. 1) only in the merge rule and in the one prompt sentence that describes it (Appendix E.5).

Keeping the work pays off (Table 1, Figure 3). In rounds that make progress, Team (Alg. 2) updates 1.7 blocks on average, whereas Algorithm 1 updates only one. This makes it about as fast as the best-of- $W$ , the fastest protocol so far, on map 1 and faster on map 2 and two floors. Its mean gap lies below that of the best-of- $W$ almost throughout the search, so it reaches almost every level of loss sooner, and it ends lower on every task (Figure 3). Against Team (Alg. 1), its mean gap falls faster on every task as well. It is also the first to reach a safe plan, with a median under four minutes on every task. That is 40–50% sooner than Team (Alg. 1) ( $p\leq 0.007$ ) and 22–39% sooner than the best-of- $W$ ( $p<0.001$ on map 2 and two floors, $p=0.27$ on map 1). Its whole search is about 30% shorter in mean than with Algorithm 1 ( $p<0.05$ on every task), with fewer rounds and a lower cost. The final plans stay close: all are safe, and their median gaps are within 2 of those of Algorithm 1 on every task, with no significant difference.

Two caveats remain. First, on map 2 the mean gap of Team (Alg. 2) levels off near 13, above that of Team (Alg. 1). Six of its runs stop above the threshold there, against one with Algorithm 1 ( $p=0.09$ ); the same runs raise its final gap on two floors. These stops were not caused by the merge discarding improvements. In each of these runs some block could still be improved, but for five rounds its agent either found nothing or proposed a change that collided with a robot missing from its correction, and the check rejected that change. Any improvement that passed the check would have been accepted (Appendix E.5). Second, merging without a check does not help. Team unsafe is not significantly faster than Team (Alg. 1), and it is slower in mean on map 1 and two floors. Its plan gets worse in 32 of 185 rounds on map 1 and in 37 of 148 on map 2, and one of its final plans keeps a collision. What makes merging pay off is the check: every merged plan is evaluated before it is accepted. The check need not cover every combination, since the greedy version of Algorithm 2, which needs far fewer checks when there are many agents, keeps the guarantee of Theorem 2 (Section 5).

Measured constants.

To compare the runs with the theory, we audited every search of Team (Alg. 1) offline, computing the exact block minima, with and without the correction, at every recorded base (Appendix E.7). The audit first confirms that the coupling is strong: about a third of the proposals that deliver all three of an agent’s robots without collisions among them still hit another agent’s robot (30% on map 1, 32% on map 2). It then measures the constants of Theorem 2. The block error bound (Assumption 3) holds with $\mu=0.43$ on map 1 and $0.70$ on map 2 at every state that starts a round. Six final states, all within 3 of the optimum, are single-block traps, where only a joint change of two blocks helps. A search captures on average about two thirds of the available corrected improvement (0.66 and 0.65), which plays the role of $q_{b}(H)$ . The correction error is zero at the true block minimizer of every recorded base and averages about 40 at the proposals the agents return (45.5 and 36.5), whereas a uniform bound over all routes would exceed $10^{4}$ . This gap is what the two-point condition (9) exploits.

The bound, round by round.

We then apply the recurrence $\Delta_{k+1}\leq(1-\alpha_{k})\Delta_{k}+B_{k}$ behind Theorem 2 to each run, computing $\alpha_{k}$ and $B_{k}$ from that round’s measurements, and average the resulting bounds over runs (Appendix E.8, Proposition A.2). Nothing is fitted to the loss curve. The bound holds in every round of every run with either merge rule, on all three tasks. It also carries over between runs: on both maps, coefficients averaged over 10 runs of Team (Alg. 1) bound the mean gap of the other 10 in every round. As the agents learn more collision pairs, the correction becomes accurate (Figure 4(b)). The error at the true block minimizers is zero, so $B_{k}$ comes only from the error at the returned proposals. That error averages about 100 in round 1, one collision the correction did not know about, and falls to a few units within six or seven rounds, an empirical counterpart of Theorem A.5. It falls faster with Algorithm 2, to 19 by round 3 against 56 with Algorithm 1: its coordinator checks combinations of proposals, and every collision found in these checks also becomes known to the correction.

With $\kappa=3$ , however, the bound is loose late in a run. On two floors it ends at 396 for Algorithm 1 and 605 for Algorithm 2 (gray curves in Figure 4(a)), while the observed mean gaps are 13 and 18. The cause is the discarded work seen above: with $\kappa=3$ the bound credits a round with only a third of the blocks’ gains, whereas Algorithm 1 accepts the best block and Algorithm 2 often more. The smaller divisors of Section 5 tighten the bound step by step. Dividing by $\min\{\kappa,|I^{k}|\}$ , which bounds the number of colors that contain an improving block, and by 1 in rounds without one, lowers the final bounds to 87 and 191. The effective number of colors $\kappa_{k}$ , which bounds the realized one by Proposition 2, needs checked combinations and therefore applies only to Algorithm 2; it lowers that bound further to 133. The realized number of colors $\kappa^{*}_{k}$ (Corollary 1), which averages about 1.5 for Algorithm 1 and 1.2 for Algorithm 2, gives the tightest bounds, 50 and 70. On all three tasks, the bound with $\kappa^{*}_{k}$ ends within a factor of 3 to 7 of the observed mean gap, instead of 30 to 60 with $\kappa=3$ (Appendix E.9).

Coupling during a run.

Why is the realized number of colors so much smaller than $\kappa=3$ ? Because the coupling changes as the plan improves. Team (Alg. 2) checks every combination, so its runs show which proposals actually conflict (Table E.10). Figure 4(c) sorts its rounds by the effective number of colors $\kappa_{k}$ that their improving proposals need; rounds in which only one proposal improves, or none, are shown apart, since they leave nothing to combine. The worst case $\kappa_{k}=3$ is rare: it occurs in 8 of the 105 rounds with several improving proposals, 6 of them in round 1. A run passes through three phases. At the start, nearly every agent improves its block, and the new routes often collide: in round 1, 34 of 58 pairs of proposals conflict on map 1 and 19 of 56 on map 2, and most rounds need two colors. Conflicting proposals are still worth combining, because delivering robots gains far more than a collision costs; the best combination accepts a conflicting pair in more than half of the first rounds. The plan therefore goes from no collisions to 2.3 and 3.3 on average after round 1. In the repair phase that follows, while the plan still has a collision or an undelivered robot, fewer agents improve per round. Their proposals still conflict often, in half of the pairs on map 1 and in more than two thirds on map 2, frequently because both remove the same collision. Yet in rounds 2 to 4, 16 of the 60 rounds with several improving proposals have no conflict at all, and the best combination accepts all of them ( $\kappa_{k}=1$ ). Once the plan is safe, the task is nearly decoupled: in most rounds at most one agent improves, and $\kappa_{k}$ averages 1.03 and 1.04; from round 5 on, nearly every round has one improving proposal or none. Coupling is thus strongest in the first rounds, when most of the loss is removed, and fades near the optimum, whereas the coloring, which must hold for every candidate, assumes three colors throughout.

In short.

Decomposition with corrections gives the most reliable and the best plans, and its advantage grows with the size of the task (Q1). Checking combinations of proposals keeps the agents’ work, so the team reaches small gaps sooner than parallel single agents and is the first to reach a safe plan on every task (Q2). The bound of Theorem 2, in its round-by-round form (Proposition A.2), holds in every round of every run with a checked merge, and with realized colors it ends within a factor of 3 to 7 of the observed gap (Q3).

## 8.2. Execution feedback in SQLite

This study isolates the acquisition trade-off of Section 6 on a real database engine; the controller is a deterministic program, not an LLM. A query joins two groups of rows through a large edge table, and SQLite can start from either group; the cheaper side depends on the groups’ edge counts, which are unknown in advance. The controller’s correction is a table of learned edge counts: after a query runs, SQLite reports how many edges it visited (SQLite, 2026), and the controller reuses this count for later queries on the same group, as execution feedback does in LEO and Bao (Stillger et al., 2001; Marcus et al., 2021). The alternative is to count the candidate groups in advance, which costs extra queries but avoids mistakes.

Figure 5(a) compares the two on three workloads over three generated databases with 4.9 million edges, charging all measurement time. On a favorable stream of 48 queries, where a few costly groups recur, learning from feedback takes 0.65 of the time of counting in advance (4.92 versus 7.53 ms). Over 192 queries, amortized advance counting narrows this to 0.84. On a balanced stream, where costly groups are frequent and rarely recur, learning loses and takes 1.64 times as long. Learning a correction thus pays off when the needed information is sparse and reused (Appendix F).

## 8.3. Synthetic task with certified constants

Objective.

Six blocks of eight binary variables, $x_{b}\in\{0,1\}^{8}$ , interact along a path. With targets $t_{bj}$ , unary weights $a_{bj}\in[1,1.5]$ and coupling weight $w=0.2$ , the loss is

(24)

 $F(x)=\sum_{b=1}^{6}\sum_{j=1}^{8}a_{bj}|x_{bj}-t_{bj}|+w\sum_{b=1}^{5}\sum_{j=1}^{8}|x_{bj}-x_{b+1,j}|.$ 

The fixed task starts at $x^{0}=1-t$ ; its unique minimizer is $t$ , with $F^{*}=3.4$ and $\Delta_{0}=58.60$ . The private score $L_{b}(u)=\sum_{j}a_{bj}|u_{j}-\tilde{t}_{bj}|$ uses the wrong target $\tilde{t}_{b}=1-t_{b}$ in blocks 1, 3 and 5, a deliberate local/global mismatch that the exact correction removes.

Search and verified assumptions.

Each private step picks a random bit of the block and, with a block-specific probability $\pi_{b}$ between 0.5 and 1.0, sets it to minimize the corrected score; the two colors are $\{1,3,5\}$ and $\{2,4,6\}$ . With exact corrections, $\delta_{b}^{*}=\delta_{b}^{U}=0$ , $\rho_{b}=\pi_{b}/8$ and $\kappa=2$ , and an exact computation certifies $\mu=0.7193$ , rounded down (Appendix G). Theorem 2 then gives, with no fitted coefficient,

 $\mathbb{E}F(x^{K})\leq F^{*}+\left(1-\frac{\mu q(H)}{2}\right)^{K}\Delta_{0}.$ 

Results and the cost of private work.

For every horizon $H$ from 1 to 128 (powers of two), all 2,000 exact-correction runs reach the optimum, whereas none of 2,000 uncorrected runs at $H=8$ does: they stall at mean gap 31 (Figure 5(b)). The certified bound holds at every epoch and is within a factor of two of the observed mean gap for three epochs at $H=8$ ; later, convergence is faster than the worst case. Figure 5(c) charges $H+C$ modeled units per epoch, where $C$ is the cost of one synchronization. Its triangles mark the horizon $H_{*}$ that minimizes $J(H)$ (Section 7), computed from the certified constants alone: 1, 11, 20 and 35 for $C=0,5,20,100$ . Like the observed minima (1, 16, 64, 64), $H_{*}$ grows with $C$ , and its interpolated cost is at most 1.29 times the best tested one. The observed minima lie further right because long private search solves each block exactly, so runs finish in about two epochs, faster than the geometric rate behind $J$ .

Approximate corrections and the floor.

Every exact correction coefficient here receives a fixed bias $\pm\nu$ with random signs, unknown to the agents. Below the smallest coefficient magnitude, 0.605, the bias changes no decision and every run reaches the optimum. Larger biases leave a floor that grows with $\nu$ , from 3.08 at $\nu=1$ to 30.0 at $\nu=2$ for $H=8$ , and is essentially the same for $H=32$ (Figure 5(d)): longer private search cannot remove it. The recurrence with measured two-point errors bounds the floor in every run (Appendix G.6).

## 9. Conclusions and limitations

Dividing a shared task among agents works when each agent can see, through a correction, how its changes affect the others. We formalized this as corrected block optimization with verified, compatible merging, whose rate and error floor are set by correction accuracy, local search quality and block geometry. A coordinator that accepts the best verified combination of proposals keeps these guarantees, and its rate depends on how well the proposals actually combine. In practice, agents need corrections built from verified interactions, given as numbers or as information in their prompts, proposals verified against a common base, and merges that are either verified as a whole or limited to non-interacting changes.

With GPT-OSS-120B, a team of three agents found collision-free plans in all 40 runs on two maps, better than those of a single agent, which was safe in all but one run; three single agents searching in parallel were faster than this team but ended with worse plans more often. On a larger task made of both maps, a team of six agents kept its better plans and needed half the single agent’s median search time. Merging the best verified combination of proposals keeps the agents’ compatible work: it cut the team’s search time by about 30%, let it reach low losses sooner than the parallel single agents and made it, in median, the first to reach a safe plan, with the same guarantees, while merging all proposals unchecked did not help.

Coupling also rose and fell during the solve: the plan had no collisions at the start, several after the first round, when nearly all agents changed their parts at once, and none at the end, when at most one agent still improved in most rounds. We expect the same arc in other shared tasks, because parts that are first changed independently must then be reconciled, and near a good solution few changes remain; a merge that adapts to the conflicts that actually occur, as the best combination does, then pays for coupling only while it lasts.

Limitations.

The block error bound excludes tasks that require coordinated changes of several blocks; in routing it holds at every audited state that starts a round, but a few final states near the optimum are single-block traps. For LLM agents we measure the constants on visited histories but cannot certify the uniform conditional contraction of Assumption 2. Our LLM evidence comes from two maps and their two-floor combination, one model and up to 20 runs per variant. On a single map, a single agent with a well-presented state also solves the task and parallel single agents search faster than Team (Alg. 1). The prompt strongly affects both protocols (Appendix D), so prompts must be matched before protocols are compared.
