## Notation

This table collects the notation used in the main text and the appendices. Each entry gives the meaning of a symbol. The same letter sometimes has a different meaning in a proof or experiment; these uses are listed separately. Block subscripts and fixed arguments are occasionally suppressed in proofs, so $f,\widehat{f},R,\widehat{R},D,g,U,q,\delta,\delta^{*},\delta^{U},\varepsilon$ then denote the corresponding block quantities. The index $j$ denotes a coordinate within a block, a color class, or a single-agent answer, as stated. A hat denotes an implemented approximation or an estimate, not a different task.

Table 2. Notation used in the paper.

Symbol

Meaning

Shared task, local objectives and corrections

 $m$ 

Number of blocks or agents.

 $b$ 

Block or agent index.

 $\mathcal{X}_{b},\mathcal{X}$ 

Finite block domain and product domain $\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{X}_{m}$ .

 $x,x_{b},x_{bj}$ 

Shared candidate solution, its block $b$ , and coordinate $j$ of that block.

 $x_{-b},(x_{-b},u)$ 

All blocks other than $b$ , and the solution with block $b$ replaced by $u$ .

 $x^{0},x^{k},x^{+}$ 

Initial solution, solution at epoch $k$ , and solution after the next commit.

 $u,U_{b},U_{kb}$ 

Trial block value, returned block proposal, and the proposal in epoch $k$ .

 $u_{b}^{*},x^{*}$ 

A true conditional block minimizer and a global minimizer.

 $F(x),F^{*}$ 

Global loss $F:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}$ and its minimum over $\mathcal{X}$ .

 $\Delta(x),\Delta_{0}$ 

Global objective gap $F(x)-F^{*}$ and its initial value $\Delta(x^{0})$ .

 $L_{b}(u)$ 

Available local objective $L_{b}:\mathcal{X}_{b}\to\mathbb{R}$ ; $L$ is its one-block example.

 $f_{b}(u;x)$ 

Exact conditional global change $F(x_{-b},u)-F(x)$ ; $f_{b}:\mathcal{X}_{b}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}$ .

 $D_{b}(x)$ 

Best true block decrease $-\min_{u}f_{b}(u;x)$ .

 $R_{b}(u;x)$ 

Exact anchored correction $f_{b}(u;x)-[L_{b}(u)-L_{b}(x_{b})]$ ; $R_{b}:\mathcal{X}_{b}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}$ .

 $\widehat{R}_{b}(u;x)$ 

Implemented approximation to the exact correction; $\widehat{R}_{b}:\mathcal{X}_{b}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}$ .

 $\widehat{f}_{b}(u;x)$ 

Corrected local change $L_{b}(u)-L_{b}(x_{b})+\widehat{R}_{b}(u;x)$ ; $\widehat{f}_{b}:\mathcal{X}_{b}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}$ .

 $\widehat{f}_{b}^{*}$ 

Minimum of $\widehat{f}_{b}(\cdot;x)$ over $\mathcal{X}_{b}$ .

 $\widehat{F}(x)$ 

Learned global surrogate $G(x)+\langle\widehat{\vartheta}^{+},\phi(x)\rangle$ .

 $M,\ell_{a},N(a)$ 

Number of objective factors, factor $a$ , and the blocks on which it depends.

 $x_{N(a)}$ 

Subvector indexed by the factor’s block set.

 $\chi(b),\kappa$ 

Color assigned to block $b$ and number of colors.

 $\mathcal{C}_{j},j^{k}$ 

Color class $\{b:\chi(b)=j\}$ , and the color selected in epoch $k$ .

 $g_{b}$ 

Verified nonnegative gain $\max\{0,-f_{b}(U_{b};x)\}$ .

 $S^{k}$ 

Blocks committed in epoch $k$ : the improving blocks of the selected color (Algorithm 1) or the best verified combination (Algorithm 2).

 $(x_{-S},U_{S})$ 

Solution $x$ with every block $b\in S$ replaced by its proposal $U_{b}$ .

 $\Phi(S)$ 

Loss $F(x_{-S},U_{S})$ of accepting the proposals of the blocks in $S$ (Section 5).

 $I^{k}$ 

Improving blocks of epoch $k$ , those with $g_{b}>0$ .

 $\xi_{bb^{\prime}}$ 

Interaction of the proposals of blocks $b$ and $b^{\prime}$ : their joint change minus the sum of their single changes; they conflict if $\xi_{bb^{\prime}}>0$ .

 $\kappa^{*}_{k}$ 

Realized number of colors of the best-combination merge in epoch $k$ : $\sum_{b}g_{b}$ over the realized gain (Section 5).

 $\kappa_{k},\deg_{k}(b)$ 

Effective number of colors, the smallest number of conflict-free sets covering $I^{k}$ , and the number of improving blocks that conflict with $b$ (Section 5); $\deg(b)$ in the synthetic task is the degree in the dependency graph.

 $\bar{\kappa}$ 

Bound on $\kappa^{*}_{k}$ in every epoch (Corollary 1).

Convergence and stopping certificates

 $k,K$ 

Current epoch index and prescribed number of epochs.

 $H$ 

Number of private search steps per epoch.

 $\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H}_{k}$ 

History conditioning a local search, or history before epoch $k$ ’s calibration.

 $\delta_{b}^{*},\delta_{b}^{U}$ 

Two-point correction-error bounds (Assumption 1): at a true block minimizer, and in conditional expectation at the returned proposal.

 $\delta_{b}$ 

Uniform absolute correction-error bound over block candidates.

 $\varepsilon(u),\varepsilon_{b}(u)$ 

Correction error $\widehat{f}(u)-f(u)$ of a candidate in the proofs, and of block $b$ in Proposition A.2.

 $Q_{b},e^{*}_{b},e^{U}_{b},\widehat{D}_{b}$ 

In one recorded epoch (Proposition A.2): captured fraction of the corrected improvement, correction errors at a true block minimizer and at the returned proposal, and the available corrected improvement.

 $\rho_{b},\rho,\rho_{\min}$ 

Per-step local contraction parameter and its minimum across blocks.

 $q_{b}(H),q(H)$ 

Captured-improvement guarantee $1-(1-\rho_{b})^{H}$ , and its minimum over blocks.

 $\mu$ 

Block error-bound constant in $\sum_{b}D_{b}(x)\geq\mu\Delta(x)$ .

 $\mu_{\rm fin}$ 

Capped minimum improvement-to-gap ratio on the finite domain.

 $\underline{f}_{b}$ 

Computable lower bound on $\min_{u}\widehat{f}_{b}(u;x)$ .

 $\alpha$ 

Descent coefficient $\mu q(H)/\kappa$ .

 $B$ 

Correction-error term $\kappa^{-1}\sum_{b}(q_{b}(H)\delta_{b}^{*}+\delta_{b}^{U})$ ; at most $\kappa^{-1}\sum_{b}(1+q_{b}(H))\delta_{b}$ under a uniform bound.

 $B_{\delta}$ 

Uniform-error counterpart $\kappa^{-1}\sum_{b}(1+q_{b}(H))\delta_{b}$ of $B$ (Appendix A.2).

 $B/\alpha$ 

Error floor in the expected-gap bound.

 $\Delta_{k},\alpha_{k},B_{k}$ 

Gap after round $k$ and the round’s measured coefficients in $\Delta_{k+1}\leq(1-\alpha_{k})\Delta_{k}+B_{k}$ .

 $\zeta$ 

Relative-error fraction in $(1+q_{b})\delta_{b}\leq\zeta q_{b}D_{b}$ .

 $\varepsilon$ 

Requested objective-gap tolerance; an expected-gap tolerance where expectation is specified.

Learning corrections from global evaluations

 $G(x)$ 

Known part of the structured global objective.

 $\phi(x),r$ 

Known feature vector in $\mathbb{R}^{r}$ and its dimension.

 $\vartheta,\widehat{\vartheta}$ 

Fixed unknown coefficients and their retained estimate.

 $\widehat{\vartheta}^{k},\widehat{\vartheta}^{+}$ 

Estimate indexed by epoch and estimate immediately after calibration.

 $c_{0}$ 

Unknown additive constant in the objective.

 $\Lambda_{b}$ 

Known bound on $\|\phi(x_{-b},u)-\phi(x)\|_{2}$ .

 $z,z_{b}$ 

Feasible calibration probe and singleton proposal $(x_{-b},U_{b})$ .

 $a,a_{b}$ 

Feature displacement $\phi(z)-\phi(x)$ , or that of proposal $z_{b}$ .

 $y,y_{b}$ 

Residual global difference after subtracting the known-part difference.

 $y-\langle a,\widehat{\vartheta}\rangle$ 

Prediction residual for the current coefficient estimate.

 $P$ 

Measurement projector $aa^{\top}/\|a\|_{2}^{2}$ ; zero for a skipped or zero-length probe.

 $I$ 

Identity matrix on the coefficient space.

 $\sigma$ 

Excitation constant in $\mathbb{E}[P\mid\mathcal{H}_{k}]\succeq\sigma I$ .

 $\eta$ 

Learning contraction factor $\sqrt{1-\sigma}$ .

 $\widetilde{\vartheta},\widetilde{\vartheta}^{+},\widetilde{\vartheta}^{\rm next}$ 

Parameter error $\widehat{\vartheta}-\vartheta$ , after calibration, and after extra reused measurements.

 $E_{k}$ 

Upper envelope of the gap along a recorded run (Proposition A.2).

 $\beta$ 

Conversion coefficient $\kappa^{-1}\sum_{b}(1+q_{b}(H))\Lambda_{b}$ .

 $\omega$ 

Potential weight $2\beta/(1-\eta)$ .

 $\tau$ 

Joint rate $\min\{\alpha,(1-\eta)/2\}$ .

 $\mathcal{V}_{k}$ 

Joint potential $\Delta(x^{k})+\omega\|\widehat{\vartheta}^{k}-\vartheta\|_{2}$ .

Binary calibration and observable uncertainty

 $d_{b},d_{\max}$ 

Number of binary coordinates in block $b$ , and the largest block dimension.

 $\Psi(x)$ 

Known coupling function in the coordinate residual model.

 $\vartheta_{bj},\widehat{\vartheta}_{bj}$ 

True and estimated coefficient of binary coordinate $(b,j)$ .

 $M_{bj}$ 

Known initial bound on $|\vartheta_{bj}-\widehat{\vartheta}^{0}_{bj}|$ .

 $s$ 

Bit displacement: signed one-bit change $z_{bj}-x_{bj}\in\{-1,1\}$ .

 $p$ 

Probe probability: probability that a block with unknown coordinates performs calibration.

 $\mathcal{U}_{b}^{k},n_{b}$ 

Remaining unknown coordinates before epoch $k$ , and their number in a block.

 $\Gamma_{k},\Gamma_{b,k}$ 

Radii: sum of remaining bounds $M_{bj}$ over all blocks, or over block $b$ .

 $d_{\rm tot}$ 

Query budget: total number of binary coordinates $\sum_{b}d_{b}$ .

 $\gamma$ 

Radius contraction parameter $p/d_{\max}$ .

 $\beta_{0}$ 

Coordinate-model conversion factor $\kappa^{-1}\max_{b}(1+q_{b}(H))$ .

 $\omega_{0},\tau_{0}$ 

Radius weight $2\beta_{0}/\gamma$ and joint rate $\min\{\alpha,\gamma/2\}$ .

 $\mathcal{W}_{k}$ 

Observable-radius potential $\Delta(x^{k})+\omega_{0}\Gamma_{k}$ .

 $M$ 

Value range: width of a known interval of objective values in the impossibility argument.

Private work and cost accounting

 $c$ 

Private cost: modeled cost of one parallel private-search slot.

 $C$ 

Other per-epoch cost, including synchronization and context preparation; it includes verifier calls in Appendix A.3 and excludes them, charged through $v$ , in Appendix A.5.

 $v$ 

Cost of one scalar global evaluation.

 $K_{\varepsilon}(H)$ 

Sufficient epoch count for expected gap at most $\varepsilon$ .

 $T_{\varepsilon}(H),T_{K}$ 

Sufficient modeled completion cost and cost through $K$ epochs.

 $\lambda$ 

 $-\log(1-\rho)$ , the exponential form of the worst-block local rate.

 $J(H)$ 

Relaxed cost ratio $(cH+C)/(1-e^{-\lambda H})$ .

 $H_{*}$ 

Positive continuous minimizer of $J(H)$ for $C>0$ .

 $\psi(H)$ 

Proof helper: $e^{\lambda H}-1-\lambda H-\lambda C/c$ .

 $N_{\rm q},K_{\rm end}$ 

Synthetic experiments: number of counted global evaluations, and the epoch at which a run completes.

Robot-routing task and its offline references

 $r_{i},i$ 

Robot label and robot index, $i=0,\ldots,8$ .

 $\mathcal{I}_{b}$ 

Set of robots owned by team block $b$ .

 $W$ 

Number of single agents searching in parallel in the best-of- $W$ baseline, equal to the number of team agents.

 $x_{i},u_{i}$ 

Routes: accepted and candidate command arrays for robot $i$ .

 $(x,y),p$ 

Grid coordinates and a free cell in a reference-search state; $x$ increases rightward and $y$ downward.

 $t$ 

Routing tick; the fixed horizon is 48 ticks.

 $s_{i},g_{i},p_{i}(t)$ 

Start, goal and position of robot $i$ at tick $t$ .

 $\tau_{i}$ 

First goal-arrival tick of a delivered robot.

 $d_{i}(p,g_{i})$ 

Shortest legal grid distance from cell $p$ to robot $i$ ’s goal.

 $c_{i}(x_{i})$ 

Delivery cost of robot $i$ . It equals the arrival tick for a delivered robot, otherwise $100+48+d_{i}(p_{i}(48),g_{i})$ .

 $C_{ij}(x_{i},x_{j})$ 

Number of simultaneous vertex and edge-swap conflicts for a robot pair.

 $\mathcal{P}^{k}$ 

External robot pairs discovered before round $k$ .

 $\widehat{R}_{b}(u;x^{k})$ 

Routing correction: anchored collision-penalty change over the retained external pairs.

 $\Delta L_{b},\Delta\widehat{R}_{b}$ 

Change in local loss and in the retained correction for a proposed route block.

 $A_{b}^{\mathcal{P}^{k}}(u;x^{k})$ 

Absolute private score using the retained external pairs.

 $A_{b}^{\rm all}$ 

Absolute block score using every external pair.

 $\bar{d}_{b}$ 

Routing bound: sum of shortest distances for robots owned by block $b$ .

 $A_{b}^{\rm CBS}$ 

Reference score: absolute score of a collision-free delivered reference plan in the soft-penalty certificate.

 $\underline{A}_{i},w_{b}$ 

Single-robot relaxation minimum and joint block witness program.

 $C_{b,\rm miss}^{k}(u)$ 

Conflicts involving undiscovered external pairs against frozen peers.

 $\varepsilon_{kb}(u)$ 

Realized score error $\widehat{f}_{b}(u;x^{k})-f_{b}(u;x^{k})$ .

 $Q_{kb},Q_{s,k,b}$ 

Fraction of the corrected improvement captured by a returned block; the counterpart of $q_{b}(H)$ .

 $\mu_{\rm obs},\mu_{s}$ 

Minimum capped improvement-to-gap ratio over recorded states, or within run $s$ .

 $e^{*}_{s,k,b},e^{U}_{s,k,b}$ 

Measured correction errors at a true block minimizer and at the returned block; realized counterparts of $\delta_{b}^{*},\delta_{b}^{U}$ .

 $\Delta_{s,k}$ 

Objective gap for run $s$ after round $k$ .

 $\alpha_{s,k},B_{s,k}$ 

Round-specific descent and correction-error coefficients computed from all returned blocks.

 $\bar{\alpha},\bar{B}$ 

Pooled arithmetic means of the measured round coefficients.

 $x_{s}^{k},K_{s}$ 

Accepted state at round $k$ in run $s$ , and that run’s stopping round.

 $E_{s,k}$ 

Upper envelope for run $s$ ’s objective gap at round $k$ .

 $\overline{E}_{k},\bar{\alpha}_{k},\bar{B}_{k}$ 

Envelope propagated with round- $k$ coefficients averaged over the runs of one half (check on the other half).

 $\overline{U}_{K}$ 

Mean loss envelope: the optimum plus the mean of $E_{s,\min\{K,K_{s}\}}$ over runs.

 $s$ (replicate), $j,k,h,b$ 

Run index; single-agent step, team round, private step and block indices in the API-seed scheme.

 $a_{j},a_{kbh}$ 

Measured duration of a checkable model answer.

 $t_{kb},t_{\rm team}$ 

Sum of a block’s checked durations in a round, and the team’s search time.

 $T_{\rm parallel}$ 
 $T_{\rm sequential}$ 

Search time of the parallel team and of the sequential replay of the same answers.

 $p$ 

Top- $p$ : nucleus-sampling parameter, fixed to $0.95$ in routing.

Synthetic optimization experiment

 $t_{bj},\tilde{t}_{bj}$ 

Desired target bit and private target bit.

 $a_{bj}$ 

Unary mismatch weight for bit $(b,j)$ .

 $w$ 

Neighbor-coupling weight, fixed at $0.2$ .

 $\pi_{b}$ 

Activity probability of block $b$ ’s randomized coordinate solver.

 $A_{bj}(x)$ 

Exact affine coefficient of candidate bit $u_{j}$ with peers frozen.

 $h_{bj}(x)$ 

Difference between the exact and private affine coefficients.

 $\widehat{A}_{bj}$ 

Private coefficient plus learned unary correction and refreshed coupling coefficient.

 $c_{b},\deg(b)$ 

Total incident coupling weight $w\deg(b)$ and block degree on the path.

 $I(x)$ 

Set of coordinates whose bits differ from the desired target.

 $\mu_{\rm dom}$ 

Unary-dominance certificate $\min_{b,j}(a_{bj}-c_{b})/(a_{bj}+c_{b})$ .

 $\widehat{D}(x)$ 

Sum of all exact affine surrogate improvements at the committed base.

 $\Gamma(x)$ 

Sum of remaining coordinate uncertainty bounds at the current base.

 $\overline{\Delta}(x)$ 

Stopping: certified gap bound $(\widehat{D}(x)+\Gamma(x))/\mu_{\rm dom}$ .

 $\nu,s_{bj}$ 

Persistent correction bias and its random sign in the approximate-correction series.

 $e^{*}_{b},e^{U}_{b},Q_{b}$ 

Realized correction errors at a true block minimizer and at the returned proposal, and the realized captured fraction, in the same series.

Database experiment

 $s,s_{\rm db}$ 

Database source index and database-generation seed.

 $A_{s},B_{s},C_{s}$ 

Left endpoint, edge and right endpoint tables for source $s$ .

 $g_{s},h_{s}$ 

Selected left and right endpoint-group IDs.

 $\ell,r$ 

Join directions: left-first and right-first traversal choices.

 $q,q_{s},a_{s}$ 

Query/report index, selected predicates for source $s$ , and its join-direction choice.

 $v_{s}(\ell,g),v_{s}(r,h)$ 

Edge fan-out of a selected left or right group.

 $J_{q}(a),a$ 

Separable fan-out surrogate and vector of three join directions.

Mathematical operations and summary conventions

 $\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}^{r}$ 

Real numbers and real vectors of dimension $r$ .

 $\langle a,a^{\prime}\rangle,a^{\top}$ 

Euclidean inner product $\sum_{j}a_{j}a^{\prime}_{j}$ and vector or matrix transpose.

 $\|\cdot\|_{2},\|\cdot\|_{2}^{2}$ 

Euclidean norm and its square.

 $\|z-x\|_{1}$ 
 $|z_{j}-x_{j}|$ 

Sum of absolute coordinate differences and scalar absolute value.

 $\mathbb{E}[Z]$ 
 $\mathbb{E}[Z\mid\mathcal{H}]$ 

Expectation and conditional expectation of a random quantity $Z$ .

 $A\succeq B$ 

Positive-semidefinite matrix order: $A-B$ is positive semidefinite.

 $\coloneqq,=,\leq,\geq$ 

Definition, equality and scalar inequality relations.

 $\min,\max,\sup$ 

Minimum, maximum and supremum over the stated index or candidate set.

 $\sum,\times$ 

Sum and multiplication or Cartesian product as indicated.

 $\in,\subseteq,\varnothing$ 

Membership, subset relation and empty set.

 $\log,\exp,\sqrt{\cdot}$ 

Natural logarithm, exponential and square root.

 $\lceil z\rceil$ 

Smallest integer greater than or equal to $z$ .

 $\min\{\cdot\},\max\{\cdot\}$ 

Minimum or maximum of listed scalar quantities.

 $\approx,O(n)$ 

Approximation and asymptotic upper-bound notation.

 $n,\pm,\mathrm{SD},\mathrm{SE}$ 

Sample count, displayed variation, sample standard deviation and standard error.

 $+$ , $*$ 

A plus superscript marks an update; a star marks an optimum. Hats mark approximations or estimates; a tilde marks the private target of the synthetic task and, in Appendix A.4, a parameter error; bars and underlines mark averages or bounds, as defined in each entry.

## Appendix A Further theory and examples

This appendix extends Sections 3–7 and uses their notation; the table on page Notation lists every symbol. All guarantees concern the specified objective $F$ , not requirements that its evaluation omits, and private work means computation without communication, not confidential data. Section A.1 works through small examples of the model. Section A.2 treats a uniform correction-error bound, characterizes the block error bound, gives an optimality certificate and proves the bound along a recorded run used in Section 8.1. Section A.3 proves the private-work horizon of Section 7. Sections A.4 and A.5 develop the learning theory summarized in Section 6, with its certificate and cost. The best-combination merge and its guarantees are stated in Section 5, with a conflict-greedy variant in Appendix E.9.

## A.1. Examples

Why local improvement may not help.

A module can pass its isolated tests while returning a format that another module cannot read. An agent searching only with those tests has no reason to repair the format. We call the difference between what the local and global objectives reward an objective mismatch. More local search does not necessarily make this mismatch larger; it may simply solve the wrong local problem more accurately. Checking a proposal globally can reject it, but rejection alone does not tell the agent how to search better.

The following one-decision example isolates this issue. Let $u=0$ mean retaining an incompatible output format and $u=1$ mean repairing it. Let $F$ be the global objective and $L$ the local objective, both minimized:

(A.1)

 $F(u)=1-u,\qquad L(u)=u,\qquad u\in\{0,1\}.$ 

Starting at zero, even an exact minimizer of $L$ keeps the incompatible format, whereas $F$ is minimized at one.

Verification therefore protects the current solution but does not resolve the cause of the failed search. To produce a useful proposal, the agent needs a different criterion for comparing its candidates, and the correction supplies it. At base $u=0$ , the local change from choosing one is $+1$ , while the global change is $-1$ . A correction of $-2$ to that candidate’s local change makes the corrected score prefer the repair, and the correction at the unchanged candidate is zero, as anchoring requires.

A two-block example.

Let $x_{1},x_{2}\in\{0,1\}$ encode two decisions. Each local objective prefers zero, but the global objective prefers one and also penalizes disagreement:

(A.2)

 $\begin{gathered}L_{1}(u)=L_{2}(u)=u,\\
F(x)=(1-x_{1})+(1-x_{2})+\tfrac{1}{4}(x_{1}-x_{2})^{2}.\end{gathered}$ 

Starting from $x=(0,0)$ , the loss is 2. Changing either block to one lowers it to $1.25$ , although its local objective rises by 1. Thus the exact anchored correction at that candidate is $-1.75$ : adding it to the local change gives the true change $-0.75$ . For either block, the corrected score of leaving it unchanged is zero.

Block-1 choice
Local change
Correction
Sum

Keep $u=0$ 
0
0
0

Choose $u=1$ 
1
 $-1.75$ 
 $-0.75$ 

Section A.4 returns to it to show how objective evaluations reveal its unknown correction coefficients.

One update.

The two blocks share the disagreement factor, so they have different colors. With exact local minimization, both propose one, with singleton gain $0.75$ . Either color is best. Break the tie in favor of block 1: the next solution is $(1,0)$ and its loss is $1.25$ . At this new base, changing block 2 to one improves the loss by $1.25$ , and the next update reaches $(1,1)$ with loss zero. Notice that the conditional score changed when its neighboring block changed; a correction must be evaluated at the current base.

Changes that clash.

Two blocks that share a factor can each lower the loss alone and yet raise it together. On two binary blocks, let

 $F(x)=(1-x_{1})+(1-x_{2})+3x_{1}x_{2}.$ 

From $x=(0,0)$ , with loss 2, changing either block to one lowers the loss to 1, but changing both raises it to 3, like two robots that take the same shortcut and collide. The factor $3x_{1}x_{2}$ joins the two blocks, so they have different colors, and Algorithm 1 accepts only one of the two changes.

A single-block trap.

Even a perfect correction cannot resolve a task that violates Assumption 3. For example, on two binary blocks let $F(0,0)=1$ , $F(1,0)=F(0,1)=2$ , and $F(1,1)=0$ . At $(0,0)$ , neither single-block change helps, so $D_{1}+D_{2}=0$ while the global gap is 1. Joint search or acceptance of intermediate worsening is needed. The parameter $\mu$ describes the task, rather than the agent or communication protocol.

## A.2. Further convergence results

A uniform correction-error bound.

A bound on the correction error at every candidate,

(A.3)

 $\sup_{u\in\mathcal{X}_{b}}|\widehat{R}_{b}(u;x)-R_{b}(u;x)|\leq\delta_{b},$ 

implies Assumption 1 with $\delta_{b}^{*}=\delta_{b}^{U}=\delta_{b}$ . Lemma 1 then gives

(A.4)

 $\mathbb{E}[g_{b}\mid\mathcal{H}]\geq q_{b}(H)D_{b}(x)-(1+q_{b}(H))\delta_{b},$ 

and Theorem 2 holds with $B=B_{\delta}\coloneqq\kappa^{-1}\sum_{b}(1+q_{b}(H))\delta_{b}$ . Such a bound must also hold for poor candidates that no reasonable search returns, so it can be far larger than the two-point constants; in routing it exceeds $10^{4}$ (Appendix E.7). Lemma 1 and the first step of the proof of Theorem 2 hold epoch by epoch, conditionally on $\mathcal{H}$ , so they remain valid when $\delta_{b}$ changes between epochs:

(A.5)

 $\mathbb{E}[F(x)-F(x^{+})\mid\mathcal{H}]\geq\alpha\Delta(x)-B_{\delta}.$ 

The learning results of Sections A.4 and A.5 use them in this form.

A relative correction error.

If instead $q_{b}(H)\delta_{b}^{*}+\delta_{b}^{U}\leq\zeta q_{b}(H)D_{b}(x)$ for a fixed $\zeta<1$ at every epoch, the proof of Theorem 2 gives the contraction factor $1-(1-\zeta)\mu q(H)/\kappa$ and no floor. This relative certificate is useful analytically but is not automatically observable: $D_{b}$ is usually unknown.

How restrictive is the block error bound?

On a finite domain, existence of some positive constant has a simple characterization. The size of that constant is a separate question: it may be arbitrarily small. The following observation also explains when a computable stopping certificate is available.

Proposition A.1 (Block geometry and an optimality certificate).

On the full finite product domain, a constant $\mu\in(0,1]$ satisfying (12) exists if and only if every point with $D_{b}(x)=0$ for all $b$ is globally optimal. When this local-to-global property holds and there are suboptimal points, one valid choice is

(A.6)

 $\mu_{\rm fin}\coloneqq\min\left\{1,\min_{x:\Delta(x)>0}\frac{\sum_{b}D_{b}(x)}{\Delta(x)}\right\}.$ 

If every point is optimal, take $\mu_{\rm fin}=1$ . Moreover, at any base where a known $\mu>0$ satisfies (12), exact block improvements give

(A.7)

 $\Delta(x)\overset{\eqref{eq:eb}}{\leq}\frac{1}{\mu}\sum_{b}D_{b}(x).$ 

More generally, suppose certified bounds $\delta_{b}$ satisfy (A.3) and computable lower bounds $\underline{f}_{b}\leq\min_{u}\widehat{f}_{b}(u;x)$ are available. Then

(A.8)

 $\Delta(x)\overset{\eqref{eq:eb},\eqref{dev:eq:error},\eqref{eq:correction},\eqref{eq:surrogate}}{\leq}\frac{1}{\mu}\sum_{b}(\delta_{b}-\underline{f}_{b}).$ 

Proof idea.

Finiteness turns strictly positive improvement ratios into a positive minimum. For certification, the error bound converts upper bounds on exact block improvements into an upper bound on the global gap.

Proof.

If (12) holds and all $D_{b}(x)$ vanish, then $\Delta(x)=0$ . Conversely, the stated local-to-global property makes every ratio in (A.6) positive; the nonempty set of ratios is finite, so its minimum is positive. At a global optimum every $D_{b}$ is zero, and hence the resulting constant satisfies (12) everywhere. For the approximate certificate, uniform error gives

(A.9)

 $\begin{gathered}\min_{u}f_{b}(u;x)\overset{\eqref{dev:eq:error},\eqref{eq:correction},\eqref{eq:surrogate}}{\geq}\min_{u}\widehat{f}_{b}(u;x)-\delta_{b}\geq\underline{f}_{b}-\delta_{b},\\
D_{b}(x)\overset{\eqref{eq:gap},\eqref{dev:eq:error},\eqref{eq:correction},\eqref{eq:surrogate}}{\leq}\delta_{b}-\underline{f}_{b}.\end{gathered}$ 

Combining these bounds yields

 $\Delta(x)\overset{\eqref{eq:eb}}{\leq}\frac{1}{\mu}\sum_{b}D_{b}(x)\overset{\eqref{eq:stop-block}}{\leq}\frac{1}{\mu}\sum_{b}(\delta_{b}-\underline{f}_{b}),$ 

which proves (A.8). The same bound holds with $\delta_{b}$ replaced by a bound $\delta_{b}^{*}$ on the error at a true block minimizer.
∎

Neither enumerating (A.6) nor solving all block subproblems is automatically cheap. Approximate proposals give lower bounds on available improvement, whereas stopping needs upper bounds. An exact block solve or a certified lower bound on each surrogate minimum supplies the missing direction. A known valid $\mu$ is also required. Thus the generic local solver does not automatically provide a stopping test, but the theory does permit one when this additional information is available.

Local-to-global results in discrete convex analysis depend on the allowed neighborhood: coordinated subset moves or exchanges may be essential (Murota, 2003). Merely calling an objective discrete convex does not establish (12) for the fixed disjoint blocks used here.

A bound along a recorded run.

The argument of Lemma 1 also applies to a single realized epoch, with realized local progress in place of $q_{b}(H)$ and realized two-point errors in place of $\delta_{b}^{*},\delta_{b}^{U}$ .

Proposition A.2 (Bound along a recorded run).

Fix a realized epoch $k$ with base $x=x^{k}$ and $\Delta(x)>0$ . Suppose that every returned block scores no worse than the current block, that is, $\widehat{f}_{b}(U_{b};x)\leq 0$ . Write

 $\varepsilon_{b}(u)\coloneqq\widehat{f}_{b}(u;x)-f_{b}(u;x),\qquad\widehat{D}_{b}\coloneqq-\min_{u}\widehat{f}_{b}(u;x).$ 

Let $Q_{b}\coloneqq-\widehat{f}_{b}(U_{b};x)/\widehat{D}_{b}\in[0,1]$ if $\widehat{D}_{b}>0$ and $Q_{b}\coloneqq 0$ otherwise, and let $e^{*}_{b}\coloneqq|\varepsilon_{b}(u_{b}^{*})|$ for a true block minimizer $u_{b}^{*}$ and $e^{U}_{b}\coloneqq|\varepsilon_{b}(U_{b})|$ . Then the merge of Algorithm 1 gives

(A.10)

 $\Delta(x^{+})\leq\min\{\Delta(x),\,(1-\alpha_{k})\Delta(x)+B_{k}\},$ 

where

 $\alpha_{k}\coloneqq\min\Bigl\{1,\frac{\sum_{b}Q_{b}D_{b}(x)}{\kappa\Delta(x)}\Bigr\},\qquad B_{k}\coloneqq\frac{1}{\kappa}\sum_{b}(Q_{b}e^{*}_{b}+e^{U}_{b}).$ 

Consequently, if every epoch with $\Delta(x^{k})>0$ satisfies these hypotheses, and with $\alpha_{k}\coloneqq B_{k}\coloneqq 0$ once $\Delta(x^{k})=0$ , which then persists because the objective never increases, the sequence $E_{0}=\Delta(x^{0})$ , $E_{k+1}=\min\{E_{k},(1-\alpha_{k})E_{k}+B_{k}\}$ satisfies $\Delta(x^{k})\leq E_{k}$ for every $k$ .

Proof.

If $\widehat{D}_{b}>0$ , then $-\widehat{D}_{b}\leq\widehat{f}_{b}(u_{b}^{*};x)=-D_{b}(x)+\varepsilon_{b}(u_{b}^{*})$ , so $-\widehat{f}_{b}(U_{b};x)=Q_{b}\widehat{D}_{b}\geq Q_{b}(D_{b}(x)-e^{*}_{b})$ . Hence

 $\displaystyle g_{b}\geq-f_{b}(U_{b};x)$ 
 $\displaystyle=-\widehat{f}_{b}(U_{b};x)+\varepsilon_{b}(U_{b})$ 

 $\displaystyle\geq Q_{b}D_{b}(x)-Q_{b}e^{*}_{b}-e^{U}_{b}.$ 

If $\widehat{D}_{b}=0$ , the right-hand side is $-e^{U}_{b}\leq 0\leq g_{b}$ . Summing over blocks and using (8), $\Delta(x)-\Delta(x^{+})\geq\kappa^{-1}\sum_{b}g_{b}\geq\alpha_{k}\Delta(x)-B_{k}$ ; capping $\alpha_{k}$ at one only weakens this inequality, and $\Delta(x^{+})\leq\Delta(x)$ because the objective never increases. Since $1-\alpha_{k}\geq 0$ , induction on (A.10) gives $\Delta(x^{k})\leq E_{k}$ .
∎

The proposition uses only quantities realized in one run, so it holds on every recorded trajectory without assumptions on the local solver beyond returning a block that scores no worse than the current one. Under the best-combination merge, $\kappa$ can be replaced by the realized number of colors $\kappa^{*}_{k}$ (Corollary 1), and, with pairwise factors and all combinations checked, by the effective number of colors $\kappa_{k}$ (Proposition 2, Appendix E.9). Appendix E.8 evaluates it on the recorded LLM runs.

## A.3. How much private work is worth doing

Section 7 charges at most $c>0$ for one parallel private step, which lasts as long as its slowest agent, and at most $C\geq 0$ for each synchronization: correction construction, certification, message transfer, loading, singleton verification and applying the selected edits. These bounds are uniform, and $C$ does not grow with $H$ . With exact corrections, let $T_{\varepsilon}(H)$ denote the modeled cost of running enough epochs to make the expected gap at most $\varepsilon$ . For $0<\varepsilon<\Delta(x^{0})$ ,

(A.11)

 $\begin{gathered}K_{\varepsilon}(H)\coloneqq\left\lceil\frac{\kappa}{\mu q(H)}\log\frac{\Delta(x^{0})}{\varepsilon}\right\rceil,\\
T_{\varepsilon}(H)\leq(cH+C)K_{\varepsilon}(H).\end{gathered}$ 

Indeed, using $1-t\leq e^{-t}$ ,

 $\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathbb{E}\Delta(x^{K_{\varepsilon}})&\overset{\eqref{eq:rate}}{\leq}&(1-\alpha)^{K_{\varepsilon}}\Delta(x^{0})\\
&\leq&e^{-\alpha K_{\varepsilon}}\Delta(x^{0})\overset{\eqref{eq:constants},\eqref{dev:eq:time}}{\leq}\varepsilon.\end{array}$ 

If correction size or evaluation cost grows with the horizon, one must replace $C$ by that actual bound before optimizing. Token work also grows with the total number of agents’ private steps.

Let $\rho\coloneqq\min_{b}\rho_{b}$ and $\lambda\coloneqq-\log(1-\rho)>0$ . Dropping the outer ceiling in (A.11) and allowing a real-valued horizon yields the objective

(A.12)

 $J(H)\coloneqq\frac{cH+C}{1-e^{-\lambda H}},\qquad H>0.$ 

Proposition A.3 (A finite useful private-work horizon).

For $C>0$ , $J$ has a unique positive minimizer $H_{*}$ , characterized by (20). Over integer $H\geq 1$ , it suffices to compare the neighboring integers around $H_{*}$ , clamped to one. For $C=0$ , the integer optimum is $H=1$ . These optimize the relaxed bound, not necessarily the ceiled bound or actual runtime.

Proof idea.

The derivative has the sign of a single strictly increasing function. Its unique zero balances the extra cost of one more private step against the additional improvement that step buys.

Proof.

Differentiating (A.12) gives

(A.13)

 $J^{\prime}(H)\overset{\eqref{eq:ratio}}{=}\frac{c[1-e^{-\lambda H}]-\lambda(cH+C)e^{-\lambda H}}{(1-e^{-\lambda H})^{2}}.$ 

Its sign is the sign of $\psi(H)=e^{\lambda H}-1-\lambda H-\lambda C/c$ . For $C>0$ , $\psi(0)<0$ , $\psi^{\prime}(H)=\lambda(e^{\lambda H}-1)>0$ for $H>0$ , and $\psi(H)$ tends to infinity. Hence there is exactly one zero, with $J$ decreasing before and increasing after it. This proves both the continuous and integer claims. For $C=0$ , $e^{t}>1+t$ for $t>0$ gives $J^{\prime}(H)>0$ .
∎

As $H$ increases, $q(H)$ approaches one: the analysis interpolates between inexact and exact block minimization. It supplies a batching calculation, not ProxSkip-type communication acceleration.

## A.4. Learning the correction

A global objective evaluation can teach the correction if it reveals something reusable. We obtain this property by assuming that the unknown part of the objective has fixed coefficients multiplying known features. Evaluations then give linear equations for those coefficients. Learning them once can help evaluate many later candidates, even as the solution changes.

In the next assumption, $G(x)$ is the known part of the objective. The vector $\phi(x)$ contains $r$ known numerical features, and the unknown vector $\vartheta$ gives their weights. A probe is a feasible candidate evaluated to learn these weights; it need not become the team’s next solution. We call these learning evaluations calibration.

Assumption A.1 (Static identifiable residual).

For a positive integer $r$ , there are a known function $G$ , known features $\phi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}$ , and an unknown fixed vector $\vartheta\in\mathbb{R}^{r}$ such that

(A.14)

 $F(x)=G(x)+\langle\vartheta,\phi(x)\rangle+c_{0}.$ 

The unknown constant $c_{0}$ cancels in differences. Known terms and features can be evaluated locally after context loading; their costs are charged. Exact scalar verifier queries are permitted on feasible speculative solutions. Feature changes obey known bounds

(A.15)

 $\|\phi(x_{-b},u)-\phi(x)\|_{2}\leq\Lambda_{b}.$ 

The representation helps only if $r$ and the cost of evaluating $G$ and $\phi$ are small. The unknown residual here means $F-G-c_{0}$ ; the measurement residual introduced next instead means an observed value minus its prediction.

An objective difference gives a linear measurement.

At base $x$ , query a feasible probe $z$ and compute

(A.16)

 $\begin{gathered}a\coloneqq\phi(z)-\phi(x),\\
y\coloneqq F(z)-F(x)-[G(z)-G(x)]\\
\overset{\eqref{dev:eq:features}}{=}\langle a,\vartheta\rangle.\end{gathered}$ 

Let $\widehat{\vartheta}$ denote the current estimate of the unknown weights. Its predicted measurement is $\langle a,\widehat{\vartheta}\rangle$ , so $y-\langle a,\widehat{\vartheta}\rangle$ is the prediction error, or residual. For $a\neq 0$ , the following update moves the estimate to the nearest vector consistent with the observed measurement. The superscript $+$ denotes the updated estimate, which is retained for later epochs:

(A.17)

 $\widehat{\vartheta}^{+}\coloneqq\widehat{\vartheta}+\frac{y-\langle a,\widehat{\vartheta}\rangle}{\|a\|_{2}^{2}}a.$ 

For intuition, return to the one-bit example and take the known part to be $G(u)=L(u)=u$ and the feature to be $\phi(u)=u$ . The unknown coefficient is $\vartheta=-2$ and the constant is $c_{0}=1$ . Evaluating the change from zero to one gives $a=1$ and $y=-2$ : the global change is $-1$ and the known local change is $+1$ . Starting from an estimate of zero, the update sets it to $-2$ . The corrected local objective then prefers one. This example requires only one measurement because it has one unknown coefficient; larger models need enough informative directions.

Learning the two-block example.

The task (A.2) has known part and features

(A.18)

 $\begin{gathered}G(x)=x_{1}+x_{2}+\tfrac{1}{4}(x_{1}-x_{2})^{2},\quad\phi(x)=(x_{1},x_{2}),\\
\vartheta=(-2,-2),\qquad c_{0}=2.\end{gathered}$ 

The algorithm knows $G$ and $\phi$ , but must learn $\vartheta$ . Start with $\widehat{\vartheta}=(0,0)$ at $x=(0,0)$ . Evaluating the probe $z=(1,0)$ gives global change $-0.75$ and known-part change $1.25$ . Their difference is $y=-2$ , while $a=(1,0)$ . Update (A.17) therefore sets the first estimated coefficient to $-2$ and leaves the second at zero. The probe is a measurement; it does not itself change the shared solution.

With that estimate, block 1’s corrected candidate now has score $-0.75$ , so local search can propose it and verification can accept it. After this accepted update, the base is $(1,0)$ . A probe at $(1,1)$ gives global change $-1.25$ , known-part change $0.75$ , and hence the same unknown coefficient value $-2$ for block 2. Its exact correction is now $-2.25$ , rather than the value $-1.75$ at the original base. The coefficient is static; the complete correction function is not. Known coupling terms must still be recomputed at the current context.

This two-query sequence illustrates coefficient reuse. It is an explanatory sequence; a general rate needs the excitation assumption below.

The update is the established Kaczmarz/sketch-and-project estimator (Strohmer and Vershynin, 2009; Gower and Richtárik, 2015). Ordinary verified edits can supply such measurements too, but they need not explore all parameter directions. We therefore permit deliberate calibration probes that are evaluated without being committed.

Measurements must reveal every unknown direction, not repeatedly test the same one. The next assumption expresses this coverage, often called excitation. Here $I$ is the identity matrix, $\succeq$ is the positive-semidefinite matrix order, and $\mathcal{H}_{k}$ is the history before epoch $k$ ’s calibration. A larger $\sigma$ means a stronger guaranteed coverage of parameter directions.

Assumption A.2 (Exciting calibration).

Conditionally on all past information before calibration, the measurement projector $P=aa^{\top}/\|a\|_{2}^{2}$ satisfies

(A.19)

 $\mathbb{E}[P\mid\mathcal{H}_{k}]\succeq\sigma I,\qquad\sigma\in(0,1/r].$ 

A skipped probe has $P=0$ and leaves the estimate unchanged; zero-length measurements are skipped. Their probability is included in $\sigma$ .

For example, uniformly sampling one of the $r$ coordinate directions gives $\mathbb{E}[P]=I/r$ , so $\sigma=1/r$ is valid. Taking traces also shows that a single rank-one probe per epoch cannot give $\sigma>1/r$ . Thus the amount and diversity of measurement information matter. This assumption is a property of an implementable probe distribution, not a consequence of successful task edits. The coordinate specialization below instead samples the remaining uncertain coordinates and uses an observable-radius analysis.

From the estimate to private work.

After calibration, replace the unknown vector in the exact correction by its estimate:

(A.20)

 $\begin{gathered}\widehat{R}_{b}(u;x)\coloneqq G(x_{-b},u)-G(x)\\
{}-[L_{b}(u)-L_{b}(x_{b})]+\langle\widehat{\vartheta}^{+},\phi(x_{-b},u)-\phi(x)\rangle.\end{gathered}$ 

Substituting into the surrogate makes the implemented local score explicit:

(A.21)

 $\begin{gathered}\widehat{f}_{b}(u;x)\overset{\eqref{eq:surrogate},\eqref{eq:learnedcorrection}}{=}G(x_{-b},u)-G(x)\\
{}+\langle\widehat{\vartheta}^{+},\phi(x_{-b},u)-\phi(x)\rangle.\end{gathered}$ 

Thus the implemented method is block descent on the learned global surrogate $\widehat{F}(x)=G(x)+\langle\widehat{\vartheta}^{+},\phi(x)\rangle$ . The local objective cancels algebraically; in this representation it supplies a modeling decomposition, not an extra source of optimization power. This exposes the actual computational requirement: after receiving the needed context and coefficients, the agent must evaluate the known terms and features cheaply. The saving comes from this representation and reuse, not from subtracting and adding $L_{b}$ . A coefficient vector whose evaluation is as expensive as the verifier would not solve the cost problem.

Then run the same $H$ -step private solver, verify singleton gains, and merge the best color. Write $\widehat{\vartheta}=\widehat{\vartheta}^{k}$ for the estimate before the calibration of epoch $k$ and $\widehat{\vartheta}^{+}=\widehat{\vartheta}^{k+1}$ for the estimate after it. With $\widetilde{\vartheta}\coloneqq\widehat{\vartheta}-\vartheta$ and $\widetilde{\vartheta}^{+}\coloneqq\widehat{\vartheta}^{+}-\vartheta$ , the surrogate error is bounded by

(A.22)

 $\sup_{u}|\widehat{R}_{b}(u;x)-R_{b}(u;x)|\overset{\eqref{eq:correction},\eqref{dev:eq:features},\eqref{dev:eq:featurebound},\eqref{eq:learnedcorrection}}{\leq}\Lambda_{b}\|\widetilde{\vartheta}^{+}\|_{2}.$ 

The unknown error norm is only used in analysis. Section A.5 supplies an observable certificate for coordinate residuals.

Lemma A.4 (Learning contracts a persistent error).

Under Assumptions A.1 and A.2, let $\eta\coloneqq\sqrt{1-\sigma}<1$ . Then

(A.23)

 $\mathbb{E}[\|\widetilde{\vartheta}^{+}\|_{2}\mid\mathcal{H}_{k}]\leq\eta\|\widetilde{\vartheta}\|_{2}.$ 

Proof idea.

The update removes precisely the component of coefficient error along the measured direction. The excitation assumption ensures that, on average, every direction loses a definite fraction of its squared error.

Proof.

An active update is an orthogonal projection of the error:

(A.24)

 $\begin{gathered}\widetilde{\vartheta}^{+}\overset{\eqref{dev:eq:measurement},\eqref{dev:eq:project}}{=}(I-P)\widetilde{\vartheta},\\
\|\widetilde{\vartheta}^{+}\|_{2}^{2}\overset{\eqref{dev:eq:measurement},\eqref{dev:eq:project}}{=}\|\widetilde{\vartheta}\|_{2}^{2}-\widetilde{\vartheta}^{\top}P\widetilde{\vartheta}.\end{gathered}$ 

The same identities hold for a skipped update with $P=0$ . Conditioning and applying the excitation bound gives

(A.25)

 $\mathbb{E}[\|\widetilde{\vartheta}^{+}\|_{2}^{2}\mid\mathcal{H}_{k}]\overset{\eqref{eq:pythagoras},\eqref{dev:eq:excitation}}{\leq}(1-\sigma)\|\widetilde{\vartheta}\|_{2}^{2}.$ 

Jensen’s inequality for the square root proves (A.23). Since $\vartheta$ is fixed, committing a new solution does not change the parameter error. This persistence is the crucial benefit of the representation.
∎

We can now track both processes together. Searching with an inaccurate correction leaves an additional task-error term proportional to the parameter error; calibration reduces that parameter error. Weighting the two errors appropriately gives one quantity that decreases geometrically. This quantity is an analysis tool: it contains the unknown optimum and unknown true weights, so the algorithm cannot use it as a stopping test.

What is coupled to what?

The potential $\mathcal{V}_{k}$ below adds $\omega$ times the coefficient error $\|\widehat{\vartheta}^{k}-\vartheta\|_{2}$ to the task gap. Calibration reduces its conditional expectation by a factor $\eta<1$ . Meanwhile, optimization reduces task error by a factor $1-\alpha$ , but adds a penalty proportional to the coefficient error remaining after calibration. The proof combines these two statements. The coefficient $\beta$ converts parameter error into objective-error units; the weight $\omega$ makes their weighted sum decrease. A potential is simply this weighted quantity used to track the analysis, not an additional objective that an agent minimizes.

Theorem A.5 (A joint task–correction contraction).

Assume known graph structure, Assumption 2 for every learned surrogate, Assumption 3, and Assumptions A.1–A.2. Set

(A.26)

 $\begin{gathered}\alpha\coloneqq\mu q(H)/\kappa,\quad\beta\coloneqq\kappa^{-1}\sum_{b}(1+q_{b}(H))\Lambda_{b}>0,\\
\omega\coloneqq 2\beta/(1-\eta),\quad\tau\coloneqq\min\{\alpha,(1-\eta)/2\},\\
\mathcal{V}_{k}\coloneqq\Delta(x^{k})+\omega\|\widehat{\vartheta}^{k}-\vartheta\|_{2}.\end{gathered}$ 

With deterministic initialization,

(A.27)

 $\mathbb{E}\mathcal{V}_{K}\leq(1-\tau)^{K}\mathcal{V}_{0}.$ 

Thus solution error and correction error vanish together; no externally supplied shrinking-error schedule is needed.

Proof idea.

Optimization contracts the task error but pays a penalty proportional to the remaining coefficient error. Calibration contracts that coefficient error. The weight $\omega$ is chosen so that the latter decrease pays for the former penalty.

Proof.

Condition first on the calibration outcome. The drift bound (A.5) applies with the current errors bounded by (A.22). Averaging over calibration yields

(A.28)

 $\begin{gathered}\mathbb{E}[\Delta(x^{k+1})\mid\mathcal{H}_{k}]\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:drift},\eqref{eq:learnederror},\eqref{dev:eq:jointconstants}}{\leq}(1-\alpha)\Delta(x^{k})+\beta\mathbb{E}[\|\widetilde{\vartheta}^{+}\|_{2}\mid\mathcal{H}_{k}].\end{gathered}$ 

Adding the weighted learning error gives

(A.29)

 $\begin{gathered}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{V}_{k+1}\mid\mathcal{H}_{k}]\\
\begin{array}[]{c@{\;}l}\overset{\eqref{eq:coupled},\eqref{eq:learncontract}}{\leq}&(1-\alpha)\Delta(x^{k})+(\beta+\omega)\eta\|\widetilde{\vartheta}\|_{2}\\
\overset{\eqref{dev:eq:jointconstants}}{\leq}&(1-\tau)\mathcal{V}_{k}.\end{array}\end{gathered}$ 

For the second step, $\tau\leq\alpha$ and
 $(\beta+\omega)\eta\leq\omega[1-(1-\eta)/2]$ ; the latter follows because the difference equals $\beta(1-\eta)\geq 0$ . Taking expectations and induction prove (A.27).
∎

In this theorem, Lemma 1 is applied epoch by epoch, after conditioning on the calibration result. Its error bound may therefore change from epoch to epoch; no fixed nonzero error floor is imposed. The rate $\tau$ is controlled by the slower of two processes: optimizing the solution with the current correction, and identifying the correction itself. The method retains an estimate of fixed coefficients, not a permanently valid table of candidate values: after $x$ changes, known features and coupling terms must still be evaluated at the new context. This is the precise sense in which the correction is reusable.

Can already-paid verification teach the correction?

Yes. Every proposal verified in line 6 of Algorithm 1, including a rejected one, supplies a measurement at the common base. With $z_{b}=(x_{-b},U_{b})$ , define

(A.30)

 $\begin{gathered}a_{b}\coloneqq\phi(z_{b})-\phi(x),\\
y_{b}\coloneqq F(z_{b})-F(x)-[G(z_{b})-G(x)]\overset{\eqref{dev:eq:features}}{=}\langle a_{b},\vartheta\rangle.\end{gathered}$ 

One can apply (A.17) to these measurements after all proposals and merge decisions have been fixed, retaining the resulting estimate for the next epoch. Each equation must use the base at which its two objective values were measured. Reuse needs no additional verifier call; feature evaluation, projection work, and communicating the updated estimate still cost work.

Proposition A.6 (Safe reuse of proposal measurements).

Under the hypotheses of Theorem A.5, keep its deliberate calibration and add any finite sequence of exact projection updates from the already-evaluated proposals, after the current search and merge decision. The bound (A.27) remains valid for the estimate after these extra updates. No excitation or independence assumption is needed for the extra measurements.

Proof idea.

Extra projections cannot increase parameter error. They do not change the proposals used to prove this epoch’s task-error bound.

Proof.

Let $\widetilde{\vartheta}^{+}$ be the error just after deliberate calibration, and let $\widetilde{\vartheta}^{\rm next}$ be its value after all proposal measurements have been processed. Every nonzero measurement gives an orthogonal projection; a zero measurement is skipped. Repeated use of the pathwise identity (A.24) gives

(A.31)

 $\|\widetilde{\vartheta}^{\rm next}\|_{2}\overset{\eqref{eq:pythagoras}}{\leq}\|\widetilde{\vartheta}^{+}\|_{2}.$ 

The task update still obeys (A.28). Consequently

 $\begin{gathered}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{V}_{k+1}\mid\mathcal{H}_{k}]\\
\begin{array}[]{c@{\;}l}\overset{\eqref{eq:coupled},\eqref{eq:reuse-norm},\eqref{eq:learncontract}}{\leq}&(1-\alpha)\Delta(x^{k})+(\beta+\omega)\eta\|\widetilde{\vartheta}\|_{2}\\
\overset{\eqref{dev:eq:jointconstants}}{\leq}&(1-\tau)\mathcal{V}_{k}.\end{array}\end{gathered}$ 

Induction proves the claim. The next epoch’s hypotheses must continue to hold for the augmented history, as required by their uniform conditional formulation.
∎

This extension justifies learning from verification, but does not justify removing deliberate calibration: proposals can all have zero measurement vectors or fail to explore an unknown direction.

The joint theorem shows that learning and optimization can converge together. Yet its estimation error involves the unknown true coefficients, so the algorithm cannot read that error from its current state. Nor does a rate in epochs alone reveal the price of the measurements. A more concrete specialization lets us address both issues.

## A.5. Certifying and paying for learning

For binary blocks with unknown individual-bit penalties, each targeted objective evaluation identifies one coefficient exactly. This gives both a computable bound on the remaining correction error and a finite total number of learning queries. We now derive those guarantees. Unlike the preceding parameter-error norm, the remaining uncertainty can be tracked without knowing the true weights. For this specialization, assume $\mathcal{X}_{b}=\{0,1\}^{d_{b}}$ with positive integer $d_{b}$ . Thus every one-bit calibration probe is feasible; a constrained subset of the binary cube need not have this property. Let $\Psi$ be a known function describing interactions between blocks. Assume all remaining unknown requirements are linear penalties on individual bits:

(A.32)

 $F(x)=\sum_{b}L_{b}(x_{b})+\Psi(x)+\sum_{b}\langle\vartheta_{b},x_{b}\rangle+c_{0}.$ 

The unknown $c_{0}$ cancels in differences. Assume known coordinate bounds $|\vartheta_{bj}-\widehat{\vartheta}^{0}_{bj}|\leq M_{bj}$ . A probe flips bit $j$ of block $b$ at the current base, producing $z$ and $s=z_{bj}-x_{bj}\in\{-1,1\}$ . Its residual is

(A.33)

 $\begin{gathered}y\coloneqq F(z)-F(x)-[L_{b}(z_{b})-L_{b}(x_{b})]\\
{}-[\Psi(z)-\Psi(x)]\overset{\eqref{dev:eq:coordinate-model}}{=}s\vartheta_{bj},\\
\widehat{\vartheta}_{bj}^{+}\coloneqq\widehat{\vartheta}_{bj}+(y-s\widehat{\vartheta}_{bj})/s.\end{gathered}$ 

This identifies one coefficient exactly from one extra scalar query when $F(x)$ is cached. Keep it forever. Conditionally on the current history, each block with unobserved coefficients probes with probability $p\in(0,1]$ , choosing uniformly among its unobserved coordinates. Independence between blocks is not required. Probes are not committed.

Let $\mathcal{U}_{b}^{k}$ be the remaining unobserved coordinates before epoch $k$ . Until identified, each coefficient estimate is kept at its initial value and retains its error bound $M_{bj}$ , while a measured coefficient has zero error. Summing these remaining bounds gives a computable uncertainty radius:

(A.34)

 $\Gamma_{b,k}\coloneqq\sum_{j\in\mathcal{U}_{b}^{k}}M_{bj},\qquad\Gamma_{k}\coloneqq\sum_{b}\Gamma_{b,k},\qquad d_{\max}\coloneqq\max_{b}d_{b}.$ 

This sampling rule stops probing already identified coefficients, so it need not satisfy uniform excitation over all original parameter directions. Its analysis instead uses the uncertainty remaining in $\Gamma_{k}$ . Known coupling terms are recomputed at the current context; only the static unknown coefficients are retained. The anchored correction error for block $b$ is at most $\Gamma_{b,k+1}$ , its radius after this epoch’s probes, because each binary coordinate displacement has absolute value at most one.

Proposition A.7 (Observable uncertainty and bounded calibration).

Write $\gamma\coloneqq p/d_{\max}$ . The above calibration satisfies

(A.35)

 $\mathbb{E}[\Gamma_{k+1}\mid\mathcal{H}_{k}]\leq(1-\gamma)\Gamma_{k}.$ 

There are at most $d_{\rm tot}\coloneqq\sum_{b}d_{b}$ additional calibration queries over the entire run. After $K$ epochs their expected number is at most $\min\{d_{\rm tot},mpK\}$ .

Proof idea.

Each successful probe removes one previously unmeasured coefficient from the uncertainty sum. Bounding the expected removed uncertainty gives contraction; counting distinct coefficients gives the finite query budget.

Proof.

If a block has $n_{b}>0$ unobserved coefficients and remaining radius $\Gamma_{b,k}$ , its expected removed radius is $p\Gamma_{b,k}/n_{b}\geq p\Gamma_{b,k}/d_{\max}$ . Empty sets contribute zero. Summing gives (A.35); solution updates do not change the learned static parameters or their uncertainty. Every calibration identifies a previously unobserved coefficient, so its count is at most $d_{\rm tot}$ . At most $mp$ queries occur in expectation per epoch, proving both bounds.
∎

Corollary A.8 (Joint contraction with observable correction uncertainty).

Assume the full binary domains, coordinate model (A.32), known initial coefficient bounds, and exact calibration protocol above. Assume a valid dependency-graph coloring, Assumptions 2 and 3 for the resulting surrogates and iterates, and deterministic initialization. Define

(A.36)

 $\begin{gathered}\beta_{0}\coloneqq\kappa^{-1}\max_{b}(1+q_{b}(H)),\quad\omega_{0}\coloneqq 2\beta_{0}/\gamma,\\
\tau_{0}\coloneqq\min\{\mu q(H)/\kappa,\gamma/2\},\quad\mathcal{W}_{k}\coloneqq\Delta(x^{k})+\omega_{0}\Gamma_{k}.\end{gathered}$ 

Then $\mathbb{E}\mathcal{W}_{K}\leq(1-\tau_{0})^{K}\mathcal{W}_{0}$ for every integer $K\geq 0$ . The radius $\Gamma_{k}$ is observable, although $\Delta(x^{k})$ need not be. In particular, $\Gamma_{k}=0$ certifies that the unknown coefficients have been learned, not that the current solution is globally optimal.

Proof idea.

Repeat the joint-potential argument with the computable uncertainty radius replacing the unknown coefficient-error norm. The same weighting principle turns two coupled inequalities into one contraction.

Proof.

Set $\alpha=\mu q(H)/\kappa$ . Here $\Gamma_{k+1}$ is the radius after this epoch’s deliberate probes; the ensuing solution update does not change it. Conditional on those probes, each block’s correction error is at most its remaining coordinate-radius sum. The gain and merge bounds therefore give

(A.37)

 $\begin{gathered}\mathbb{E}[\Delta(x^{k+1})\mid\mathcal{H}_{k}]\\
\overset{\eqref{dev:eq:localgain},\eqref{eq:merge},\eqref{eq:eb},\eqref{eq:observableconstants}}{\leq}(1-\alpha)\Delta(x^{k})+\beta_{0}\mathbb{E}[\Gamma_{k+1}\mid\mathcal{H}_{k}].\end{gathered}$ 

Adding the weighted radius yields

(A.38)

 $\begin{gathered}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{W}_{k+1}\mid\mathcal{H}_{k}]\\
\begin{array}[]{c@{\;}l}\overset{\eqref{eq:radius-gap},\eqref{eq:radiuscontract},\eqref{eq:observableconstants}}{\leq}&(1-\alpha)\Delta(x^{k})\\
&{}+(\beta_{0}+\omega_{0})(1-\gamma)\Gamma_{k}\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:observableconstants}}{\leq}&(1-\tau_{0})\mathcal{W}_{k}.\end{array}\end{gathered}$ 

For the last step, $\tau_{0}\leq\alpha,\gamma/2$ and the coefficient margin is

 $\begin{gathered}\omega_{0}(1-\gamma/2)-(\beta_{0}+\omega_{0})(1-\gamma)\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:observableconstants}}{=}\beta_{0}\gamma\geq 0.\end{gathered}$ 

Total expectation and induction establish the stated bound.
∎

Reusing verification while preserving the radius.

For a binary proposal, subtract the contributions of all already identified coefficients from its residual. If its displacement contains exactly one still-unknown coefficient, the remaining residual identifies that coefficient by division by its nonzero bit displacement. Mark it identified, just as after a deliberate bit probe. Doing this after proposal generation only removes extra nonnegative terms from the final radius. In the proof, retain the radius just after deliberate probing for the task-error bound, and bound the final radius by that intermediate radius in the potential. The same coefficient inequality proves Corollary A.8; Proposition A.7 also remains valid. Local contraction must hold for the augmented histories. A general multi-coordinate projection need not preserve the individual bounds $M_{bj}$ ; it belongs to Proposition A.6’s norm analysis instead.

## A.5.1. The cost of acquiring the correction

Let a scalar verifier call cost $v$ , a parallel private slot cost $c$ , and other per-epoch overhead cost $C$ . Here $C$ excludes verifier calls, which are charged separately through $v$ ; in the exact-correction model of Section A.3, they were included in its aggregate overhead. All terms below use the same modeled cost units. Evaluating each agent’s proposal separately costs at most $m$ calls; color additivity supplies the new base value without an extra union query. The bound below is for Algorithm 1; the exact best-combination merge adds up to $2^{m}-m-1$ calls per epoch, and with pairwise factors $|I^{k}|(|I^{k}|-1)/2$ calls find the best combination of improving blocks (Appendix E.9). For a prescribed $K$ ,

(A.39)

 $\mathbb{E}T_{K}\leq K(C+cH)+v[1+mK+\min\{d_{\rm tot},mpK\}].$ 

The four terms count initial evaluation, local computation and synchronization, proposal evaluations, and calibration queries. The initial base query is charged once. Feature evaluation, coupling-context refresh, and correction-message transfer belong in $C$ or $c$ ; they are not free. A sufficient horizon for $\mathbb{E}\Delta\leq\varepsilon$ is

 $K=\lceil\tau_{0}^{-1}\log(\mathcal{W}_{0}/\varepsilon)\rceil,\qquad 0<\varepsilon<\mathcal{W}_{0}.$ 

This is a fixed-horizon guarantee. Since $\tau_{0}\leq p/(2d_{\max})$ , larger $H$ cannot speed it up beyond the calibration rate; with $p=1$ all coefficients are identified within $d_{\max}$ epochs, after which Section A.3 applies.

## A.5.2. Why arbitrary objectives need structure

Without structure, evaluations at previously visited candidates need not reveal anything about an unvisited one.
On an unrestricted finite candidate set, suppose some candidate $u$ has never been queried. Two objectives can agree on every observed value and differ arbitrarily at $u$ . They generate the same transcript and correction estimate, but its error at $u$ cannot be bounded uniformly for both objectives. If values are known only to lie in an interval of width $M$ , the same argument precludes a guaranteed radius below $M/2$ there. Adaptive queries do not change this indistinguishability argument. To infer unmeasured candidate values, the method needs a low-dimensional identifiable model, another regularity assumption, or enough queries to cover the candidates. Sparse evaluations alone cannot provide a uniformly accurate correction for an arbitrary objective.

## Appendix B Detailed related work

This appendix complements the related work of the main paper. It discusses corrected local computation, distributed optimization, surrogate learning, the organization of LLM collaboration and LLM planning for several robots.

Relation to local training.

ProxSkip uses a persistent control variate to counter the drift caused by skipping an expensive proximal operation (Mishchenko et al., 2022). SCAFFOLD uses control variates to address client drift in federated learning (Karimireddy et al., 2020). We borrow the question these mechanisms answer: what makes private steps point toward the shared objective? Here the answer is a correction to candidate values, not a gradient vector or an averaging rule. Agents own disjoint blocks, external context is frozen within an epoch, and synchronization combines accepted block replacements instead of averaging full models. Our theorem is therefore a block-descent result; whether ProxSkip-type acceleration carries over remains open.

Table B.1 makes the distinction explicit. Appendix A.4 supplies a persistent correction learned from objective-evaluation residuals and a joint error analysis for a restricted identifiable model. Appendix A.5 makes its uncertainty observable and charges calibration explicitly.

Table B.1. Relation to ProxSkip (Mishchenko et al., 2022). Our correction can be learned from verifier feedback; its update and rate differ from ProxSkip’s.

ProxSkip

Present construction

Correction

Updated recursively through occasional proximal steps

Learned persistently from scalar verifier residuals in an identifiable model

Private work

Corrected gradient steps

Search within an assigned artifact block

Analysis

Joint iterate–correction error

Joint task–correction contraction; observable uncertainty for coordinate probes

Communication benefit

Accelerated communication under its optimization assumptions

Batching and a finite calibration budget; no ProxSkip acceleration claim

Classical optimization and distributed local search.

Parallel numerical iteration and its communication costs have a long history (Bertsekas and Tsitsiklis, 1989). Inexact coordinate descent explicitly permits approximate block solves (Tappenden et al., 2016); our local contraction is an assumption on such a solve, not a new solver. Theorem 2 is an inexact block-descent argument under a quantitative local-to-global condition. Its finite domains and candidate-value errors differ from the smooth convex setting of those coordinate-descent analyses, but the descent principle is shared.

MGM chooses improving agents through neighbor gain comparisons, excluding simultaneous conflicting moves; DSA randomizes local updates (Maheswaran et al., 2004; Zhang et al., 2005). Our coordinator evaluates all proposals at a common base and accepts either the improving proposals of the best color (Algorithm 1) or the best verified combination of proposals (Algorithm 2). The monotonicity principle is closely related, while the selection rule and oracle interface differ. Work on $k$ -optimal DCOP solutions enlarges the coordinated neighborhood and studies its solution guarantees (Bowring et al., 2008); this is directly relevant when our single-block error bound fails. Assigning every agent utility $-F$ also gives an exact potential game with potential $-F$ (Monderer and Shapley, 1996). Unilateral improvement then reaches a coordinatewise equilibrium; global optimality still needs the additional geometry in Proposition A.1.

Expected separable overapproximations in parallel coordinate descent handle interference through randomized update models and upper bounds (Richtárik and Takáč, 2016). Our exact-additivity coloring argument is simpler and more conservative. The best-combination merge replaces $1/\kappa$ by the fraction of the verified gain it actually keeps, which is at least one over the number of conflict-free groups for pairwise factors (Section 5).

Surrogate learning and measurements.

Additive Bayesian optimization uses structural decompositions to make expensive objective optimization tractable (Kandasamy et al., 2015). Linear-bandit best-arm identification studies which measurements reveal enough about a shared unknown parameter to select a good action (Soare et al., 2014). Our representation is a noiseless, known-feature surrogate with deterministic candidate-error bounds and block descent. Learning an objective from scalar evaluations is not new; we add a bound that converts the remaining correction error into lost progress and an accounting of measurement cost (Section 7).

Established ingredients and proposed combination.

Block optimization supplies the local-to-global framework (Tseng, 2001; Nesterov, 2012; Richtárik and Takáč, 2016). Distributed constraint optimization studies variables owned by agents, with costs that couple their choices. Its DPOP algorithm communicates tables summarizing the best achievable cost for specified neighboring choices, exposing the importance of message size (Petcu and Faltings, 2005). The contribution of this paper is the explicit combination of a candidate-value correction, its approximation error, a verified merge, and a communication-horizon calculation for an agent-artifact model.

## B.1. AI-agent collaboration

The following work studies how LLM agents divide tasks, communicate and use feedback. We compare these mechanisms with the objective correction and convergence analysis developed here.

Shared artifacts and dialogue.

MetaGPT and ChatDev organize specialized agents around software-development workflows and intermediate artifacts (Hong et al., 2024; Qian et al., 2024). AutoGen builds applications by connecting agents that exchange messages, while AgentVerse studies collaborative agent groups (Wu et al., 2024; Chen et al., 2024b). Multiagent debate improves answers through exchanged critiques (Du et al., 2024). These works motivate specialization and information exchange. Our model instead makes a particular optimization interface explicit: owned solution blocks, candidate scores, and an exact scalar task verifier.

Communication topology and cost.

In a communication graph, nodes represent agents or operations and edges specify who sends information to whom. GPTSwarm optimizes such language-agent graphs; DyLAN adapts the participating agent network; G-Designer selects communication patterns for a given task (Zhuge et al., 2024; Liu et al., 2024; Zhang et al., 2025c). AgentPrune removes redundant message-passing edges, AgentDropout eliminates agents dynamically, and DALA allocates opportunities to speak through auctions (Zhang et al., 2025b; Wang et al., 2025; Fan et al., 2026). Thus, optimizing communication expenditure is an established research direction. Here the question is how omitted global requirements distort private search, and which persistent correction permits useful work between synchronizations. Our dependency graph describes objective factors and compatible edits; it is not a learned message-routing graph.

Feedback and evolving collaboration.

EvoMAC uses verification-derived textual feedback to evolve agents and their connections (Hu et al., 2025). AFlow searches over workflows—programs that specify the sequence and connections of model calls—using feedback from executing them (Zhang et al., 2025a), and evolving orchestration adapts how agents are selected and coordinated (Dang et al., 2025). Test-time communication for discovery is another closely related setting (Park et al., 2026). These works already connect feedback, accumulated experience, and collaboration. Our narrower contribution is to estimate the missing part of a numerical objective, bound the estimation error, and prove joint convergence of the solution and the estimate when the unknown coefficients can be identified.

LLM planning for several robots.

In RoCo, one LLM agent per robot negotiates a joint plan through dialogue and re-plans from motion-planner feedback such as collisions (Mandi et al., 2024). SMART-LLM decomposes instructions and assigns the subtasks to robots (Kannan et al., 2024); centralized, decentralized and hybrid LLM planners have been compared as teams grow (Chen et al., 2024a); and LLMs still fail at multi-agent path finding on maps with obstacles (Chen et al., 2025). Our routing agents also receive collision feedback, but as a numerical correction to their own objective, computed for the robot pairs whose collisions the exact verifier has reported, from those robots’ current routes, and only verified combinations of proposals are accepted.

Calibration, reliability, and scaling.

CommCP calibrates LLM-generated messages through conformal prediction for robots cooperating to answer questions about their physical surroundings (Zhang et al., 2026). Its message-reliability objective differs from our correction requirement, which concerns only a true block minimizer and the returned proposal (Assumption 1). Empirical analyses of multi-agent failures and scaling further motivate checking coordination and verification rather than presuming that more agents or messages help (Cemri et al., 2025; Kim et al., 2026). Our assumptions state what such checks must deliver for parallel work to add up to global progress.

## Appendix C Routing: task, verifier and measurement

This appendix defines everything the routing experiments share: why we chose the task, the two maps and their two-floor combination, commands, loss, local objective and correction, how feedback reaches the agents, the protocols, the measurement of search time and the exact references that certify optima and block minima. Appendix D explains how we chose the prompt and the protocols, trying two versions of the prompt, and Appendix E reports the results of the main text.

## C.1. Which tasks need corrections

Three regimes.

A team of agents is useful when a task is too large or too hard for one agent, and the parts can be searched in parallel. Whether the team also needs coordination depends on how the parts interact. A simple task, such as changing a light bulb, gains nothing from a team: splitting it only adds overhead. A task whose parts are independent, such as one agent watering a garden while another goes shopping, can be split without coordination; each agent optimizes its own part, and the team finishes sooner. In the language of Section 3, such a task is separable: if $F(x)=\sum_{b}L_{b}(x_{b})$ up to a constant, then the exact correction $R_{b}$ is zero, the dependency graph has no edges, a single color contains every block, and all improving proposals can be merged in one epoch. Theorem 2 then holds with $\delta_{b}^{*}=\delta_{b}^{U}=0$ and $\kappa=1$ , but it says nothing that independent parallel search does not already achieve.

The case that motivates this paper lies between these extremes: tasks whose parts are coupled. Examples include the modules of a large program, the structure, wiring and plumbing of a building, the subsystems of an aircraft, and the routes of robots that share a floor. Each part is manageable alone, but changing it alters what is best for its neighbors, so independent local improvements need not combine into a better whole. Here corrections tell each agent how its candidates affect the others, and coloring decides which improvements can be applied together. The strength of the coupling, not the number of agents, determines how much these mechanisms matter.

Why a routing task.

We wanted an experiment with few agents in which this coupling is strong, controlled and measurable. Multi-robot routing meets these needs. Its rules are simple: five commands, walls and a fixed horizon, and a reader can check a plan by eye. It is nevertheless hard for current LLMs, which must track coordinates, obstacles and timing; LLMs still fail at multi-agent path finding on maps with obstacles (Chen et al., 2025), and in our runs with prompt v1 a single model writing all nine routes reached a safe plan in only one run of ten (Appendix D). Most importantly, coupling is built into the task: robots owned by different agents can collide, so an agent’s best plan for its own robots can harm another agent’s robots. The exact verifier measures this coupling directly; in the team runs of the main text, 30% of the proposals that delivered all three of an agent’s robots without internal collisions still collided with another agent’s robot on the first map, and 32% on the second (Appendix E.7). A certified optimum and exact block minima also let us measure every constant of the theory.

Why not software.

Code generation was the first application we considered, and the introduction uses it as an example. It is harder to use for a controlled study of coupling. Good software is designed to limit coupling between modules (Parnas, 1972), so small coding tasks rarely exhibit strong interactions between the parts that different agents would own; those interactions mainly appear in large systems. A coding benchmark would also need test suites whose failures can be attributed to particular interactions, and a numerical objective whose optimum is known. Building such a benchmark at a scale where coupling is strong and measurable is a natural next step.

## C.2. Maps, robots and the two-floor task

The task is discrete multi-agent pathfinding with vertex and edge-swap conflicts and a stay-at-goal convention (Stern et al., 2019). Figure C.1 and Table C.1 specify both instances. Coordinates are $(x,y)\in\{0,\ldots,15\}^{2}$ , with $x$ increasing rightward and $y$ downward. On the first map, three $2\times 2$ obstacles occupy $\{5,6\}\times\{4,5\}$ , $\{9,10\}\times\{7,8\}$ and $\{5,6\}\times\{10,11\}$ . On the second map, the twelve wall cells form a horizontal wall of four cells, a vertical wall of four cells and two pieces of two cells. Every other cell is traversable. The simulation horizon is 48 ticks.

A model writes one JSON object with a programs field containing a literal command array for every robot it controls. Allowed commands are up, down, left, right, and wait; each consumes one tick and changes position by $(0,-1)$ , $(0,1)$ , $(-1,0)$ , $(1,0)$ , or $(0,0)$ , respectively. The model constructs the arrays rather than selecting a precomputed route. Arrays have at most 48 commands. After an array ends, the robot waits in its last cell through tick 48. Upon its first arrival at its goal, the robot must remain there. Every evaluation restarts all robots at their original starts at tick zero; optimization iterations do not advance a physical journey.

Two sixteen-by-sixteen grids side by side. Left, map 1 with three two-by-two obstacles; right, map 2 with a horizontal wall of four cells, a vertical wall of four cells and two pieces of two cells. On each, nine robots have numbered circles at their starts and numbered squares at their goals, colored by the agent that owns them, and a thin arrow points from each start to its goal.

Figure C.1. The two maps.
