Table C.1. Assignments and shortest legal grid distances on both maps. On the first map the blocks are $\{r_{0},r_{1},r_{6}\}$ , $\{r_{2},r_{3},r_{7}\}$ and $\{r_{4},r_{5},r_{8}\}$ ; on the second, $\{r_{0},r_{1},r_{2}\}$ , $\{r_{3},r_{4},r_{5}\}$ and $\{r_{6},r_{7},r_{8}\}$ . The sums are the optima.

The second map has the same size, horizon, loss and number of wall cells as the first. Starts and goals were drawn at random, and the map was kept because its statistics match the first map’s: the sum of shortest distances is 147 against 144, and letting every robot take a shortest path produces five collisions against four. On it, the first agent’s three robots move from left to right, the second’s from top to bottom and the third’s from bottom to top. The two-floor task places both maps on the two floors of one building: eighteen robots, $F=F_{1}+F_{2}$ with optimum $291$ and all-waiting value 2955; robots on different floors never collide, and a plan meets the threshold when both floors are safe and within 9 of their optima.

## C.3. Global loss, local loss and correction

The loss, the local objective and the correction are (21)–(23) of Section 8.1; this subsection gives their exact conventions. Let $p_{i}(t)$ be robot $i$ ’s position at tick $t$ , and let $\tau_{i}\leq 48$ denote its first arrival at its goal $g_{i}$ if it arrives. For a robot that does not arrive, $d_{i}$ is the shortest four-neighbor grid distance from $p_{i}(48)$ to $g_{i}$ , respecting walls. Arrival time, including waits, costs one unit per tick. The horizon term applies to every undelivered robot even when its submitted array is short.

For each pair $i<j$ , $C_{ij}$ counts a vertex conflict whenever $p_{i}(t)=p_{j}(t)$ , and an edge-swap conflict whenever the robots exchange distinct neighboring cells during the same tick. Conflicts are checked over ticks zero through 48; repeated co-location contributes one event per pair and tick. Geometric path intersection at different ticks is safe. The collision weight is $100$ for both conflict types. A conflict adds a penalty but does not stop or redirect either submitted trajectory. Walls, in contrast, are hard constraints. They restrict only each robot’s own route, so they define the block domains $\mathcal{X}_{b}$ rather than coupling blocks; collisions between robots are penalized instead, because they are the coupling under study. Empty arrays initialize the search with all robots stationary: $F(x^{0})=9(100+48)+144=1476$ on the first map and $1479$ on the second. The sums of individual shortest-path distances are 144 and 147. Separately computed collision-free witnesses attain these sums, certifying the global optima $F^{*}=144$ and $F^{*}=147$ . Appendix C.7 describes the reference algorithm and its certificates.

The local loss (22) of a team block counts its three robots’ delivery costs and all conflicts among them, and the correction (23) covers the external pairs in $\mathcal{P}^{k}$ , those discovered before global round $k$ , with the other blocks frozen at $x^{k}$ . The checked private score change is $L_{b}(u)-L_{b}(x_{b}^{k})+\widehat{R}_{b}(u;x^{k})$ . A discovered pair remains known, but the peer’s time-indexed positions are refreshed from the current accepted plan at the next round. If every external pair is known, this score change equals the conditional global loss change; before then it can miss conflicts. The experiment learns which pairs to check; the penalty coefficients are known.

## C.4. How feedback works

The prompts state the rules and show the checked state visible to the agent; their two versions are described in Appendix D. The following mechanics are common to both.

Initially $\mathcal{P}^{0}=\varnothing$ . If global verification discovers an external collision, the following round supplies that pair’s event information and the peer’s refreshed trajectory. Unrelated robots remain hidden. A spatial line alone would not suffice: the correction uses simultaneous occupancy and movement at specific ticks. Agents receive their previous proposal even if it was not accepted, and the prompt distinguishes it from the current incumbent. Repairs must be new complete arrays from the original start; appending a wait after an earlier collision cannot repair that collision.

Format failure versus route failure.

A missing or malformed JSON answer, wrong set of IDs, non-array output, or unknown command token is not a checked search answer. It is retried with the identical prompt and settings and a deterministic new retry seed. Format failures are re-asked at most three times per logical attempt, and transport failures at most three times with prompt v1 and twenty times with prompt v2; exhaustion pauses execution for diagnosis. In contrast, a readable array that enters a wall, leaves the grid, exceeds 48 commands, or moves away after arrival is a checked answer. Any such error rejects the entire owned proposal, retains the incumbent and returns route feedback; its candidate global loss is not evaluated. Otherwise, unfinished deliveries and collisions yield a finite score under (21). These answers count toward search attempts and time even when rejected as worse.

## C.5. Protocols

All protocols start from the same empty plan and accept only improvements of the global loss.

Team.

A global round is one batch of three concurrent block searches (six on two floors) followed by verification and a commit: one block per round on one map with Algorithm 1, one per floor on two floors, and the accepted combination with the other merge rules. All searches use the same frozen $x^{k}$ and $\mathcal{P}^{k}$ .

(1) 

Each agent proposes arrays for its three robots, receives private feedback, and may make up to $H=3$ checked answers. Python retains its best strictly improving proposal for $L_{b}+\widehat{R}_{b}$ , including the unchanged block as an option.

(2) 

Check the three proposals globally: substitute one proposed block into $x^{k}$ while keeping both other blocks unchanged. Compare these three full-plan losses with $F(x^{k})$ .

(3) 

Accept only the proposal with the largest positive global decrease. Ties favor the lower block index; if none improves, retain $x^{k}$ . All block pairs may interact, so a conservative complete dependency graph has three colors, each containing one block.

(4) 

Retain all external collision pairs found during global checks, including those in unselected proposals. Peer trajectories for the next round come from the new accepted plan, rather than a discarded proposal.

This is parallel proposal generation with serial block commitment. Within each private search, the correction and peer routes stay frozen. A deterministic routine computes a certified minimum or a valid lower bound for the available three-robot objective. Search stops early only when a submitted score equals that bound; otherwise it uses its remaining $H$ attempts. Appendix C.7 describes this routine and the independent offline certificates. The team’s stopping rule differs between the two prompt versions (Appendices D and E); restarting a run never resets a counter.

Single agent and best-of- $W$ .

A single agent controls all robots, sees and scores the full plan and receives full-plan feedback; every checked answer is evaluated immediately, and any strictly lower-loss legal plan is accepted at once. There is no grouping into rounds and no hidden-pair correction. The answer scope (all routes, up to $K$ routes or exactly one route per answer) and the stopping rules are given with each variant. The best-of- $W$ runs $W$ copies of the one-route single agent in parallel on the same accepted plan and prompt, with their own seeds; the best strictly improving answer of a round is accepted, its round time is the slowest of the $W$ answers, and $W$ equals the number of team agents.

## C.6. Model, seeds and measurement

The model is openai/gpt-oss-120b, an open-weight mixture-of-experts reasoning model described in the OpenAI model card (OpenAI, 2025). All routing runs, with both prompt versions, use the same OpenRouter/Groq endpoint, pinned without fallback, with reasoning effort medium, temperature $0.6$ and top- $p=0.95$ . No max_tokens, max_completion_tokens, or reasoning-token cap is sent. The network timeout is 3600 seconds; there is no absolute client deadline or automatic reasoning escalation. The API does not expose a controlled accelerator allocation.

Inference service and hardware.

OpenRouter supplies the API and routes requests to the selected inference provider (OpenRouter, 2026); Groq performs inference. The saved payloads restrict routing to groq with allow_fallbacks=false, and returned provider metadata identifies Groq. Groq documents GPT-OSS-120B serving through its LPU (Language Processing Unit) inference platform (Groq, 2025; Groq, 2026). This identifies the accelerator family; the chip generation, accelerator count, location, batching and service load are not exposed by the API.

The client measures each request with a monotonic timer around the HTTP call, response download and response processing. Consequently, the durations below include network, routing and service latency, as well as model generation; these components are not separated. Search time aggregates only checkable-answer durations according to (C.1); elapsed time and failed-request costs are recorded separately. All routing variants use the same provider restriction.

For replicate $s$ , the base API seed is $4{,}000{,}000+100{,}000s$ . Single-agent checked step $j$ , starting at zero, adds $j$ with prompt v1 and $1000j$ with prompt v2 (copy $c$ of a best-of- $W$ adds $1000j+c$ ); team round $k$ , private step $h$ and block $b$ , also zero-indexed, add $1000k+100h+b$ . Retry seeds are deterministic hashes of the logical seed and retry index. Seed settings are recorded, but provider reproducibility is best effort and the protocols induce different request streams. Map, settings, thresholds and limits were fixed before the runs of each campaign, and failed seeds are not rerolled.

Let $a_{j}$ be the latency of a returned answer whose command object can be checked. The single-agent search time is $\sum_{j}a_{j}$ . If agent $b$ has checked latencies $a_{kbh}$ in round $k$ , the team search time is

(C.1)

 $t_{\rm team}=\sum_{k}\max_{b\in\{0,1,2\}}\sum_{h}a_{kbh}.$ 

We call this the search time, as in the main text. The three agents’ times are not added as elapsed parallel time. Geometric errors and collisions count; missing or malformed answers and transport failures do not count toward search time. Actual elapsed time, preparation, stopping checks, verification, all physical requests, usage and reported charges are recorded separately. The simulator is inexpensive and has no artificial evaluation delay; this study does not measure a saving from expensive global evaluations.

## C.7. Reference routes and certificates

Reference computations certify the global optimum on the loss curves and the block minima used in the local-quality and block-geometry diagnostics. They use the model evaluator’s commands, horizon, goal convention and weights.

Global optimum.

Breadth-first search on the obstacle grid computes each robot’s shortest distance $d_{i}(s_{i},g_{i})$ , as listed in Table C.1. On the first map, their sum is a lower bound on every full-plan loss:

(C.2)

 $F(x)\geq\sum_{i=0}^{8}d_{i}(s_{i},g_{i})=144.$ 

A delivered robot cannot arrive before its grid distance. An undelivered robot costs at least 148, exceeding every individual distance (at most 20 on the first map and 23 on the second), and conflict penalties are nonnegative. Conflict-based search (CBS) (Sharon et al., 2012) finds a nine-robot plan with all deliveries, no conflicts and total arrival cost 144. Independent replay confirms that it attains the bound, proving $F^{*}=144$ even when unfinished or colliding plans are considered. The saved search expands seven high-level CBS nodes. The same argument certifies $F^{*}=147$ on the second map, where CBS again finds a delivered, collision-free plan whose arrival cost equals the sum of shortest distances, and $F^{*}=144+147=291$ on two floors, whose floors do not interact.

Search algorithms.

For one robot, breadth-first search uses states $(t,p)$ : tick $t\in\{0,\ldots,48\}$ and free cell $p$ . A move or wait advances time by one; each transition checks obstacles, bounds and imposed vertex or directed-edge constraints. Goal arrival is accepted only if remaining there through tick 48 respects the constraints. Each state has one predecessor record, merging alternative command prefixes. With 244 free cells on either map, there are at most $49\times 244=11{,}956$ states per single-robot search.

CBS starts with independently shortest constrained routes and orders its high-level nodes by total arrival cost. The earliest vertex conflict creates two branches, forbidding that cell at that tick for one robot or the other. An edge swap similarly branches on the directed transitions. Only the affected route is recomputed. The cheapest conflict-free node gives the minimum cost among delivered, conflict-free plans satisfying the constraints. CBS avoids enumerating all joint command arrays, but its tree can grow exponentially; computation-budget exhaustion certifies neither optimality nor impossibility.

Fixed-base block references.

For a fixed base $x^{k}$ , let $A_{b}^{\mathcal{P}^{k}}(u;x^{k})$ be the absolute private score: $L_{b}(u)$ plus the external conflict penalties for retained pairs $\mathcal{P}^{k}$ . Then

(C.3)

 $\widehat{f}_{b}(u;x^{k})=A_{b}^{\mathcal{P}^{k}}(u;x^{k})-A_{b}^{\mathcal{P}^{k}}(x_{b}^{k};x^{k}).$ 

The other six robots’ programs remain fixed. Only retained external pairs enter the reference for the agent’s available objective; using all hidden peers here would change the denominator of $Q$ . Separately, let $A_{b}^{\rm all}$ include every external pair. Costs involving only the other blocks cancel, so the true conditional global improvement is

(C.4)

 $D_{b}(x^{k})=A_{b}^{\rm all}(x_{b}^{k};x^{k})-\min_{u}A_{b}^{\rm all}(u;x^{k}).$ 

For either reference, the relevant frozen peer positions become vertex constraints, and peer moves become reverse-edge constraints, for a collision-free witness search. CBS resolves conflicts among the three owned robots. All minima range over legal owned arrays, including waits and unfinished deliveries, rather than only a route catalogue.

Certifying the soft-penalty optimum.

Finding the best collision-free delivered plan alone need not minimize the soft loss. Let $\bar{d}_{b}=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{b}}d_{i}(s_{i},g_{i})$ and let $A_{b}^{\rm CBS}$ be that plan’s absolute score. Any plan with a penalized conflict costs at least $\bar{d}_{b}+100$ , and any unfinished plan costs at least

 $\bar{d}_{b}+\min_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{b}}\{148-d_{i}(s_{i},g_{i})\}.$ 

The online routine certifies its CBS value only when $A_{b}^{\rm CBS}$ does not exceed either bound. Otherwise it uses a valid single-robot relaxation bound for the early-stop test, without claiming that the CBS value is the soft minimum.

The offline audit additionally uses independently implemented dynamic programming over $(t,p)$ . For each owned robot it minimizes delivery cost plus the relevant frozen-peer conflict penalties, including conflicts after arrival, and retains the non-delivery terminal cost of (21). Here conflicts remain penalized rather than forbidden. Dropping only conflicts among owned robots relaxes the joint block objective. If $\underline{A}_{i}$ is this single-robot minimum and $w_{b}$ is the joint witness, then, for either choice of external pairs,

(C.5)

 $\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{b}}\underline{A}_{i}\;\leq\;\min_{u}A_{b}(u;x^{k})\;\leq\;A_{b}(w_{b};x^{k}).$ 

A separate replay checks the witness’s legality and score. On the first map both inequalities are equalities in every audited subproblem, for the v1 and v2 team runs. On the second map, where conflicts among owned robots bind, the single-robot bound lies 1 or 2 below the witness in 83 of the 1,074 subproblems of the v2 team runs (40 of 786 and 81 of 1,008 in the runs with best combination and unsafe merging); their minima are certified instead by the CBS optimum together with the penalty and non-delivery bounds above. Every block minimum used in the audit of Appendix E.7 is therefore certified. Diagnostic witnesses and lower bounds are saved separately from model answers. These certificates support the assumption checks of Appendix E.7; they do not supply routes to agents or alter any recorded decision.

## Appendix D Routing: prompt and protocol design

We wrote two versions of the prompt. With prompt v1 we tried several protocols for the single agent and the team on the first map, ten runs each. Where these runs stalled, the model had to simulate the other robots’ commands itself; prompt v2 presents the full checked state instead and made both protocols faster. For the main text we chose the combination that worked best: prompt v2, a single agent that replaces one route per answer, the best-of- $W$ such agents as the parallel control, and the team that rewrites its three routes. Task, loss, correction, protocols and measurement are those of Appendix C. Figure D.1 shows the mean curves of all variants and Table D.7 their outcomes.

Three panels of mean gap to the optimum against search time on logarithmic axes, all on the first map. (a) Single-agent protocols with prompt v1: the two whole-plan protocols stall near a gap of 700; answers with up to three or two routes stall near 37 and 44; one route per answer continues lower and ends near 23. (b) Team protocols with prompt v1: the team ends near 12, the team with the efficiency paragraph near 23, the team with partial updates near 38, the team without correction stalls near 250, and the sequential replay of the team reaches the team's values more slowly. (c) Prompt v1 against prompt v2: with v2 the one-route single agent ends near 12 instead of 23 and the team near 6 instead of 12; the single agent also ends sooner; the single agent with exactly three routes per answer and prompt v2 ends near 77.

Figure D.1. How the protocols and the prompt were chosen (first map; mean gap $F-F^{*}$ against search time). (a) Single-agent protocols with prompt v1, ten runs each. (b) Team protocols with prompt v1, ten runs each; the sequential replay plays the team’s answers one agent at a time. (c) Prompt v1 against prompt v2: twenty runs for the v2 one-route single agent and team, ten for the other curves. Stars mark the end of recorded support.Three panels of mean gap to the optimum against search time on logarithmic axes, all on the first map. (a) Single-agent protocols with prompt v1: the two whole-plan protocols stall near a gap of 700; answers with up to three or two routes stall near 37 and 44; one route per answer continues lower and ends near 23. (b) Team protocols with prompt v1: the team ends near 12, the team with the efficiency paragraph near 23, the team with partial updates near 38, the team without correction stalls near 250, and the sequential replay of the team reaches the team's values more slowly. (c) Prompt v1 against prompt v2: with v2 the one-route single agent ends near 12 instead of 23 and the team near 6 instead of 12; the single agent also ends sooner; the single agent with exactly three routes per answer and prompt v2 ends near 77.

## D.1. Prompt v1

An initial prompt gives the command rules, coordinate orientation, horizon, loss weights, an ASCII obstacle map, owned robot IDs and start/goal coordinates, and their current command arrays. Team agents initially do not receive other blocks’ assignments or routes. The single agent receives all nine assignments and checks every pair internally. Each request states that the output must contain complete literal arrays for exactly the owned IDs.

After a checked answer, Python reports its score and per-robot arrival or remaining distance. For an illegal route it identifies the first rejected command, its tick, the previous and attempted cells, and legal directions from that cell. Feedback includes the legal position prefix and an ASCII route drawing for a failed or unfinished route. For visible conflicts it gives the robot pair, tick, vertex cell or swapped edge, and time-labeled map frames. A safe arrived route does not need a repeated full drawing. A supplied external peer is accompanied by its complete time-indexed positions, with only a constant trailing wait suffix compressed; the last listed position persists thereafter. At most three illustrative collision frames are shown, while complete event lists and peer motion remain available.

Format failures were re-asked at most three times and transport failures at most three times. The team stopped upon reaching the threshold ( $F\leq 153$ with a safe plan), after five rounds without an improving commit, or at a ceiling of eight global rounds.

## D.2. Protocols tried with prompt v1

Team and whole-plan single agent.

The team is the protocol of Appendix C. The first single agent rewrote all nine routes in every answer. It controls all nine arrays and receives full-plan feedback. Each checked answer is evaluated immediately; any strictly lower-loss legal plan becomes the incumbent immediately. There is no grouping into three-answer batches, no external hidden-pair correction, and no global-round counter. The chain stops upon reaching the stopping threshold, after five consecutive checked answers without an accepted improvement, or after sixty checked answers. A route-invalid answer contributes to that five-answer streak. Actual chains used 5–13 checked answers; sixty was a safety ceiling, not the number performed. Both variants also stop early once all nine robots are delivered without conflicts and $F\leq 153$ ; this rule only ends a run.

The twenty runs of these two protocols complete under their stopping rules. The team safely delivers every robot in $10/10$ runs, with final losses 149–167; the whole-plan single agent does so in $1/10$ . Table D.7 reports final loss, search time and reported cost for these and all other variants. Table D.1 reports elapsed time and answer counts; Table D.2 retains every seed.

Table D.2. Every completed seed, without selection. Time is the search time defined above. All ten team outcomes deliver 9/9 without conflicts. Single-agent delivered/conflict counts refer to its final accepted plan, including an unchanged initial plan.

Figure D.1(a, b) plots the mean gap $F-F^{*}$ over all ten runs of each variant; an accepted plan is held until the next accepted change, and a stopped run keeps its final loss. The team and its sequential replay end at mean $F=156.50$ , versus $841.00$ for the whole-plan single agent.

The journals contain 676 returned, checkable model responses and 142 HTTP 429 failures, for 818 physical requests. All returned responses include API-returned reasoning. Checked responses include 120 route-invalid proposals and 437 legal candidate proposals with collisions. Across both variants, the provider reports USD 3.553312 in charges; charges for the 142 HTTP failures are unknown, so reported costs are not a certified total bill. Diagnosed rate-limit interruptions resume after cooldowns by reusing saved answers and identical prompts; they do not restart a seed or change the model, map, loss or reasoning. Elapsed time includes recovery pauses. Independent replay checks every returned command, frozen base, local score, correction, block selection and final plan.

A flat curve can conceal attempted repairs.

Single-agent seed 0 stops at the initial loss 1476 after five checked answers. It does not simply return stationary plans: the first answer has three illegal routes, the third has one, the fourth is legal but worse with $F=2092$ , and the fifth has one illegal route. Entire-plan rejection retains the incumbent, while the next prompt still contains attempted arrays and offending commands. With the efficiency paragraph and patience fifteen (below), seed 0 again ends at $F=1476$ .

The nine failed runs each end with five nonimproving answers, which take $162.98\pm 24.31$ seconds per run (mean $\pm$ sample SD): 34 of these 45 answers are route-invalid. All error durations already count toward search time. The last response, at $513.28$ seconds, belongs to the successful seed; no later attempts were measured. Counts and clocks are verified by independent replay.

Team without correction.

This ablation disables only the cross-block correction channel of the team; Appendix E repeats it with prompt v2. Agents receive no acquired external pairs, no peer timelines and no global collision feedback in their local searches; feedback about their own robots, including internal collisions, is unchanged. The coordinator still evaluates the full objective, logs collisions and commits the single best improving block. Model, provider, sampling parameters, prompt, $H=3$ , the eight-round ceiling, the five-round plateau rule, the safe target and the ten prescribed seed streams are those of the v1 team. The first request payloads are byte-identical, so the two variants diverge only when the corrected controller would first have revealed a conflict. Local early stopping is unchanged: a block that is optimal for its local objective may stop requesting answers while the joint plan still contains external collisions.

Table D.3. All ten no-correction team outcomes. Every final plan delivers all nine robots; collisions are cross-block events in the final plan.

All ten runs complete, and every final plan delivers all nine robots (Table D.3). Nine retain one to four cross-block collisions, 2.4 on average over all ten runs; only seed 2 reaches a safe plan, with $F=152$ . Final loss is $392.90\pm 120.66$ , search time $337.97\pm 95.34$ seconds, with $39.8\pm 10.4$ checked answers over $7.6\pm 1.3$ rounds (mean $\pm$ sample SD). Subtracting the collision penalty of 100 per event leaves a mean of 152.9, close to the corrected v1 team’s $156.50$ . Delivery quality within blocks is therefore comparable, and the remaining loss consists of exactly the cross-block interactions that the uncorrected local objective omits. Because only improving blocks are committed, the uncorrected team never increases $F$ , but its agents cannot tell which of their candidates would remove an external collision, so search stalls once every block is locally good. Reported charges total USD 1.32 over 476 requests, 78 of which return no charge. Independent replay checks every route, acceptance decision and outcome.

Whole-plan single agent with an efficiency paragraph.

This variant adds to the prompt of the whole-plan single agent the paragraph

Work through the routes efficiently, avoiding repeated calculations or restating the same plan. Once you have a concrete candidate, return its JSON so the checker can provide feedback for the next improvement.

and raises its patience to fifteen answers without improvement, with a ceiling of sixty answers. All ten runs end normally and are independently replayed. One run reaches a safe plan, with $F=154$ ; five deliver all nine robots but keep one to five collisions, and four never accept a route. The mean final loss is $860.00\pm 555.40$ and the search time $728.52\pm 366.76$ seconds, with $26.4\pm 15.5$ answers per run (Table D.7). Table D.4. Single agents with the efficiency paragraph and patience fifteen, by the number of routes an answer may replace: independently replayed final plans of all forty runs. Coll.: collisions in the final plan; Ans.: checked answers. ∗: no route was ever accepted, so the plan is the initial one and delivers no robot; every other final plan delivers all nine. Runs with a safe plan and $F\leq 153$ stopped early, seed 1 of the whole-plan agent at its ceiling of sixty answers, and all others after fifteen answers without improvement. The whole-plan agent’s elapsed time, which adds retries, exceeds its search time by less than 5 s in every run except seed 2 (75 s).

Reported costs total USD 2.22; one request returned no charge.

With patience five and no efficiency paragraph, the whole-plan agent ends at $841.00\pm 483.14$ after $281.36\pm 111.67$ seconds, also with one safe run. The longer patience therefore buys more search time but not a better final plan: writing all nine routes in one answer remains the bottleneck. Letting the agent change only some routes per answer, with the same prompt and patience, raises safe completion to seven or eight runs of ten with up to two or three routes, and to nine with one route per answer (below).

Single agents that replace some routes.

These agents still observe and score the complete nine-robot problem but replace at most $K\in\{1,2,3\}$ routes per answer and keep the others unchanged. At every answer the agent sees all nine starts, goals and accepted arrays, plus the full global route and collision feedback. It chooses up to $K$ distinct robot IDs, independent of the team’s fixed groups. Arrays for omitted IDs remain exactly unchanged. The selected replacement arrays are merged literally into the accepted plan and checked together. A geometry error in any selected array rejects the entire proposal. Otherwise, the merged plan is accepted immediately if and only if its full global $F$ strictly decreases. Collisions can occur in accepted intermediate plans if their total loss improves; safe completion always requires zero collisions.

A run stops after fifteen consecutive checkable answers without an accepted improvement, or once its plan is safe with $F\leq 153$ . The safety ceiling is sixty checked answers for $K=3$ and 120 for $K\in\{1,2\}$ , because one route per answer needs more answers; the $K=3$ runs used at most 47, so the ceiling never stopped them. There are no client output or reasoning-token caps. Geometry-invalid but schema-valid answers consume checked attempts and search time; malformed answers and transport failures are retried with the same prompt and do not.

The shared coordinate, map, loss, collision and global feedback instructions are those of the team prompt, and the efficiency paragraph quoted above is included.
The scope instruction reads:

You are one agent controlling all nine robots in robots. Propose a partial update to their delivery programs. … All nine robots, their starts, goals and current programs are visible to you.

Only the following sentences depend on $K$ . For $K=3$ they are

Choose from one to three distinct robot IDs in each response. Return complete replacement arrays ONLY for those chosen IDs. … Final answer: exactly one JSON object with key programs mapping one to three selected robot IDs to their new complete literal arrays of command names.

For $K=2$ they read “Choose one or two distinct robot IDs in each response” and “mapping one or two selected robot IDs”. For $K=1$ they read “Choose exactly one robot ID in each response. Return a complete replacement array ONLY for that chosen ID” and “mapping exactly one selected robot ID”. The structured task records max_updated_robots $=K$ and the matching output_scope. Before the $K\in\{1,2\}$ runs, an offline check confirmed that the code produces, for $K=3$ , exactly the first prompt recorded in the $K=3$ runs.

Table D.7 summarizes the five single-agent protocols and Table D.4 lists every partial-update run.

• 

One route per answer: nine of ten runs are safe and six stop early; final loss $166.80\pm 37.60$ ; search time $761.71\pm 456.76$ seconds with $40.3\pm 20.6$ answers.

• 

Up to two routes: seven runs are safe and six stop early; final loss $188.20\pm 54.87$ ; search time $489.47\pm 228.26$ seconds with $24.6\pm 10.1$ answers.

• 

Up to three routes: eight runs are safe and five stop early; final loss $180.60\pm 45.75$ ; search time $559.08\pm 325.35$ seconds with $24.0\pm 11.4$ answers.

All partial-update protocols reduce the gap at a similar rate during the first 300 seconds; afterwards the one-route agent continues to improve, while runs with larger answers more often stall with a collision. The whole-plan agents stall far higher.

Requiring a complete answer for all nine routes proved difficult: the whole-plan single agent is safe in one run of ten, with or without the efficiency paragraph. Smaller answers make the single agent more reliable: with one route per answer it is safe in nine runs of ten and reaches the lowest mean loss of all single-agent protocols. The price is time: it needs about 40 answers per run, and its median time to a safe plan with $F\leq 153$ is 696 seconds, against 341 and 331 seconds with up to two and three routes. We therefore use the one-route protocol as the single-agent baseline, with prompt v2 in the main text. With prompt v1, it reduces the gap faster at first than the v1 team, but the team’s mean loss is lower after about 560 seconds; the v1 team is safe in all ten runs, its search takes $589\pm 65$ seconds against $762\pm 457$ , and its final loss varies far less ( $156.50\pm 5.60$ ).

Team with the efficiency paragraph.

This variant adds the same paragraph to the team prompt and changes nothing else; without the paragraph, its first three requests are those of the v1 team. All ten runs end normally and are independently replayed. Every final plan delivers all nine robots, and nine are safe; seed 8 keeps one collision. The mean final loss is $167.40\pm 34.15$ and the search time $436.65\pm 90.79$ seconds, with $51.8\pm 10.8$ answers per run (Table D.7). Table D.5 lists every run, together with the team with partial updates (below).

With partial updates, seed 0 is the adopted pilot; the runs with a safe plan and $F\leq 153$ stopped early, the others at the eight-round cap, and elapsed time exceeds search time by less than 3 s in every run except seed 0 (31 s).

The paragraph is the only difference from the v1 team. It shortens the mean search time from 589 to 437 seconds and lowers the reported cost per run from USD 0.28 to 0.23, while nine of ten runs remain safe; the mean final loss rises from $156.5$ to $167.4$ , mostly because of the one unsafe run. The prompt therefore changes the team’s results only slightly.

Team with partial route updates.

Here each agent may replace the routes of one, two or three of its own robots instead of returning all three; arrays for omitted robots are copied exactly from the frozen plan, separate subset proposals are not combined, and correction, verification and the single best commit are unchanged. The original instruction was:

Return complete arrays for EVERY owned ID, even if unchanged.

Its replacement is:

Choose from one to three distinct IDs from owned in each response. Return complete replacement arrays ONLY for those chosen IDs. All omitted owned robots keep their installed current arrays exactly unchanged. You cannot change robots outside owned. Each private attempt starts from the same current arrays shown below.

The opening instruction becomes:

Propose a partial update to delivery programs for your owned robots in robots.

The final-answer instruction becomes:

Final answer: exactly one JSON object with key programs mapping one to three selected owned IDs to a literal array of command names. No shorthand, loops, code or alternative answers.

The structured task adds two fields:

• 

max_updated_robots is 3;

• 

output_scope has the exact value “One to three selected owned IDs only; omitted arrays remain exactly current.”

All other instructions, including ownership, objective and correction, are unchanged.

All ten runs end normally, six by early stop and four at the eight-round cap. All deliver nine robots, but replicates 2, 7 and 9 each retain one collision, giving seven safe runs. Replicates 2 and 7 retain an edge swap between $r_{4}$ and $r_{8}$ , which belong to the same agent, at $t=6$ ; replicate 9 retains a collision between $r_{6}$ and $r_{7}$ , which belong to different agents, at $t=13$ . The mean final loss is $182.40\pm 48.08$ and the search time $310.40\pm 94.67$ seconds (Tables D.7 and D.5).

Partial updates halve the team’s mean search time (310 versus 589 seconds). In exchange, three runs end with a collision, and the mean final loss rises from $156.5$ to $182.4$ . Partial updates thus trade reliability for speed for the team. Because a team’s value lies in solving the task reliably, we keep the team that rewrites all its routes as the main protocol.

Panels show mean realized search quality Q, mean error term B, mean descent coefficient alpha, and the smallest geometry ratio over the states that start a round. Dots are individual runs; white diamonds with bars show mean and sample standard deviation. Search quality is higher with prompt v2; the error term and descent coefficient change little.

## D.3. Why prompt v1 stalled

With prompt v1, six of the thirty partial-update single-agent runs ended with exactly one collision and fifteen answers without improvement. An offline check with the exact single-robot best response (Appendix C.7) shows that in each of these six final plans one of the two colliding robots could be rerouted, with the other eight routes fixed, removing the collision without delaying that robot’s arrival; in three of them the best such route even arrives earlier than the accepted one. Five of the six collisions are head-on: two robots move toward each other along the same row or column, so waiting only moves the point where they meet. The ninety answers given in these stalled phases show two failure modes. First, 56 of the 113 edited routes keep exactly the same cells and only insert or remove waits. Second, the 20 answers that did remove the original collision all created a new one with another robot. Prompt v1 listed every robot’s commands but gave tick-by-tick positions only for the robots involved in a reported collision, and drew collision frames only for the previous attempt; to check a detour against the other robots, the model had to simulate their commands itself. The map had no column labels, and the rule that a swap between ticks $t-1$ and $t$ is reported at tick $t$ was not stated.

In prompt v2 we therefore changed the presentation, not the information structure, of both prompts. Asked again in the six stalled states, the single-agent prompt v2 produced an accepted improvement in 21 of 60 answers (ten per state, with and without the list of rejected attempts), including at least one in every state, against none of the ninety recorded answers with prompt v1. The team prompt had the same presentation, and only 22 of the 207 private searches that could improve their block reached the exact corrected block minimum.

## D.4. Prompt v2

Both prompts state the command, timing, collision and loss rules once, without strategy advice: no route, distance to the optimum or repair rule is supplied. They then present the checked state:

• 

the map with column and row labels, and the list of wall cells;

• 

every controlled robot’s start, goal, current program, arrival tick and cost;

• 

a timeline with the position of every visible robot at every tick, marking robots that have arrived;

• 

every visible collision of the accepted plan, described in words (for example, “tick 7: r2 moves [7,6] $\to$ [8,6] while r7 moves [8,6] $\to$ [7,6]”) and drawn as maps of all visible robots at the tick before and the tick of the collision;

• 

the attempts already checked against the accepted plan, with their losses and collisions.

The single agent sees all nine robots, the global loss and the stopping threshold, and lists the attempts rejected since the plan was last improved. A team agent sees only its three robots and its known peers, exactly as with prompt v1: robots of other agents whose collisions with its robots the coordinator has already found, with their current fixed timelines (Section 3.1). Other robots appear in no table, drawing or collision list. A team agent sees neither the global loss nor the threshold; it lists its earlier attempts in the current round and, if its previous proposal collided in the coordinator’s global check, those collisions. Without correction, a team agent sees only its own robots. Answers have the same format as with prompt v1: complete literal command arrays for the chosen robots.

## D.5. Effect of the prompt version

Figure D.1(c) compares the two prompt versions. For the single agent with one route per answer, prompt v2 raises safe completion from nine of ten runs to all twenty, lowers the final loss from $166.80\pm 37.60$ to $156.00\pm 10.53$ , and shortens the search from $761.7$ to $613.0$ seconds on average with fewer answers (27.8 against 40.3). For the team, it shortens the search from $589.4$ to $481.0$ seconds and lowers the final loss from $156.50\pm 5.60$ to $149.90\pm 2.67$ ; both are safe in every run, and the v2 team reaches the threshold in all twenty runs against three of ten. The team’s stopping rule changed as well: the v1 team stopped after at most eight rounds, which ended seven of its ten runs before the plan reached $F\leq 153$ , four of them right after an improving round, whereas the v2 team may run up to thirty rounds; this comparison therefore also reflects that change. Geometrically invalid answers did not become rarer: they are 17% of the checked answers for both v2 protocols, against 12% for the single agent and 11% for the team with v1. The gains come from repairs: the v2 team’s searches reach the exact corrected block minimum in 82 of the 343 searches that can improve their block (22 of 207 with v1) and capture on average a fraction 0.66 of the available corrected improvement, pooled over searches (0.58 with v1).

Table D.6. Mean $\pm$ sample SD over runs (ten for v1 settings, twenty for v2). Smallest ratio: the minimum of $\sum_{b}D_{b}/\Delta$ over the states of a run that start a round; its mean is not a certificate over the union of runs.

Applying the audit of Appendix E.7 to every team run with a full set of block references shows how the prompt moves the measured constants (Figure D.2, Table D.6): the v1 team, the v1 team with the efficiency paragraph (72 rounds, 216 returned searches, 518 checked answers) and the v2 team on both maps. Averaged per run, the realized search quality rises from $0.59\pm 0.08$ with v1 to $0.63\pm 0.11$ with the paragraph and to $0.70\pm 0.15$ and $0.69\pm 0.14$ with v2 on the two maps, while the error term stays at the same level ( $47.4\pm 12.7$ , $49.2\pm 18.3$ , $49.7\pm 16.5$ and $40.4\pm 19.5$ ) and the descent coefficient changes little ( $0.25$ , $0.26$ , $0.27$ and $0.26$ ). The error at every true block minimizer is zero for every prompt, so $B$ comes entirely from returned proposals. The smallest geometry ratio at the states that start a round is at least 0.6, 0.69, 0.43 and 0.70 in every run, with means between 0.81 and 0.93. With the efficiency paragraph, the per-round recurrence holds in all 72 rounds and ends at a mean gap bound of $267.46$ against an observed mean gap of $23.40$ . A better prompt thus raises search quality and changes which states are visited, while the correction errors stay at the same level.

## D.6. The protocols of the main text

Three observations explain the choice of protocols in the main text. First, a single agent that rewrites all nine routes in every answer rarely reaches a safe plan, with or without the efficiency paragraph and longer patience. Letting it update only some robots per answer, while it still sees and scores the whole plan, raises safe completion to seven or eight runs of ten with up to two or three robots, and to nine with exactly one; with prompt v2, one route per answer is safe in all twenty runs and exactly three routes per answer in nine of ten. The one-route agent is therefore the single-agent baseline. Second, with prompt v1 a partial-update option makes the team faster but less reliable, with three runs ending in a collision, two of them between robots of the same agent; the team that rewrites all its routes is kept for the main text. The efficiency paragraph shortens the v1 team’s search (437 versus 589 seconds) but leaves one run unsafe and raises its mean loss to 167.4. Third, prompt v2 shortens the search of both the one-route single agent and the team, and the team without correction is rarely safe with either prompt. With prompt v2, three one-route single agents searching in parallel are faster than the team with Algorithm 1 on both maps, while the team ends with the best plans. On two floors, six such agents are faster than the team in mean but not in median, the single agent needs about twice the team’s search time, and the team again ends with the best plans. The main text therefore uses prompt v2 with the one-route single agent, its best-of- $W$ as the parallelism-matched control, the team that rewrites all three of an agent’s routes, with Algorithm 1 and with the best-combination and unsafe merges, and the team without correction; the sequential replay is reported in Appendix E.10.

## Appendix E Routing results with prompt v2

This appendix reports every run behind Figure 3 and Table 1, all with prompt v2 (Appendix D): on the first map, twenty runs each for the single agent with one route per answer, the best-of-three such agents and the team, and ten each for the single agent with exactly three routes per answer and the team without correction; on the second map, twenty each for the single agent, the best-of-three and the team, and ten for the team without correction; on two floors, twenty each for the single agent and the best-of-six, with the team variants evaluated on the runs of both maps; and for two other merge rules of the team, best combination and unsafe, twenty runs each on both maps. It then shows recorded collision repairs, audits the assumptions of Theorem 2 on the team runs, and evaluates the trajectory-wise bound.

## E.1. Protocols and measurement

The team protocol is that of Appendix C: three agents with fixed blocks, up to $H=3$ answers per agent and round on a frozen base, one accepted block per round, and the certificate-based early stop of a private search. The team stops once the plan is safe and within 9 of the optimum ( $F\leq 153$ on the first map), after five rounds without an improving commit (fifteen answers per agent, as many as the single agent), or after thirty rounds. The single agent stops once the plan is safe and within the threshold, after fifteen consecutive answers without improvement, or after 120 answers; it either replaces one route per answer or returns complete programs for exactly three robots of its choice per answer, the size of a team agent’s answer (a program may be repeated unchanged). An answer with another number of robots is a format failure. The best-of-three runs three copies of the one-route single agent in parallel: every round, all copies receive the same prompt with their own seeds (copy $c$ in round $r$ adds $1000r+c$ ), the best strictly improving answer is accepted and the other two are discarded; if no answer improves, all three enter the list of rejected attempts. Its round time is the slowest of the three answers, as for the team, and it stops once the plan is safe and within the threshold, after five rounds without improvement (fifteen answers) or after forty rounds.

Model, provider and sampling settings are those of Appendix C.6. Replicate $s$ uses base seed $4{,}000{,}000+100{,}000s$ ; the team adds $1000k+100h+b$ , and the single agent adds $1000j$ for its $j$ -th answer. Format failures are re-asked with a deterministic new seed, at most three times, and transport failures at most twenty times; neither counts toward search time. Fourteen times in about 5,400 requests on the two maps, the provider stopped an answer at its 65,536-token output limit after the visible answer had degenerated into repetition; each such answer was kept, counted as a format failure, and re-asked once with the next retry seed. Search time is defined by (C.1); the sequential replay sums the three agents’ times in every round instead of taking the maximum. Every run was replayed independently from the recorded answers: proposals, block selection, accepted plans, losses, known pairs and clocks.

## E.2. Results on the first map

Table E.1 lists every run; Table D.7 gives means and costs. Seeds 10–19 of the single agent with one route and of the team were run after seeds 0–9 with byte-identical code and settings; the best-of-three ran all twenty seeds with the same code.

• 

Single agent, one route per answer: safe in all twenty runs, sixteen of which stop at $F\leq 153$ ; final loss $156.00\pm 10.53$ (median 153); search time $613.0\pm 397.8$ seconds (median 448) with $27.8\pm 11.7$ answers. Fifteen runs finish within 900 seconds; five need 955 to 1,532 seconds, three of which stop above the threshold, at 157, 160 and 191; the fourth run above the threshold stops at 180 after 855 seconds.

• 

Best-of-three one-route single agents: safe in nineteen runs, sixteen of which stop at $F\leq 153$ ; final loss $162.90\pm 48.82$ (median 152); search time $326.4\pm 198.1$ seconds (median 256) over $13.1\pm 3.8$ rounds with $39.3\pm 11.3$ answers. Seed 10 stops at $F=370$ with two collisions; seeds 0, 11 and 12 stop at 154 to 158 after 587 to 934 seconds.

• 

Single agent, exactly three routes per answer (ten runs): safe in nine runs, all of which stop at $F\leq 153$ ; final loss $220.80\pm 220.38$ (median 152); search time $561.2\pm 253.5$ seconds with $20.4\pm 9.9$ answers. In seed 2 the agent stopped at $F=848$ , with four robots undelivered and one collision, after fifteen answers without improvement, eight of them geometrically invalid. Overall 37% of its answers are invalid, against 17% for the one-route agent, because one invalid route rejects the whole three-route answer.

• 

Team: safe in all twenty runs, all of which stop at $F\leq 153$ ; final loss $149.90\pm 2.67$ (median 150.5); search time $481.0\pm 227.3$ seconds (median 440; two runs take longer than 700 seconds, at most 987) over $7.3\pm 2.7$ rounds with $47.1\pm 16.1$ answers. Seed 19 reaches the optimum $F=144$ ; the two longest runs need 12 and 13 rounds.

• 

Team without correction (ten runs): no run is safe; final loss $457.10\pm 109.17$ , search time $326.2\pm 172.0$ seconds over $10.8$ rounds on average. Every run delivers all nine robots and keeps one to five collisions, all between robots of different agents. Six runs end at a fixed point: every agent’s own robots take shortest routes without colliding with each other, so all three blocks are certified at the start of a round, no model is called, and every later round would repeat the plan. The other four stop after five rounds without improvement, still answering.

• 

Sequential replay of the team: $853.9\pm 374.2$ seconds against $481.0\pm 227.3$ in parallel, a ratio of means of 1.78; answers, selections and losses are unchanged.

Table E.1. Every run on the first map with prompt v2, independently replayed; — marks a seed that was not run for that variant. † marks a final plan that is not safe. Ans.: checked answers; team times use the per-round maximum (C.1).

Statistical comparison.

Two-sided permutation tests over the twenty runs of each protocol compare final losses and search times. The team’s final loss is lower than the single agent’s (difference of means 6.1, $p<0.001$ ; of medians 2.5, $p=0.005$ ); on every seed the single agent ends at least as high as the team, and higher on eighteen. Against the best-of-three, the difference of means is 13.0 ( $p=0.011$ ) and of medians 1.5 ( $p=0.09$ ). The team reaches the threshold in all twenty runs, the two single-agent protocols in sixteen each ( $p=0.11$ , Fisher’s exact test). In search time, the single agent and the team have the same median (difference 7 seconds, $p=0.97$ ), the single agent’s mean is higher by 132 seconds ( $p=0.21$ ) and its spread is larger (standard deviation 1.75 times the team’s, $p=0.044$ ). The best-of-three is faster than the team (difference of medians 184 seconds, $p=0.011$ ; of means 155 seconds, $p=0.027$ ), with a similar spread ( $p=0.62$ ); it is faster on fifteen of the twenty seeds.

Its threshold is $F\leq 156$ , nine above the optimum $F^{*}=147$ .

Table E.3. All runs on the second map; mean $\pm$ sample SD over all runs, including unsafe ones.

Tables E.3 and E.2 and Figure 3(b) summarize the runs. The team is safe in all twenty runs and reaches the threshold in nineteen; the remaining run stops at $F=206$ , and seed 10 reaches the optimum. Its final loss has median 151, lower than the single agent’s 155 and the best-of-three’s 156 (permutation tests on the medians, $p=0.002$ and $p=0.001$ ); on eighteen of twenty seeds it ends at least as low as each of them. The means differ less clearly ( $p=0.26$ and $p=0.08$ ) because one single-agent run stops at $F=353$ with two collisions and two best-of-three runs stop with one collision each. Only the team’s mean gap falls below 10, at about 520 seconds. In search time, the best-of-three is fastest (median 272 against 416 seconds for the team, $p=0.02$ ); the single agent and the team do not differ significantly in median or mean, but the single agent’s spread is larger (standard deviation 1.8 times the team’s, $p=0.03$ ), with six runs longer than 700 seconds against three. Played in turn, the team’s answers would take $778\pm 305$ seconds instead of $482\pm 202$ . Without correction, eight of ten runs end with collisions between agents. Seven of them end at the fixed point described for the first map, with no model calls in their last five rounds; the eighth also repeats itself, since in each of its last five rounds one agent returns routes that are optimal for its own robots but collide with other agents’ robots, and they are rejected. This is why the uncorrected curve stops after about 310 seconds: more rounds would add no answers, or only rejected ones.

## E.4. Two floors

The two-floor task (Appendix C) places the first map (robots r0–r8) and the second map (robots renamed s0–s8) on the two floors of one building; both instances are copied unchanged from the single-floor campaigns apart from the renaming. Robots on different floors never collide, so $F=F_{1}+F_{2}$ with optimum $F^{*}=291$ and all-waiting value 2955. A plan meets the threshold when both floors are safe with $F_{1}\leq 153$ and $F_{2}\leq 156$ .

Team variants.

The team has six agents with the blocks of the single-floor runs. Its dependency graph consists of two disjoint triangles; each color class holds one block of each floor, and the coordinator commits, in each round, the best improving block of each floor, if any. This is Algorithm 1 with the pairing of the two floors’ blocks into colors chosen anew in each round: any such pairing is a proper three-coloring, and the committed gain is at least that of the best color of any fixed coloring, so the analysis applies with $\kappa=3$ . Each agent’s prompt shows only its own floor, exactly as in the single-floor runs. A two-floor team run is therefore a run on each floor played in lockstep: round $k$ lasts $\max(T_{1,k},T_{2,k})$ , where $T_{f,k}$ is the slowest agent’s search on floor $f$ in that round, and a floor that has met its threshold or gone five rounds without improvement makes no further calls; the run ends when both floors have stopped. The team, its sequential replay, which sums the times of all six agents, and the team without correction are evaluated on the recorded single-floor runs, pairing seed $s$ of the first map with seed $s$ of the second, without new model calls.

Single agent and best-of-six.

The single-agent prompt states the rules once, adding that each floor is its own grid and that robots on different floors never collide, and shows each floor exactly as the single-floor prompt does: the map with its labels and wall list, every robot’s program and cost, the timeline and every current collision with its maps. The accepted changes and the rejected attempts are listed once for the whole plan, with the floor of each changed robot; the loss is shown per floor and in total, and the goal names both thresholds. Each answer replaces the route of one robot on either floor, and a change is accepted only if $F$ strictly decreases. The single agent stops once both floors meet their thresholds, after fifteen answers without improvement or after 240 answers; the best-of-six runs six copies of it as the best-of-three does and stops after five rounds without improvement (thirty answers) or after eighty rounds. Neither cap was reached. Seeds, retries and the clock are those of the single-floor runs; the best-of-six ran seeds 0–9 first and seeds 10–19 afterwards with byte-identical code. The provider stopped two of the 4,207 requests at its output limit, and each was re-asked once as before.

Results.

Table E.4 summarizes the variants and Table E.5 lists every run.

• 

Single agent: safe in all twenty runs; nine meet both thresholds, thirteen the first floor’s and eleven the second’s. Final loss $319.60\pm 23.31$ (median 313.5, at most 409); search time $1148.7\pm 419.6$ seconds (median 1200; eleven runs take longer than 1000 seconds) with $63.2\pm 16.2$ answers. Eleven runs stop after fifteen answers without improvement.

• 

Best-of-six: safe in all twenty runs, sixteen of which meet both thresholds; final loss $312.40\pm 16.26$ (median 306.5, at most 363); search time $516.8\pm 123.9$ seconds (median 511, at most 739) over $23.8$ rounds with $142.5\pm 17.1$ answers.

• 

Team: safe in all twenty runs, nineteen of which meet both thresholds; final loss $304.05\pm 12.73$ (median 302); search time $656.4\pm 206.1$ seconds (median 595, at most 1013) over $9.3$ rounds with $91.9\pm 20.7$ answers summed over both floors.

• 

Team without correction (ten runs): no run is safe; final loss $785.50\pm 181.90$ , search time $382.1\pm 176.1$ seconds.

• 

Sequential replay of the team: $1632.1\pm 468.5$ seconds against $656.4\pm 206.1$ in parallel, a ratio of means of 2.49 (1.78 on the first map, with three agents).

Table E.5. Every run on two floors. $F_{1}{+}F_{2}$ : final loss of each floor; ∗ marks a run that misses a threshold. Every final plan of the single agent, the best-of-six and the team is safe; no final plan without correction is. Team runs pair seed $s$ of both maps; Seq.: sequential replay.

Statistical comparison.

We apply the tests used on the first map. Against the single agent, the team’s final loss is lower (difference of means 15.6, $p=0.002$ ; of medians 11.5, $p<0.001$ ; the single agent ends higher on seventeen seeds and equal on two), the team meets both thresholds more often (19 against 9, $p=0.001$ , Fisher’s exact test), and it is faster (difference of medians 606 seconds and of means 492 seconds, both $p<0.001$ ; faster on seventeen seeds), with half the spread (standard deviation ratio 2.04, $p=0.002$ ). Against the best-of-six, the team’s median final loss is lower by 4.5 ( $p=0.004$ ), the means differ by 8.4 ( $p=0.09$ ), and the best-of-six ends higher on sixteen seeds; the threshold counts do not differ significantly (19 against 16, $p=0.34$ ). The best-of-six is faster in mean (by 140 seconds, $p=0.013$ ) but not in median (by 84 seconds, $p=0.19$ ), with a smaller spread (ratio 0.60, $p=0.02$ ), and it is faster on fifteen seeds.

Bound.

The bound defined in Appendix E.8 below applies with six blocks and $\kappa=3$ : $\alpha_{s,k}$ adds $Q_{s,k,b}D_{b}$ over all six blocks and divides by $3\Delta_{s,k}$ , and $B_{s,k}$ adds the error terms of both floors; a floor that has stopped contributes neither. The one-step inequality (E.5) holds in all 186 rounds. Propagated per seed from $E_{s,0}=2664$ , held after each run stops and averaged, the envelope ends at mean gap 395.9 after thirteen rounds, against 13.1 observed; it stays within a factor of 2.2 of the observed mean gap through round 3 (1.11, 1.46 and 2.15). The pooled means of the coefficients are $\bar{\alpha}=0.222$ and $\bar{B}=66.7$ .

## E.5. Best-combination and unsafe merges

Algorithm 1 accepts the best color; on one map the dependency graph is complete, so one block per round. Two variants change only this step. With best combination, the coordinator checks every combination of the blocks whose proposal differs from the base, at most seven on one map, and accepts the one with the lowest verified loss, or nothing if none improves (the exact branch of Algorithm 2). Because the improving blocks of each color are among these combinations, the round gain is at least that of the best color, and Theorem 2 applies unchanged (Section 3.3). The unsafe team accepts every changed block together, whatever the loss, and the run returns the best plan it has seen. Everything else is that of the team with Algorithm 1: code, prompt v2, model, settings, seed streams, the five-round stopping rule (which counts rounds that do not improve the best plan) and the measurement. The prompt states the merge rule in one sentence, so that sentence differs: with Algorithm 1 the coordinator “installs the best block proposal of the round”, with best combination it “checks every combination of the block proposals of the round on the full plan and installs the combination that lowers the loss most”, and in the unsafe team it “installs the block proposals of all agents of the round together”. Collision pairs found in any checked combination become known pairs. Before the runs, an offline test with scripted answers confirmed that the code with the merge rule of Algorithm 1 reproduces the decisions and prompts of the main runs exactly; the independent replay checks every combination, decision and clock. On two floors both variants are composed from the two maps as for Algorithm 1: the best combination of six blocks on independent floors is the best combination of each floor. The provider stopped 19 of the 3,664 requests at its output limit, eight of them in one run; each was re-asked once.

Results.

Table E.6 summarizes the runs and Table E.7 lists them. With best combination, a round that changes the plan accepts on average 1.67 blocks on map 1 and 1.72 on map 2, and in every run the first round accepts two or three. The team is faster than with Algorithm 1 on every task: on map 1 the search takes $339.7\pm 196.9$ instead of $481.0\pm 227.3$ seconds (difference of means $p=0.04$ , of medians $p=0.03$ ), on map 2 $310.4\pm 204.1$ instead of $482.3\pm 201.6$ ( $p=0.01$ and $p=0.04$ ), and on two floors $451.6\pm 227.1$ instead of $656.4\pm 206.1$ ( $p=0.005$ and $p=0.08$ ), with fewer rounds and answers and USD 0.18 and 0.17 instead of 0.23 and 0.22 per run on the two maps. In median, it also reaches a safe plan first. A collision or an undelivered robot adds at least 100 to the loss, so a gap below 100 implies a safe plan. The median search time until the gap first falls below 100 is 185.1, 154.7 and 223.2 seconds on the three tasks with best combination, against 313.7, 288.1 and 444.7 with Algorithm 1, 236.1, 207.2 and 362.9 for the best-of- $W$ , 329.1, 274.7 and 675.4 for the single agent and 377.6, 256.6 and 425.7 for the unsafe team; a run that never gets there counts as infinitely slow. In permutation tests on the medians, only the best-of-three on map 1 is not significantly slower ( $p=0.27$ ; $p\leq 0.007$ for every other comparison). The final losses do not differ significantly from those of Algorithm 1 on any task ( $p\geq 0.24$ ), with medians 150, 153 and 302.5 against 150.5, 151 and 302. On map 1 nineteen of twenty runs meet the threshold; on map 2 fourteen do, against nineteen with Algorithm 1 ( $p=0.09$ , Fisher’s exact test), and six runs stop above it, at $F$ between 157 and 203, against one.

Why six runs stall on map 2.

In each of the six, the last five rounds accept nothing although a block can still improve: at the final state the exact corrected block minimum lies 43 to 52 below the current value in three of the runs and 2 to 14 below in the other three, so no run stops at a single-block trap. In five runs the other two blocks are certified optimal for their agents’ corrected objectives, so those agents make no calls; in seed 1 two agents keep searching. In four runs (seeds 1, 8, 14 and 18) a searching agent did return a proposal that improved its corrected objective, but the proposal collided with robots of pairs that the correction did not yet know, so verification rejected it and the collisions became known pairs; in seed 14 the agent’s next proposal collided with yet another unknown pair, and in seeds 1, 8 and 18 the agent found no further improvement before the stop. In seeds 13 and 19 the agent found no improvement of its corrected objective in fifteen answers. The merge rule would accept any single improving block, so the stalls come from local search with an incomplete correction, not from the merge. These states differ from those of Algorithm 1 because the first rounds differ: after round 1 the plan has 3.3 collisions on average, against 0.1, since two or three agents’ routes are accepted together, and it is safe after 2.8 rounds instead of 5.2. Once no collision is left, the realized search quality falls for every merge rule; on map 2 it is 0.28 with best combination (57 of 85 such searches capture nothing) against 0.34 with Algorithm 1 (47 of 87). On map 1 the difference does not occur (one run above the threshold against none).

Unsafe merging.

The unsafe team accepts 2.2 blocks per changing round on map 1 and 2.1 on map 2, but its accepted plan becomes worse in 32 of its 185 rounds on map 1 and 37 of 148 on map 2, when blocks changed together create new collisions; after round 1 its plan has 6.3 collisions on average on map 1 and 3.0 on map 2. Its search time does not differ significantly from that of Algorithm 1 on any task ( $601.4$ against $481.0$ seconds on map 1, $423.7$ against $482.3$ on map 2, $716.8$ against $656.4$ on two floors; $p>0.1$ ), it needs more answers, and its final losses are similar ( $p\geq 0.18$ ); one final plan on map 2 keeps a collision. Accepting worse plans did not significantly lower its final loss, and one final state on each map is a single-block trap.

Audit and bound.

The audit of Appendix E.7, defined below, applies to both variants (Table E.9). With best combination, the realized search quality averages 0.65 and 0.60 on the two maps, 26% and 28% of the locally safe proposals collide with another agent’s robot, and the geometry ratio is at least 0.55 and 0.75 at every state that starts a round; four and two final states are single-block traps, all of them final states at which a run had met its threshold and stopped. The one-step inequality (E.5) holds in all 113 and 111 rounds and in all 142 on two floors, and the averaged envelope ends at mean gap 328.7, 399.4 and 604.7 against 5.4, 12.8 and 18.2 observed (gray curves in Figure E.4); coefficients from seeds 0–9 again bound the mean gap of seeds 10–19 in every round, and vice versa. The envelope ends higher than with Algorithm 1 because these runs stop after fewer rounds; Appendix E.9 sharpens it by counting only the conflicts that occur. For the unsafe team the recurrence does not apply, since the loss can increase; its search quality averages 0.63 and 0.68 and its share of colliding locally safe proposals 29% and 34%.

## E.6. Recorded collision repairs

The loss curves hide what an accepted change looks like. The plan of Figure 2 arose from a repair shown in detail in Figure E.1. In team seed 19 on the first map, the plan accepted after round 3 has $F=244$ and one collision: $r_{1}$ and $r_{8}$ , which belong to different agents, exchange cells at tick 11. The pair was known from an earlier global check, so in round 4 the agent owning $r_{0},r_{1},r_{6}$ saw $r_{8}$ ’s current timeline. Its first answer moved $r_{1}$ to another shortest route with the same arrival tick 20; it also appended waits after arrival to $r_{0}$ and $r_{6}$ , which leave their cells unchanged. Its local loss did not change, $\Delta L_{b}=0$ , while the correction fell by 100. The verifier accepted the block, and the plan reached $F=144$ , the optimum, with all nine robots delivered and no collision. On the first map, 15 of the 121 accepted team rounds remove the last collision between agents in this way, through a pair the agent knew from the correction.

Figure E.2 shows two further repairs at the collision tick. In team seed 11, round 7, the agent of $r_{0},r_{1},r_{6}$ knew the swap of $r_{0}$ with $r_{7}$ at tick 8; its third answer changed only $r_{0}$ ’s route, kept its arrival tick 20 (local change 0, correction change $-100$ ) and removed the plan’s last collision ( $F$ from 259 to 159; the run ends at 151). The single agent repairs the same kind of conflict without a correction, because it sees every robot: in seed 4, its 22nd answer replaced $r_{4}$ ’s route, kept its arrival tick 15 and removed the swap of $r_{4}$ with $r_{8}$ at tick 6 ( $F$ from 250 to 150, the run’s final plan). In all three cases the paths still cross in space but no longer at the same tick, so spatial intersections alone do not determine safety.

## E.7. Audit of the convergence assumptions

The team runs allow a direct comparison with Assumptions 1–3. Using the exact references of Appendix C.7, an offline audit computes, at every recorded base of every team run, the exact corrected and true block minima. On the first map it covers 145 global rounds, 435 returned block proposals and 942 checked answers (160 of them geometrically invalid) at 165 state occurrences, one of which is optimal; on the second map, 159 rounds, 477 proposals and 895 checked answers (274 invalid) at 179 state occurrences. It includes unselected proposals, searches that return the incumbent and route-invalid answers. Table E.9 compares the measured constants across prompts and tasks.

Coupling.

Of the 382 returned proposals that deliver all three of an agent’s robots without internal collisions, 115 (30%) still collide with another agent’s robot on the first map, and 147 of 461 (32%) on the second; with prompt v1 the share was 83 of 211 (39%). Local delivery and internal safety therefore do not suffice for global compatibility. We count simultaneous vertex or edge-swap events, not intersections of spatial route drawings.

Local search quality: Assumption 2.

For each returned block $U_{kb}$ with a positive initial surrogate gap, compute

(E.1)

 $Q_{kb}=\frac{-\widehat{f}_{b}(U_{kb};x^{k})}{-\min_{u}\widehat{f}_{b}(u;x^{k})}\in[0,1].$ 

The unchanged block is a retained option, so this is the fraction of available corrected local improvement captured by the entire $H=3$ search. It is not the quality of only the winning block or the fraction of the global gap closed. Early termination at a certified optimum can be viewed as leaving that optimum unchanged for the remaining private steps. A route-invalid answer consumes a private step and leaves the best score unchanged; all legal answers are replayed, including worse ones.

There are 343 searches with a positive corrected gap on the first map and 338 on the second; the others start at a surrogate optimum and are reported separately rather than assigned $Q=1$ . The pooled mean is 0.66 on the first map and 0.65 on the second, with 77 and 87 zero-gain searches and 82 and 111 that reach the exact corrected optimum (Table E.8); weighting by the initial local gap gives captured fractions of 0.82 and 0.85. The mean falls from 0.93 and 0.99 in round 1 to below 0.5 from round 5 on in the rounds with at least ten searches (Figure E.3(a)). This compares different histories, and later rounds exclude runs that have stopped. Assumption 2 requires a lower bound on the conditional expected fraction at every history, with $q_{b}(H)=1-(1-\rho_{b})^{H}$ , so Appendix E.8 uses each round’s realized $Q$ in the pathwise recurrence rather than a pooled constant.

Figure E.3. Offline assumption diagnostics for the team with prompt v2. (a) Mean realized $Q$ over searches with a positive corrected gap, in the rounds with at least ten such searches. (b) Exact ratio $\sum_{b}D_{b}(x)/\Delta(x)$ at every recorded state, one line per run; dashed lines mark the smallest value over the states that start a round, $\mu=0.43$ on map 1 and $0.70$ on map 2; dots mark final states, crosses the single-block traps (ratio 0).Left: mean realized search quality by global round for both maps; it falls from about 0.93 and 0.99 in round one to between 0.3 and 0.5 after round five. Right: the ratio of the summed block improvements to the global gap at every recorded state, one thin line per run and colored by map, with dashed lines at 0.43 for map 1 and 0.70 for map 2; dots mark final states and crosses the six single-block traps at ratio zero.

Correction accuracy: Assumption 1.

In the theory, the retained correction (23) is $\widehat{R}_{b}$ ; the true correction also includes the undiscovered external pairs. Let $C_{b,\rm miss}^{k}(u)$ count conflicts with those pairs against the frozen peers. The exact score error is

(E.2)

 $\begin{split}\varepsilon_{kb}(u)&=\widehat{f}_{b}(u;x^{k})-f_{b}(u;x^{k})\\
&=-100\big[C_{b,\rm miss}^{k}(u)-C_{b,\rm miss}^{k}(x_{b}^{k})\big].\end{split}$ 

Both corrections are anchored at zero. At every recorded base, the missing-pair conflict count is zero; any existing conflict has already been discovered. Hence a nonzero error at these bases is optimistic: a missing conflict makes the private score understate the true global change.

Among the 782 legal answers on the first map, the mean absolute error is 94.5 and the maximum 3,500; among the 435 returned proposals, 106 have a nonzero error, with mean absolute error 45.5 and maximum 500. On the second map the 621 legal answers have mean absolute error 53.3 (maximum 600), and 114 of the 477 returned proposals have a nonzero error, with mean 36.5 (maximum 600). In 28 and 40 searches the private score improves without a positive verified global gain, which distinguishes local search progress from progress after the correction and the global check.

These observed errors differ from a uniform bound $\delta_{b}$ , which takes a supremum over all legal block programs, including poor collision-seeking routes; such a bound implies Assumption 1 but is not needed for it. For each recorded state and block we compute that supremum exactly. A time-expanded min/max dynamic program counts undiscovered external conflicts for each owned robot; because those counts add across robots, their extrema add over the product domain. Obstacles and absorbing goals remain enforced. Internal conflicts are penalized in $F$ , rather than prohibited in the candidate domain. Independent replay verifies the extremizing joint programs.

Over the recorded states and blocks, these exact suprema average 12,933 on the first map (from 11,400 to 14,000) and 12,518 on the second (from 10,900 to 13,600). A deterministic bound covering any history is $\delta_{b}=86{,}400$ : a block has at most $3\cdot 6=18$ external pairs, each contributing at most 48 events because starts are distinct, and an anchored count difference is at most $18\cdot 48$ ; multiplying by the collision weight gives the bound. Finite uniform boundedness and anchoring are therefore certified, but these worst-case bounds far exceed the initial global gap of 1332 and give no useful floor in Theorem 2. Assumption 1 avoids this obstacle: it needs the error only at a true block minimizer, where it is zero at every audited state, and at the returned proposal, where it averages 45.5 and 36.5.

Block geometry: Assumption 3.

Using the exact true minima in (C.4), compute over the recorded states $x$ that start a round

(E.3)

 $\mu_{\rm obs}=\min_{x}\min\left\{1,\frac{\sum_{b}D_{b}(x)}{F(x)-F^{*}}\right\}.$ 

It equals 0.43 on the first map, where the ratio is below 0.6 at one of the 145 states that start a round, and 0.70 on the second (Figure E.3(b)). Among final states, two on the first map, at $F=147$ and $F=145$ , and four on the second, at $F=148$ or $149$ , are single-block traps: every $D_{b}=0$ although the gap is positive, so only a joint change of two blocks can improve them. All six are final states at which the run had met its threshold and stopped; no round starts from a trap, and the recurrence is applied only at states that start a round, where $\mu$ holds. The certificate covers the recorded states, not the full product domain; with prompt v1, $\mu=0.6$ held at all 88 recorded states, including the final ones.

Scope of the connection to the theory.

The task has a finite product domain of legal command arrays, exact global verification, unary and pairwise factors, and a conservative three-color dependency graph. The frozen parallel searches and best singleton commit implement the theory’s merge with $\kappa=3$ . The audit certifies the block error bound on the states that start a round and quantifies actual corrected local progress and score error. Assumptions 1 and 2 are statements about conditional expectations; the audit measures their realized counterparts on the recorded states. The two-point errors of Assumption 1 are small, whereas a uniform bound over all routes would be far larger, and Appendix E.8 uses the measured values. Because conflict weights are known, the experiment does not exercise Assumptions A.1–A.2.

Table E.9.
