Prompt v1 values use all states and its ten runs; the two-floor rows pool the searches of both maps, and their $\mu$ is not computed. For the unsafe team the round coefficients and the bound are not defined, because its loss can increase. The correction error at true block minimizers is zero in every case, so $\bar{B}$ is the mean error at returned proposals (summed over both floors on two floors).

## E.8. The bound, round by round

This subsection defines the retrospective upper bound of Figures 4(a) and E.4 and evaluates it on every team run. The bound uses actual search quality and correction errors in each round, rather than full-domain worst-case errors or constant coefficients obtained by pooling different histories. For seed $s$ and an active round $k$ , define

(E.4)

 $\begin{split}\alpha_{s,k}&=\frac{\sum_{b}Q_{s,k,b}D_{b}(x_{s}^{k})}{3\Delta(x_{s}^{k})},\\
B_{s,k}&=\frac{1}{3}\sum_{b}\bigl(Q_{s,k,b}e^{*}_{s,k,b}+e^{U}_{s,k,b}\bigr).\end{split}$ 

Here $Q$ is the realized corrected-local progress fraction, $D_{b}$ is the exact available true block improvement, $e^{*}$ is the absolute anchored score error at a certified true block minimizer, and $e^{U}$ is that error at the returned proposal. If the initial surrogate gap is zero, $D_{b}=0$ in these data, so its $QD_{b}$ contribution is zero. All true-minimizer errors $e^{*}$ are zero; returned-candidate errors include the actual missing-pair penalties and the zero-error cases.

For each recorded search, the argument of Lemma 1, applied to realized rather than expected quantities, gives

 $g_{s,k,b}\geq Q_{s,k,b}D_{b}-Q_{s,k,b}e^{*}_{s,k,b}-e^{U}_{s,k,b}.$ 

The best of the three singleton colors has at least their mean verified gain. Hence

(E.5)

 $\Delta(x_{s}^{k+1})\leq(1-\alpha_{s,k})\Delta(x_{s}^{k})+B_{s,k}.$ 

Independent replay verifies this inequality and $0\leq\alpha_{s,k}\leq 1$ in every active round, using all returned block proposals, not just selected blocks. Averaging over blocks is conservative here: with $\kappa=3$ each color is a single block on one map, so the committed gain is the largest singleton gain. The largest per-block lower bound gives the sharper recurrence $\Delta(x_{s}^{k+1})\leq\max\{0,\Delta(x_{s}^{k})-\max_{b}(Q_{s,k,b}D_{b}-Q_{s,k,b}e^{*}_{s,k,b}-e^{U}_{s,k,b})\}$ , which we also check.

Propagate the bound separately for each seed. Initialize $E_{s,0}=\Delta(x_{s}^{0})$ ( $1476-144=1332$ on the first map) and set

(E.6)

 $E_{s,k+1}=\min\{E_{s,k},(1-\alpha_{s,k})E_{s,k}+B_{s,k}\}.$ 

The minimum uses the accepted loss’s monotonicity. Since $1-\alpha_{s,k}\geq 0$ , induction with (E.5) proves $\Delta(x_{s}^{k})\leq E_{s,k}$ at every recorded state. After run $s$ stops at $K_{s}$ , hold both its actual loss and envelope fixed. Only then average the twenty envelopes:

(E.7)

 $\overline{U}_{K}=F^{*}+\frac{1}{20}\sum_{s=0}^{19}E_{s,\min\{K,K_{s}\}}.$ 

Results.

On the first map, the one-step inequality (E.5) and $0\leq\alpha_{s,k}\leq 1$ hold in all 145 active rounds. Held after each run stops and averaged over the twenty runs, the envelope (E.7) ends at mean gap 254.2 after thirteen rounds, against 5.9 observed; it stays within a factor of 2.1 of the observed mean gap through round 3 (1.13, 1.49 and 2.03). With the largest per-block lower bound instead of the mean, the final mean gap is 6.8. The mean error term $B_{s,k}$ falls from 137 in round 1 to 0 in round 7, with small values in later rounds over the few runs still active. On the second map the inequality holds in all 159 rounds, and the envelope ends at 226.8 against 7.2 observed, or 8.0 with the largest per-block lower bound. On two floors it holds in all 186 rounds and ends at 395.9 against 13.1 (Appendix E.4). The pooled means of the coefficients are $\bar{\alpha}=0.246$ , $0.242$ and $0.222$ and $\bar{B}=45.5$ , $36.5$ and $66.7$ on the three tasks; they summarize the logs and are not substituted into the recurrence, because keeping each round’s coefficients preserves their dependence on the current gap and history. Figure E.4 shows the pointwise range of the observed gap over runs. With prompt v1 on the first map, the bound held in all 78 rounds of the ten team runs and ended at 211.7 against 12.5, or 15.3 with the largest per-block lower bound.

This is a valid upper bound on the recorded trajectories: the pathwise form of Theorem 2, with realized $Q$ in place of $q_{b}(H)$ and measured two-point errors in place of $\delta_{b}^{*},\delta_{b}^{U}$ . It takes no supremum over collision-seeking routes and fits no decay rate to the loss curve. Because its coefficients come from the same runs, it explains the recorded progress rather than predicting fresh runs.

Coefficients from other runs.

The recurrence can also be evaluated with coefficients measured on different runs: average $\alpha_{s,k}$ and $B_{s,k}$ over the runs of one half that are active in round $k$ , and propagate $\overline{E}_{k+1}=\min\{\overline{E}_{k},(1-\bar{\alpha}_{k})\overline{E}_{k}+\bar{B}_{k}\}$ from $\overline{E}_{0}=1332$ . On the first map, coefficients from seeds 0–9 give a curve above the mean gap of seeds 10–19 in every round, ending at 141.5 against 6.4, and with the halves exchanged it ends at 167.9 against 5.4, again above in every round; on the second map the curves also lie above in every round in both directions, and with prompt v1, splitting the ten seeds into halves gave the same result.

## E.9. Greedy merging and conflicts

Section 5 shows that the best-combination merge keeps the guarantees of Theorem 2 and that its rate depends on the realized number of colors $\kappa^{*}_{k}$ , which is at most the effective number of colors $\kappa_{k}$ . This subsection gives a greedy version that needs only quadratically many checks, measures the conflicts and the resulting envelope on the recorded runs, explains why the block error bound is still needed, and lists ways to weaken it.

A greedy rule.

With pairwise factors, (18) determines the loss of every combination from the single checks and one check per pair, so $|I^{k}|(|I^{k}|-1)/2$ further checks suffice to find all conflicts and the best combination of improving blocks. When even the search over these combinations is too costly, a greedy rule suffices. Write $\deg^{\prime}(b)$ for the number of remaining blocks that conflict with $b$ ; the rule repeatedly keeps the remaining block $v$ with the largest $g_{v}/(1+\deg^{\prime}(v))$ and deletes $v$ and the blocks that conflict with it. It returns a conflict-free set with gain at least $\sum_{b\in I^{k}}g_{b}/(1+\deg_{k}(b))$ (Sakai et al., 2003). Indeed, each of the $1+\deg^{\prime}(v)$ blocks $u$ deleted in that step, $v$ included, satisfies $g_{u}/(1+\deg^{\prime}(u))\leq g_{v}/(1+\deg^{\prime}(v))$ , so together they contribute at most $g_{v}$ to $\sum_{u}g_{u}/(1+\deg^{\prime}(u))$ ; since $\deg^{\prime}(u)\leq\deg_{k}(u)$ and every block is deleted exactly once, the kept gains add up to at least $\sum_{b\in I^{k}}g_{b}/(1+\deg_{k}(b))$ . Accepting the better of this set and the best color keeps Theorem 2, and Corollary 1 when every improving proposal conflicts with at most $\bar{\kappa}-1$ others, using a quadratic number of checks.

Recorded runs.

The runs with best combination checked all combinations of changed blocks, which measures every $\xi_{bb^{\prime}}$ . The checks confirm (18) in all 44 and 29 rounds in which all three blocks changed on the two maps, and Proposition 2, together with the bound $\sum_{b}g_{b}/(1+\deg_{k}(b))$ of the greedy rule above, in all 113 and 111 rounds. Of the pairs of improving proposals, 71 of 135 conflict on map 1 and 42 of 90 on map 2. In 56 and 28 of these pairs, accepting both proposals creates a collision between their robots that neither proposal causes alone; in the other 15 and 14, both proposals remove the same collision. Another 11 and 15 pairs help each other ( $\xi_{bb^{\prime}}<0$ ). Rounds with two or more improving proposals have no conflict in 16 of 63 cases on map 1 and 10 of 42 on map 2. With the rounds that have fewer than two improving proposals, $\kappa_{k}=1$ in 66 of the 113 rounds on map 1 and 79 of the 111 on map 2, $\kappa_{k}=2$ in 41 and 30, and $\kappa_{k}=3$ in 6 and 2. The realized number of colors $\kappa^{*}_{k}$ averages 1.19 and 1.15 over the rounds that change the plan, never exceeds 1.98 and 2.00, and is below 1 in 8 and 9 rounds. In the rounds with two or more improving proposals it averages 1.29 and 1.26, against 1.84 and 1.81 for $\kappa_{k}$ , and it is smaller than $\kappa_{k}$ in 49 of 63 and 35 of 42 of them: conflicting proposals are accepted together because that still lowers the loss most. Table E.10 splits the rounds into the start, the repair phase, in which the base still has an undelivered robot or a collision, and the refinement of a safe base. At the start every conflict is a new collision between the agents’ new routes, yet the best combination often accepts both proposals; during repair many conflicts come from two proposals that remove the same collision; once the base is safe, conflicts are rare because at most one agent improves in most rounds.

Realized coupling in the best-combination runs by phase: number of rounds, mean number of improving proposals, conflicting pairs among pairs of improving proposals, conflicts in which the two proposals together create a collision that neither causes alone, mean effective number of colors, mean number of accepted blocks, and rounds whose accepted combination contains a conflicting pair.

With $g_{b}\geq Q_{b}D_{b}-Q_{b}e^{*}_{b}-e^{U}_{b}$ (Appendix E.8), (16) gives $F(x)-F(x^{+})=\sum_{b}g_{b}/\kappa^{*}_{k}\geq\sum_{b}(Q_{b}D_{b}-Q_{b}e^{*}_{b}-e^{U}_{b})/\kappa^{*}_{k}$ , and Proposition 2 gives the same with $\kappa_{k}$ in place of $\kappa^{*}_{k}$ . Hence the recurrence (E.5) holds with the divisor 3 in (E.4) replaced by either value in round $k$ of run $s$ , with $\alpha_{s,k}$ capped at one; for Algorithm 1 the realized number is $\sum_{b}g_{b}/\max_{b}g_{b}$ . It exceeds one whenever the round discards an improving proposal, which happens in 79 of the 121 rounds of Team (Alg. 1) that change the plan on map 1 and in 64 of 115 on map 2; over all rounds that change the plan it averages 1.50 and 1.53 and reaches 2.85 and 2.94. The replay checks this inequality in every round, and the envelope (E.6) with these coefficients bounds every recorded gap. Figure E.4 separates three effects. At most $\min\{\kappa,|I^{k}|\}$ colors contain an improving block, so dividing by this number is valid for any merge that keeps at least the best color. If no block improves, then $|I^{k}|=0$ and every $g_{b}$ is zero, so $\sum_{b}(Q_{b}D_{b}-Q_{b}e^{*}_{b}-e^{U}_{b})\leq 0$ : the gain bound holds with any positive divisor. As for $\kappa_{k}$ and $\kappa^{*}_{k}$ in Section 5, we then divide by 1. It lowers the final envelope of the best-combination runs from 328.7 to 107.2 on map 1, from 399.4 to 162.5 on map 2 and from 604.7 to 190.6 on two floors, where each floor uses its own divisor, which is valid because the best combination is optimal on each floor; for the runs with Algorithm 1 it lowers the envelope from 254.2 to 54.6 and from 226.8 to 62.2. Dividing by the effective number of colors $\kappa_{k}$ , which needs the best combination, lowers the best-combination envelopes further to 54.8, 116.3 and 133.0. Dividing by the realized number $\kappa^{*}_{k}$ , which also credits conflicting proposals accepted together, lowers them to 18.1, 87.6 and 69.9, against observed mean gaps of 5.4, 12.8 and 18.2; for Algorithm 1, its realized number gives 27.5, 33.5 and 50.0, against 5.9, 7.2 and 13.1. Through round 3, the best-combination envelope stays within a factor of 1.5, 3.7 and 1.7 of the observed mean gap with $\kappa^{*}_{k}$ and 2.7, 5.7 and 3.1 with $\kappa_{k}$ , against 6.4, 17.8 and 8.9 with $\kappa=3$ .

Each dashed curve reaches the values of its solid curve later, by a factor between about 1.6 and 2.7, and on two floors the sequential replays of the teams end near or beyond the single agent's search time.

Each dashed curve reaches the values of its solid curve later, by a factor between about 1.6 and 2.7, and on two floors the sequential replays of the teams end near or beyond the single agent's search time.

What still needs the block error bound.

The best combination combines the agents’ proposals, and an agent returns its current block unless it finds an improvement on its corrected objective. At a single-block trap with exact corrections no block improvement exists, so every agent returns its current block and every merge of the returned proposals stays at the trap. No merge rule over the returned proposals can therefore replace Assumption 3: with exact corrections, a reached trap keeps a positive gap forever, whereas Theorem 2 shows that the assumption suffices together with Assumption 2. Four and two final states of the best-combination runs on the two maps are such traps (Appendix E.5), and the unsafe team, which also accepts worse plans, ends in one such trap on each map.

Directions.

Three changes could weaken the assumption. First, a pairwise error bound $\sum_{b}D_{b}(x)+\sum_{b<b^{\prime}}D_{bb^{\prime}}(x)\geq\mu_{2}\Delta(x)$ , where $D_{bb^{\prime}}(x)$ is the best improvement from changing blocks $b$ and $b^{\prime}$ together, holds at every trap that a joint change of two blocks can leave. Exploiting it requires proposals that are not individually improving: each agent could return a short list of candidates, such as its best proposal and alternatives that reroute its robots involved in reported collisions, and the coordinator would check combinations across the lists. A rate would then need a joint counterpart of Assumption 2: with some probability, the lists contain both parts of a good two-block change. Second, blocks can be coarsened after a stall: when a round accepts nothing, two agents’ blocks are merged for the next round, so one agent controls six robots. A two-block change of the fine partition is a single-block change of the coarse one, and Theorem 2 applies to the coarse partition with its own $\mu$ . Third, conflicts could be bounded in expectation: Corollary 1 needs $\kappa_{k}\leq\bar{\kappa}$ in every epoch, whereas a condition such as $\mathbb{E}[\sum_{b}g_{b}/(1+\deg_{k}(b))\mid\mathcal{H}]\geq\bar{\kappa}^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\sum_{b}g_{b}\mid\mathcal{H}]$ would allow occasional dense conflicts; it couples the gains with the conflicts and would have to be measured like the other constants.

## E.10. Parallelism and decomposition

We distinguish the effect of simplifying an individual search from the effect of executing searches concurrently. The discussion relates these mechanisms to the recorded comparison and the sequential timing replay.

Reducing the difficulty of a model request.

Each team search writes three routes; with prompt v1 the whole-plan single agent writes nine in every answer and reaches a safe plan in one run of ten. A single agent that still sees and scores the whole plan but replaces only one route per answer is safe in nine runs of ten with prompt v1 and in all twenty with prompt v2 on the first map (Appendix D), so much of the difficulty lies in the size of each answer. Within the team, the correction removes collisions between agents: without it, the team is safe in one run of ten with prompt v1, and in none of ten, two of ten and none of ten runs with prompt v2 on map 1, map 2 and two floors.

The same model in both policies.

Full-plan search can revise several ownership groups jointly and has access to all assignments and full-plan feedback. On one map, our block protocol with Algorithm 1 instead commits at most one proposed block per round, even though three searches run concurrently. External conflicts omitted from the available objective must first be exposed by verification; revising a route against the refreshed peer trajectory can require another response in a later round. Block commitment, conflict discovery and repair are therefore serial steps that parallel search does not remove; from the all-waiting start, the team needs at least three rounds to accept routes for all three agents. A model that reliably writes a correct full plan in one answer avoids this overhead, which is why a single agent that improves one route at a time can fall faster at first.

A sequential block control.

The sequential replay replays the recorded team histories on a sequential worker (Figure E.5). It completes the three private searches in turn with the same $H$ , objective and feedback. The base, retained pairs and peer trajectories remain frozen until all proposals are available, then the verifier applies the team’s merge rule. Committing before the next search would change the policy. Replay preserves every answer, selection and round loss, changing only timestamps; it makes no new model requests.

For a fixed sequence of checked responses, let $t_{kb}=\sum_{h}a_{kbh}$ be block $b$ ’s response time in round $k$ . The two search times would be

(E.8)

 $\begin{split}T_{\mathrm{sequential}}&=\sum_{k}\sum_{b}t_{kb},\\
T_{\mathrm{parallel}}&=\sum_{k}\max_{b}t_{kb}.\end{split}$ 

Thus $T_{\mathrm{parallel}}\leq T_{\mathrm{sequential}}$ , and for fixed durations $T_{\mathrm{sequential}}$ is at most three times $T_{\mathrm{parallel}}$ (six times on two floors). With prompt v2 the replay gives $853.9\pm 374.2$ against $481.0\pm 227.3$ seconds on the first map (ratio of means 1.78), $778.2\pm 304.6$ against $482.3\pm 201.6$ on the second (1.61), and $1632.1\pm 468.5$ against $656.4\pm 206.1$ on two floors (2.49), where six agents search at once; with prompt v1 it gave $1207.14\pm 165.61$ against $589.39\pm 64.85$ on the first map (2.05). For the other merge rules the ratios are 1.77, 1.68 and 2.48 with best combination and 1.96, 1.80 and 2.71 for the unsafe team on map 1, map 2 and two floors (Figure E.5). Played in turn, the team with Algorithm 1 would be slower than the one-route single agent on every task, so parallel execution is what makes the team fast; its better plans do not depend on it. Both follow (C.1). Actual sequential execution could change serving latencies and samples, so the replay is not a measured variant. Per-round durations, clocks, loss sequences and curves are retained with the analyses.

## Appendix F SQLite: acquiring and reusing feedback

This appendix gives the complete database experiment: the data and query choices, feedback acquisition policies, timing procedure and recorded results. The study isolates the cost of obtaining and reusing execution information.

## F.1. Purpose of the SQLite study

The robot-routing study (Section 8.1, Appendix E) is our primary experiment with live LLMs. This second study isolates a different question: can execution of a useful task supply reusable information more cheaply than calibrating alternative decisions in advance? We test this question in a real SQLite engine, with constructed data and deterministic planning procedures. The procedures are not language models, and the experiment does not measure parallel-agent inference. The synthetic study in Appendix G supplies the controlled connection to the convergence assumptions; this database study supplies a measured application of feedback acquisition.

Prior work already uses execution feedback to improve query planning. LEO corrects cost estimates from observed executions; Bao learns to guide an existing optimizer (Stillger et al., 2001; Marcus et al., 2021). We do not propose a new optimizer; we use this setting to measure the cost of acquiring and processing selected, reusable observations.

## F.2. Data, queries and available decisions

Each report combines three aggregate queries, one per source; the main text calls a report simply a query. For source $s\in\{0,1,2\}$ , tables $A_{s}$ and $C_{s}$ hold endpoint objects with group identifiers, and table $B_{s}$ holds edges with endpoint IDs and an integer amount. A query selects a group $g_{s}$ in $A_{s}$ and a group $h_{s}$ in $C_{s}$ , then returns the count and sum of amounts of edges connecting the selected objects. The report is one SQL UNION ALL of the three aggregate branches. Correctness is independent of the selected join direction.

Each endpoint table has 4,096 groups and two objects per group, hence 8,192 rows. Half of the $A_{s}$ groups are randomly designated high-degree: each of their objects has 400 outgoing edges, while every other object has one. Thus $B_{s}$ contains

 $2\cdot 2{,}048\cdot 400+2\cdot 2{,}048=1{,}642{,}496$ 

edges. Independently, a randomly chosen half of the $C_{s}$ groups receives 97% of the generated edges. Membership is not encoded in group IDs. Edge amounts are integers sampled from 1 through 9,999. Indexes cover endpoint groups and both edge endpoint IDs; ordinary ANALYZE is performed. The three-source database contains 4,927,488 edge rows in total and remains static throughout a workload.

For each branch, the controller chooses one of two physical plans: start from the selected $A_{s}$ group and traverse toward $C_{s}$ (left), or start from $C_{s}$ and traverse toward $A_{s}$ (right). CROSS JOIN and the relevant edge index enforce these alternatives; the native control lets SQLite choose. All alternatives compute the same aggregate, including when no edges match.

Let $v_{s}(\ell,g)$ and $v_{s}(r,h)$ be the numbers of edges incident to the selected left and right groups. Their sum over the three selected directions defines a separable work surrogate,

(F.1)

 $J_{q}(a)=\sum_{s=0}^{2}v_{s}(a_{s},q_{s}),\qquad a\in\{\ell,r\}^{3}.$ 

Fan-out predicts work, but is not identical to elapsed time or total VM steps. The experimental outcomes are the independently measured execution and controller costs, rather than treating (F.1) as exact runtime. There is no collision-like coupling between branches: a centralized factorized controller can make the same choices without enumerating all eight joint plans.

## F.3. Acquiring a persistent correction

The controller stores estimates for each source, side and group. Unknown entries start at 401, the source’s average fan-out. It chooses the direction with the lower estimate, breaking ties in the order preferred by the native planner. After execution, the engine’s NVISIT counter reports how many edge-index entries the chosen branch visited (SQLite, 2026). This replaces the estimate for the executed side’s group. The estimate is retained for later queries with that group, even if the opposite endpoint predicate changes. Unexecuted alternatives are not revealed automatically.

The selective-feedback policy retrieves the detailed profile only if the completed report used more than 1,000 SQLite virtual-machine steps. A trigger updates all three executed branches. The engine collects counters even on reports below this threshold; the gate saves retrieval and processing, not collection. Both instrumentation and retrieval costs remain in the learner’s measured time. The threshold was selected during development and frozen before confirmation on the three fresh database seeds.

The observation changes the decision model rather than the answer to the query: it is reused cost information, the learned counterpart of a correction. Because each report brings new predicates, the stream is not a descent on one fixed solution; the study therefore measures the acquisition trade-off of Section 7 rather than a convergence rate.

## F.4. Workloads and controls

The favorable stream has 48 reports. In each source, five-sixths of branch queries pair a low-degree $A_{s}$ group with a high-degree $C_{s}$ group, favoring the initial left choice. The remaining sixth repeatedly uses two high-degree $A_{s}$ groups with varying low-degree $C_{s}$ groups. Outliers are staggered across sources: half of reports contain one such branch and half contain none. A 192-report stream repeats this same cycle four times, testing how advance calibration amortizes. A balanced 48-report stream mixes the two patterns equally within each source, with less reuse of recurring costly groups. The workloads were deliberately constructed and developed adaptively.

The main controls include native planning, a fixed left plan, samples of 128 or 2,048 edges per source, complete joined histograms, exact cached active-group counts, and bounded cached counting. The strongest counting control first counts the predicted better side, then limits counting of its alternative once that alternative cannot improve the decision. It batches requests across sources and retains exact counts and reusable lower bounds. Calibration queries and their execution costs are charged. This control does not repeatedly identify every possible decision.

Cached plan trials execute both local directions when a predicate pair first appears, retain the lower-VM-step direction and reuse it. The first trial supplies the requested answer and is charged as productive work, while the other is calibration. This avoids charging both alternatives as pure overhead. Further controls retrieve feedback on every report, use centralized selective feedback with the same observations, or freeze feedback after four reports. The centralized controller in the main timing table enumerates eight joint surrogate choices; a separate factorized implementation removes that unnecessary enumeration and independently checks decision equivalence.

## F.5. Execution, timing and correctness

The engine is SQLite 3.46.1, compiled from pinned public source with statement scan-status support. Fresh database seeds are 51031, 68437 and 89759. Source-specific generation uses seed $s_{\rm db}+18{,}181s$ , and the query generator uses $s_{\rm db}+818{,}181$ . There are three timing repetitions per database and workload, with randomized policy order. The instrumentation-controlled record contains $3\times 3\times 3\times 12=324$ complete workload timings. These are three independent datasets, not nine independent datasets per table entry.

The timer covers the complete Python/SQLite controller: calibration, execution, instrumentation, profile processing, correctness assertions and trace bookkeeping. Data generation, index construction, ordinary ANALYZE, connection opening and initial native-plan inspection lie outside this timer. Initial database preparation took 10.76–12.21 seconds per seed and produced approximately 193.7 MB per database; it is separately recorded. Measurements use warm local execution, without artificial network or evaluator delays. Baselines that do not consume profiles have scan-status collection disabled in the timing comparison. VM steps are engine instruction counts, not CPU instructions or a measured hardware time conversion.

During confirmation, every reported answer is checked against a Python reference accumulated independently while generating records. The instrumentation-control run replays those already verified answers. A separate offline audit validates all 483 extracted group-degree measurements in six 48-report streams. A factorized centralized control with the same observations matches all local choices and VM totals in 27 replayed streams. Regenerating the appendix independently recomputes the summary statistics from the 324 saved timings; it does not rerun the benchmark.

Policy
Favorable, 48 reports

## F.6. Results and a recorded correction

Selective feedback takes 4.92 ms on the favorable 48-report stream, compared with 7.53 ms for bounded cached counting: 0.65 of the time, an absolute saving of 2.61 ms. Per database, the relative time ranges from 0.62 to 0.71. Only six reports per database trigger detailed profiles. At 192 reports, the relative time rises to 0.84 as advance calibration amortizes. Feedback after every report costs 7.15 ms on the shorter stream, showing why retrieval and processing should be charged.

The balanced stream reverses the result: selective feedback costs 17.13 ms versus 10.45 ms for bounded counting, or 1.64 times as much. It retrieves profiles on 48, 47 and 48 reports for the three databases, respectively. Learning from feedback thus pays off when costly groups are few and recur, and advance calibration is preferable when they are frequent; this is the trade-off predicted in Section 7.

Time and VM work need not rank policies identically. On the favorable 48-report stream, bounded counting has lower median VM work (34,433 steps versus 48,106 for selective feedback), but performs 96 additional calibration statements per run. Selective feedback needs no calibration statements. Its lower controller time is consistent with saving these invocations; lower VM work alone does not explain the ranking. Figure F.1 shows both measurements: controller time in panel (a) and VM work in panels (b, c).

One concrete trace illustrates how reuse changes later choices. On database seed 51031, zero-indexed report 0 uses source-0 groups $(0,2470)$ and chooses left. Its edge-index scan visits 800 entries, replacing the initial left-group estimate 401 with 800. At report 12, group 0 recurs with the different right group 2199; the retained estimate makes the controller choose right. Total work of the three-branch report is 337 VM steps, compared with 6,521 for report 0. These are different predicate pairs, so the difference is a trace illustration, not a controlled single-query speedup. Group 1 shows the same pattern at reports 6 and 18, with 6,521 and 345 VM steps. The later reports stay below the profile threshold and do not request new detailed feedback.

The centralized selective learner makes the same decisions. Its separately audited factorized version has pooled-median controller times 5.35, 16.28 and 16.99 ms for the three workloads. These replays check implementation and information equivalence. A supplementary SQLite STAT4 control, with richer statistics granted for free and profiling disabled, records 6.14, 22.46 and 12.90 ms of SQL-only time; it excludes the outer controller and is therefore not directly comparable with Table F.2.

## F.7. Relation to the assumptions

For a fixed report, the two-direction action spaces are finite, the known additive surrogate has independent source factors, and its blockwise minimization is exact. Thus the planner selects a surrogate minimizer, rather than exhibiting stochastic LLM search quality. If exact fan-outs were supplied for that fixed report, the additive fan-out objective would have block error-bound constant one. These structural facts concern the work surrogate in (F.1), not the controller’s actual runtime over the workload.

Assumption 2 holds for every $\rho_{b}<1$ , since each two-choice surrogate is minimized exactly. Successive reports change predicates instead of improving one solution under a fixed $F$ , so Theorem 2 does not apply to the stream, and the learned table is updated by direct replacement of observed entries, the coordinate case of the sketch-and-project update of Appendix A.4, from per-branch counters rather than from scalar objective differences. The study instead tests the cost model of Section 6: whether the information a correction needs is cheaper to acquire from useful work or by measuring alternatives in advance. Theorem 2 is checked with measured constants in the routing study and with certified constants in the synthetic study.

## F.8. Scope and retained data

The workload is constructed to make the trade-off visible. Its joins are highly selective: 431 of 432 audited favorable branch aggregates and 429 of 432 balanced aggregates contain no matching edges, although finding no match still incurs execution work. There are three independent databases, totals are in milliseconds, the branches are separable, and host hardware details were not recorded.

The result is a measured acquisition trade-off: useful executions can reveal a small recurring set of missing costs cheaply enough to outperform a strong cached calibration control, whereas frequent or poorly transferable observations reverse the comparison. The appendix uses the final instrumentation-controlled results.

## Appendix G Synthetic binary optimization

This appendix specifies the synthetic task and its four experimental series. It provides the assumption certificates, the cost of private work, the error floor under approximate corrections, and results for learning and reusing corrections.

## G.1. Purpose and experimental scope

This third study directly instantiates the finite block-optimization protocol with an analytically tractable randomized local solver. It asks how an objective correction changes progress, how many local steps are worthwhile between synchronizations, and whether learning or reusing the correction repays its query cost. Unlike the routing experiments (Appendices C–E), it contains no LLM inference. Unlike Appendix F, its convergence constants can be established on the entire task domain. All series use one fixed 48-variable task.

The four series are: exact correction with varying private horizon; approximate correction with a persistent bias; correction learned through charged scalar evaluations; and reuse of already-paid proposal evaluations with an observable stopping certificate. Their stopping rules and cost conventions are stated separately. Each series has its own script, and the tables and figures are rebuilt from the saved results.

## G.2. Objective, blocks and all fixed settings

The objective is (24): six blocks $x_{b}\in\{0,1\}^{8}$ on the path $1$ – $2$ – $3$ – $4$ – $5$ – $6$ , where neighboring blocks pay for disagreements of corresponding bits and each bit pays for disagreeing with its target $t_{bj}$ . The coupling weight is $w=0.2$ . Targets are sampled first as independent binary values, then unary weights are sampled uniformly from $[1,1.5]$ using NumPy’s default random generator with seed 20260925. The actual targets, all 48 weights and the source are retained; subsequent experiments read this same instance rather than sampling another task. Initialization is $x^{0}=1-t$ . The unique minimizer is $t$ , with $F^{*}=3.4$ , and the initial objective gap is $\Delta_{0}=58.6047775135$ .

The private objective $L_{b}(u)=\sum_{j}a_{bj}|u_{j}-\tilde{t}_{bj}|$ omits coupling and uses the private target $\tilde{t}_{b}$ . Blocks 1, 3 and 5 use $\tilde{t}_{b}=1-t_{b}$ , while the other blocks have $\tilde{t}_{b}=t_{b}$ . This deliberately inserts a local/global mismatch. Activity probabilities $\pi_{b}$ range from 0.5 to 1.0. The complete target and activity assignment is given in Table G.1.

## G.3. Exact correction and search

With neighbors frozen at $x$ , the coefficient of candidate bit $u_{j}$ in the global conditional objective is

(G.1)

 $A_{bj}(x)=a_{bj}(1-2t_{bj})+w\!\sum_{r:\,|r-b|=1}(1-2x_{rj}).$ 

The corrected score is affine. Defining

 $h_{bj}(x)=A_{bj}(x)-a_{bj}(1-2\tilde{t}_{bj}),$ 

the anchored correction is $R_{b}(u;x)=\sum_{j}h_{bj}(x)(u_{j}-x_{bj})$ . It exactly equals the global conditional change minus the local change. A constant offset could not reverse the wrong private preference; the candidate-dependent vector does. Eight coefficients per block replace a table of 256 candidate values. Neighbor contributions are recomputed at each synchronization and remain fixed during local search.

Each epoch starts from the same shared base for all six searches. At a private step, block $b$ chooses a coordinate uniformly and, with probability $\pi_{b}$ , sets it to minimize its affine corrected score; otherwise it leaves the bit unchanged. After $H$ steps, all six proposed singleton replacements are checked with the exact global objective. Their nonnegative verified gains are summed over the two colors $\{1,3,5\}$ and $\{2,4,6\}$ . The larger-gain color is committed, using only strictly improving blocks. Ties select the first color. Same-color edits share no objective factor, so their verified gains add exactly. This differs from routing’s complete three-block dependency graph, where each accepted color contains only one block.

The exact-correction series uses the horizons $H=1$ , 2, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64 and 128, with 2,000 solver trajectories per horizon. Solver randomness uses seed $20260925+H$ . Each corrected run has a ceiling of 512 epochs and stops on exact attainment of the offline-known optimum; every run succeeds before that ceiling. The uncorrected $H=8$ ablation uses only the private unary scores and a ceiling of 128 epochs. It solves none of 2,000 runs and leaves mean gap 30.979, so the correction is necessary on this task.

## G.4. Certifying the assumptions

We establish correction accuracy, local contraction and block geometry for this fixed task. These certificates determine the constants used in the comparison between the theoretical bound and observed convergence.

Correction accuracy.

The exact vector satisfies Assumption 1 with $\delta_{b}^{*}=\delta_{b}^{U}=0$ ; indeed its error is zero for every binary candidate and neighbor context. The saved implementation checks 200 sampled exact-correction identities, with maximum discrepancy $1.33\times 10^{-14}$ , and 10,000 anchored approximate-correction inequalities. The algebraic identity supplies the uniform guarantee; sampled checks audit its implementation.

If a correction computed at neighbor context $z$ is reused after neighbors change, reanchoring at the current owned block gives the valid bound

 $\sup_{u}|\widehat{R}_{b}(u;x)-R_{b}(u;x)|\leq 2w\sum_{r:\,|r-b|=1}\|z_{r}-x_{r}\|_{1}.$ 

The experiment refreshes context every epoch, so this staleness error is zero. Obtaining changed neighbor context is part of synchronization, not an assumed free oracle.

Conditional local contraction.

Each unresolved coordinate’s nonnegative contribution to affine suboptimality disappears with probability $\pi_{b}/8$ per private step. Linearity of conditional expectation gives Assumption 2 with $\rho_{b}=\pi_{b}/8$ , uniformly over bases and coefficient vectors. Thus

(G.2)

 $\rho_{\min}=0.0625,\qquad q(H)=1-(1-0.0625)^{H},\qquad\kappa=2.$ 

This is an analytical property of the coordinate solver, not a value fitted to observed trajectories.

Block geometry and reference optimum.

Let $c_{b}=w\deg(b)$ , which is 0.2 for endpoints and 0.4 for interior blocks. Every $a_{bj}>c_{b}$ . Repairing a wrong bit to $t_{bj}$ therefore saves more unary cost than it can add in incident coupling cost. This proves uniqueness of $t$ and gives a uniform valid block error bound

(G.3)

 $\mu_{\rm dom}=\min_{b,j}\frac{a_{bj}-c_{b}}{a_{bj}+c_{b}}=0.4307855764.$ 

Indeed, for wrong-bit set $I(x)$ ,

 $\sum_{b}D_{b}(x)\geq\sum_{(b,j)\in I(x)}(a_{bj}-c_{b}),\quad\Delta(x)\leq\sum_{(b,j)\in I(x)}(a_{bj}+c_{b}).$ 

Their ratio establishes Assumption 3 on the full domain.

The tighter finite-state certificate is $\mu=0.7193550158$ . It does not require enumeration of $2^{48}$ joint configurations. The objective separates across the eight bit positions: each feature has just $2^{6}=64$ block patterns. For each feature we enumerate its exact minimum and all singleton improvements, then minimize the improvement-to-gap ratio over its nonoptimal states. Full-state gaps and best-block improvements sum over features, so the full-state ratio is a weighted average of their ratios; the least feature ratio is valid globally and is attained by making only that feature nonoptimal. The total certificate uses $8\times 64=512$ states. A separate appendix builder independently recomputes $F^{*}$ and this constant from the retained coefficients.

Theory-to-data comparison.

Using this certified $\mu$ , exact corrections and (G.2), Theorem 2 gives

(G.4)

 $\mathbb{E}F(x^{K})\leq F^{*}+\left(1-\frac{\mu q(H)}{2}\right)^{K}\Delta_{0}.$ 

Figure 5(b) compares this sequence with the sample means for $H=8$ and $32$ at integer epochs; no coefficient is fitted. The mean gap stays below the bound at every epoch. Completed trajectories remain at $F^{*}$ .

## G.5. Private work and synchronization

One parallel private slot costs $c=1$ ; shared preparation, transfer, verification and integration cost $C$ per epoch. In the known-correction series, verification is included in this common overhead. A run finishing at epoch $K_{\rm end}$ has modeled cost $(H+C)K_{\rm end}$ . We show $C\in\{0,5,20,100\}$ , including the idealized zero-overhead control. Costs are in modeled work units; six agents use six local slots per private step in total.

Figure 5(c) plots these costs. At $C=20$ , mean cost decreases from 1296.4 at $H=1$ to 176.7 at $H=64$ , then rises to 296.0 at $H=128$ . The observed best horizons among the eight tested are 1, 16, 64 and 64 for $C=0,5,20,100$ . The relaxed cost criterion of Section A.3 gives integer minimizers $H_{*}=1$ , 11, 20 and 35; the ceiled expected-gap criterion gives $H=22$ at $C=20$ when $\varepsilon=0.01\Delta_{0}$ and $H=1,\ldots,256$ are searched. Both move right with $C$ , as the observed minima do. Figure 5(c) places each $H_{*}$ on its cost curve by log-log interpolation between the tested horizons; the interpolated cost at $H_{*}$ is 1.00, 1.03, 1.16 and 1.29 times the best tested cost for $C=0,5,20,100$ . The criterion is smaller for large $C$ because it minimizes a worst-case bound on the expected gap, whereas the experiment measures first exact completion: with $H\geq 64$ nearly every block is solved exactly and runs finish in 2.0–2.1 epochs on average, one per color, faster than the geometric rate of the bound.

## G.6. Biased corrections and the floor

Theorem 2 allows a correction error that does not vanish to leave a floor that more private work cannot remove; this series shows that such a floor appears. The exact-correction series cannot show this, because its error is zero. This series keeps the task, solver, verification and merge above, but agents search the exact conditional coefficients plus a fixed bias,

 $\widehat{A}_{bj}(x)=A_{bj}(x)+\nu\,s_{bj},\qquad s_{bj}\in\{-1,1\},$ 

with independent uniform signs drawn once per run and unknown to the agents. The corrected objective remains affine in $u-x_{b}$ and hence anchored; its error at a candidate $u$ is $\sum_{j}\nu s_{bj}(u_{j}-x_{bj})$ . We test $\nu\in\{0,0.25,\ldots,2\}$ and $H\in\{8,32\}$ with 2,000 runs per configuration and 160 epochs, using seed $20261009$ offset by $1000H+100\nu$ .

For every search the series records the correction errors $e^{*}_{b}=|\widehat{f}_{b}(u_{b}^{*})-f_{b}(u_{b}^{*})|$ at a true block minimizer $u_{b}^{*}$ and $e^{U}_{b}=|\widehat{f}_{b}(U_{b})-f_{b}(U_{b})|$ at the returned proposal $U_{b}$ , the realized fraction $Q_{b}$ of the corrected improvement, and the exact block improvement $D_{b}$ ; $e^{*}_{b}$ and $e^{U}_{b}$ are the realized counterparts of $\delta_{b}^{*}$ and $\delta_{b}^{U}$ . The argument of Lemma 1, applied to these realized quantities, gives $g_{b}\geq\max\{0,Q_{b}(D_{b}-e^{*}_{b})-e^{U}_{b}\}$ ; the merge commits at least the larger color sum of these lower bounds. Propagating this inequality separately for each run from the initial gap yields an envelope that the observed gap never exceeds. The script asserts the local inequality, the merge inequality and the envelope for every run and epoch, and confirms that the envelope equals the observed gap when $\nu=0$ .

Table G.3. Approximate corrections: final mean gap after 160 epochs ( $\pm$ one standard error), runs reaching the optimum out of 2,000, and the mean per-run recurrence envelope.

The smallest magnitude of an exact coefficient is $\min_{b,j}(a_{bj}-c_{b})=0.605\geq 0.6$ . A bias below it never reverses the sign of a coefficient, so decisions and trajectories coincide with exact correction and every run reaches the optimum. Above it, a bit whose coefficient magnitude is below the bias and whose bias opposes the required change is never repaired, and the gap settles at a floor that grows with $\nu$ . Once the bias exceeds every coefficient magnitude (at most $a_{bj}+2w\leq 1.9$ ), about half of the coefficients are reversed and the floor approaches the uncorrected level 31. The floors for $H=8$ and $H=32$ differ by at most 0.49 at every $\nu$ , against values up to 30: longer private search reaches the floor sooner but does not lower it. The envelope is conservative for small biases because it charges both two-point errors in every search, even when they do not change a decision. A uniform bound would be $\delta_{b}=8\nu$ , attained at the initial state in which every bit is wrong, and its floor $B_{\delta}/\alpha$ is already 64 at $H=8$ and 37 at $H=32$ for $\nu=0.25$ and exceeds the initial gap for every $\nu\geq 0.5$ ; only the two-point errors make the bound informative here.

## G.7. Learning the correction

This series tests the learning mechanism of Section 6 and Appendix A.4: agents start without the correction and learn its fixed coefficients from charged scalar evaluations. It keeps the same task, coupling and coordinate solver but hides the static unary correction coefficients. Writing

 $\vartheta_{bj}=a_{bj}(1-2t_{bj})-a_{bj}(1-2\tilde{t}_{bj}),$ 

the algorithm starts at $\widehat{\vartheta}=0$ with known bounds $M_{bj}=2a_{bj}$ . The local scores and coupling are available; target bits and true coefficients are used only by the verifier and offline diagnostics. A one-bit probe supplies exactly the scalar residual in (A.33), with displacement $\pm 1$ , and identifies that coordinate. Coefficients are retained forever; changed neighbors affect only the recomputed coupling context.

Before each epoch’s $H$ private steps, every block with unobserved coordinates probes with probability $p$ , choosing uniformly among those coordinates. The tested probabilities are $1,0.5,0.25$ and horizons are $8,32,64$ . The refresh baseline queries all 48 coefficients at every epoch; identify-once queries them only at initialization. Both use the same scalar interface and pay acquisition costs. An uncorrected baseline retains known coupling but never calibrates the unary correction; it differs from the earlier private-score-only ablation.

Each configuration has 1,000 trajectories with seed $20260926+H+100p$ . Controls at $p=1$ use that same seed convention. Every calibrated configuration reaches the optimum. The uncorrected $H=32$ case solves none of 1,000 trajectories within 256 epochs. These trajectories stop at the offline-known optimum; the next series replaces this rule with an observable certificate.

The remaining coefficient radius $\Gamma_{k}$ is observable and upper-bounds candidate correction error. With deliberate probing, Proposition A.7 applies with $\gamma=p/8$ and at most $d_{\rm tot}=48$ extra calibration queries over a whole run. The all-coordinate excitation assumption is unnecessary here, because an identified coefficient is not probed again; the binary specialization supplies the uncertainty analysis. Separate projection and coefficient-inequality checks audit the relevant identities.

Second: mean completion cost of every acquisition policy at eight, 32 and 64 private steps, with one-standard-error bars. Third: true mean loss and the optimum plus the mean online stopping certificate against epoch at eight private steps, for dedicated calibration and verification reuse. Fourth: mean verifier queries against the number of private steps for dedicated calibration, verification reuse and identify-once.

Here a completed run costs $(H+20)K_{\rm end}+N_{\rm q}$ , where $N_{\rm q}$ counts the initial evaluation, calibration calls and verification of changed proposals. The base score is cached, unchanged proposals need no new call, and the selected color’s value follows from exact additivity. These query charges are explicit, unlike the exact-correction series where verification is included in $C$ , so costs are compared within a series. Offline diagnostic evaluations are excluded.

At $H=8,p=1$ , gradual learning costs 444.8, compared with 811.6 for repeated refresh and 367.9 for identify-once (Figure G.1(a, b)). At this horizon, retaining learned coefficients thus nearly halves the cost of reidentifying them every epoch; at $H=32$ and 64, where refresh runs finish in 3.4 and 2.1 epochs while gradual learning needs at least eight epochs to probe each block, repeated refresh is cheaper than gradual learning (356.8 against 541.7 and 287.7 against 755.3). When the hidden part is static and all 48 coefficients can be queried at once, identifying them once is cheapest; at larger $H$ the gap widens because private steps taken before calibration is complete are wasted (gradual learning 541.7 and 755.3, identify-once 239.7 and 234.8). Reducing $p$ reduces total queries only slightly but increases epochs and cost. Gradual learning pays off instead when only a few of many unknown values are ever needed and they recur, as in the favorable SQLite workloads (Appendix F).

## G.8. Reuse and observable stopping

The fourth series compares dedicated calibration, dedicated calibration plus verification reuse, and identify-once at $p=1$ and $H=8,32,64$ , with 1,000 trajectories per configuration. After proposals are fixed, a singleton evaluation can identify one still-unknown coefficient when its displacement contains exactly one unknown coordinate after known contributions are subtracted. Both accepted and rejected proposals are eligible. Measurements with several unknown coordinates are skipped. Updating the estimator does not regenerate this epoch’s proposals; identified coefficients are used from the next epoch on. Identified coordinates are removed from future deliberate calibration, and calibration plus reused identifications never exceeds 48 per trajectory.

Stopping now uses an observable gap certificate. At the new committed base let $\widehat{A}_{bj}$ be the private coefficient plus learned unary correction and refreshed coupling coefficient. Exact affine minimization gives the total surrogate improvement

 $\widehat{D}(x)=\sum_{b,j}\big[\widehat{A}_{bj}x_{bj}-\min\{0,\widehat{A}_{bj}\}\big].$ 

If $\Gamma(x)$ sums the remaining coordinate radii, (A.8) supplies

(G.5)

 $\Delta(x)\leq\overline{\Delta}(x)\coloneqq\frac{\widehat{D}(x)+\Gamma(x)}{\mu_{\rm dom}}.$ 

The known weights and coupling determine $\mu_{\rm dom}$ in (G.3); the stopping decision does not use the hidden target or optimum. It costs $O(48)$ arithmetic and no new verifier query, and stops when $\overline{\Delta}(x)<10^{-9}$ . This arithmetic and residual processing are included in $C=20$ . All 9,000 trajectories stop with zero certified gap, and saved mean certificates dominate saved mean true gaps (Figure G.1(c)). The simulator’s run-time assertions check domination separately for each trajectory.

At $H=8$ , reuse lowers mean cost from 444.8 to 441.6 and identifies 3.186 coefficients without extra queries. At $H=32$ and 64, the corresponding reused counts fall to 0.703 and 0.046 and cost savings become negligible. Mean completion epochs are unchanged, and identify-once remains cheaper at every horizon (Figure G.1(d)). Reuse is limited because an uncalibrated block with the wrong private target proposes no change in the coordinates needing correction, so its evaluation reveals nothing about them; deliberate probing remains necessary.

Summary.

On a task where every constant is certified, the observed mean gap stays below the bound of Theorem 2, a persistent correction error leaves a floor that longer private search does not lower, the best private horizon grows with the synchronization cost as $J(H)$ predicts, and learned coefficients are retained so that calibration stops after at most 48 queries. Costs are in modeled units on one fixed instance.
